Organizations typically set up these types of blockchain to get the best of both worlds, and it enables better structure when assigning who can participate in the network and in what transactions.
    
*   **Consortium Blockchain**
    
    Similar to permissioned blockchains, consortium blockchains have both public and private components, except multiple organizations will manage a single consortium blockchain network. Although these types of blockchains can initially be more complex to set up, once they are running, they can offer better security. Additionally, consortium blockchains are optimal for collaboration with multiple organizations.
    

# [Private Transactions](#toc.xhtml#s39a)

Many blockchain networks operate as public databases, meaning that anyone with an Internet connection can view a list of the network’s transaction history. Although users can access details about transactions, they cannot access identifying information about the users making those transactions. It is a common misperception that blockchain networks like bitcoin are anonymous, when in fact they are only confidential.

When a user makes a public transaction, their unique code — called a public key, as mentioned earlier — is recorded on the blockchain, while their personal information is not. If a person has made a Bitcoin purchase on an exchange that requires identification, then the person’s identity is still linked to their blockchain address — but a transaction, even when tied to a person’s name, does not reveal any personal information.

To see how a bank differs from blockchain, let’s compare the banking system to Bitcoin’s implementation of blockchain. *[Table 1.2](#c01.xhtml#tab1_2)* provides other details.

# [Cryptocurrencies: The Beginning of Blockchain’s Technological Rise](#toc.xhtml#s40a)

Blockchain’s most well-known use (and maybe most controversial) is in cryptocurrencies. Cryptocurrencies are digital currencies (or tokens), such as Bitcoin, Ethereum, or Litecoin, that can be used to buy goods and services. Just like a digital form of cash, crypto can be used to buy everything from your lunch to your next home. Unlike cash, crypto uses blockchain to act as both a public ledger and an enhanced cryptographic security system, so online transactions are always recorded and secured.

A cryptocurrency is a digital or virtual currency that is secured by cryptography, which makes it nearly impossible to counterfeit or double-spend[^(\[11\])](#c01.xhtml#ftn11). Many cryptocurrencies are decentralized networks based on blockchain technology — a distributed ledger enforced by a disparate network of computers. A defining feature of cryptocurrencies is that they are generally not issued by any central authority, rendering them theoretically immune to government interference or manipulation.

“Crypto” refers to the various encryption algorithms and cryptographic techniques that safeguard these entries, such as elliptical curve encryption, public-private key pairs, and hashing functions.

A cryptocurrency can be considered as a form of digital asset based on a network that is distributed across a large number of computers. This decentralized structure allows them to exist outside the control of governments and central authorities. Experts believe that blockchain and related technology will disrupt many industries, including finance and law.

Cryptocurrencies can be mined or purchased from *cryptocurrency exchanges*. Not all e-commerce sites allow purchases using cryptocurrencies. In fact, cryptocurrencies, even popular ones like Bitcoin, are hardly used for retail transactions. However, the skyrocketing value of cryptocurrencies has made them popular as trading instruments. To a limited extent, they are also used for cross-border transfers.

# [Cryptocurrencies as a Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s41a)

Blockchain technology is central to Bitcoin’s and other cryptocurrencies’ usefulness and allure. A collection of linked blocks from an online ledger is what blockchain, as its name suggests, basically is. Every network participant has independently verified a set of transactions that make up each block. This makes it nearly impossible to fabricate transaction histories, as each node must validate each newly generated block before it is confirmed. Every machine on a node, or individual network, that keeps a copy of the ledger must concur on the information that goes into the online ledger. Blockchain technology is useful for a variety of industries and processes, including online voting and crowdfunding, according to industry experts. Companies involved in finance, such as JPMorgan Chase & Co., are testing the use of blockchain technology to reduce transaction costs by expediting payment processing.

# [Cryptocurrency: Procedure of Its Working](#toc.xhtml#s42a)

In practice, all cryptocurrencies ought to be digital currencies that use blockchain technology to record and secure every transaction. A cryptocurrency (for example, Bitcoin) ought to be used in a digital form of cash to pay for everything from everyday items to larger purchases like cars and homes. These items can be bought using one of several digital wallets issued by various trading platforms, then digitally transferred upon purchase of an item, with the blockchain recording the transaction and the new owner. The beauty of carrying transactions through cryptocurrencies is that everything is recorded in a public ledger and secured using cryptography, making an irrefutable, timestamped, and secure record of every payment.

At the time of writing this para (January 2022), there are roughly 6,700 cryptocurrencies in the world, which have a total market cap of around $1.6 trillion, with Bitcoin holding a majority of the value. These tokens have become incredibly popular over the last few years, with one Bitcoin equaling $60,000. Here are some of the main reasons why everyone is suddenly taking notice of cryptocurrencies:

*   Blockchain’s security makes theft much harder since each cryptocurrency has its own irrefutable identifiable number that is attached to one owner.
*   Crypto reduces the need for individualized currencies and central banks. With blockchain, any of the cryptocurrencies can be sent to anywhere and anyone in the world without the need for their native currency exchange or without interference from central banks.
*   Cryptocurrencies have made certain people rich, with early adopters becoming billionaires. Whether this is a positive feature remains to be seen, as some retractors believe that speculators do not have the long-term benefits of crypto in mind.
*   More and more large corporations are fascinated around the idea of a blockchain-based payments using digital currency. In February 2021, Tesla famously announced that it would invest $1.5 billion into Bitcoin and accept it as payment for their electric cars.

# [Types of Cryptocurrency](#toc.xhtml#s43a)

There are thousands of cryptocurrencies present in the market today. Out of which, Bitcoin is the most popular and valuable cryptocurrency. An anonymous person, Satoshi Nakamoto, invented and introduced it to the world via a white paper in 2008.

Bitcoin, which was made available to the public in 2009, remains the most widely traded and covered cryptocurrency. As of November 2021, there were over 18.8 million bitcoins in circulation, with a total market cap of around $1.2 trillion. Only 21 million bitcoins will ever exist.

Most of the cryptocurrency providers claim to have a different specification and set of attractive functions. For example, Ethereum’s ether markets itself as a booster device for the underlying smart contract platform. Ripple’s XRP is used by banks to facilitate transfers between different geographies.

Bitcoin is by far the most popular cryptocurrency followed by other cryptocurrencies such as **Ethereum, Binance Coin, Solana,** and **Cardano.**

In the wake of Bitcoin’s success, many other cryptocurrencies, known as “**altcoins** (short for alternative coins),” have been launched. Some of these are clones or forks of Bitcoin, while others are new currencies that were built from scratch. They include **Solana, Litecoin, Ethereum, Cardano,** and **EOS**. By November 2021, the aggregate value of all the cryptocurrencies in existence had reached over $2.1 trillion — Bitcoin represented approximately 41% of that total value.

# [Advantages and Disadvantages of Cryptocurrency](#toc.xhtml#s44a)

Cryptocurrencies were introduced and traded with the intent to revolutionize financial infrastructure. As with every revolution, however, there are tradeoffs involved. Some advantages and disadvantages of cryptocurrencies are as follows:

**Advantages:**

*   A new and decentralized paradigm for money is represented by cryptocurrencies. This system eliminates the need for centralized middlemen, such as banks and other financial institutions, to uphold trust and oversee interparty transactions. So, a cryptocurrency-based system removes the chance that a single point of failure, like a Big Bank, could trigger a chain reaction of global financial crises similar to the one that happened in 2008 due to the failure of the US institutions.
*   The goal of cryptocurrencies is to facilitate direct money transfers between two people without the involvement of a credit card issuer or bank, which is a reliable third party. These decentralized transfers are safeguarded by implanting the public and private keys as well as various incentive schemes, such as PoS or PoW.
*   Compared to traditional money transfers, cryptocurrency transfers between two transacting parties are quicker because they do not involve third parties as middlemen. In decentralized finance, flash loans are an excellent illustration of this type of decentralized transfer. These loans are used for trading and can be completed in a matter of seconds without the need for collateral.
*   Coin investments have the potential to yield profits. Over the last 10 years, cryptocurrency markets have experienced a sharp increase in value, reaching nearly $2 trillion by 1st December. As of 2021, the value of Bitcoin in cryptocurrency markets exceeded $862 billion.
*   An experiment is being conducted by the remittance economy.

**Disadvantages:**

*   Though they claim to be an anonymous form of transaction, cryptocurrencies are actually pseudonymous. They leave a digital trail that agencies such as the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) or CBI in India can decipher. This opened up possibilities for governments or federal/central authorities to track the financial transactions of ordinary citizens.
*   Cryptocurrencies have become a popular tool with criminals for nefarious activities such as money laundering and illicit purchases. The case of Dread Pirate Roberts, who ran a marketplace to sell drugs on the dark web, is already well known. Cryptocurrencies have also become a favorite of hackers who use them for ransomware activities.
*   In theory, cryptocurrencies are meant to remain decentralized in operation, their wealth distributed between many parties on a blockchain. In real life, ownership is highly concentrated. For example, an MIT study found that just 11,000 investors held roughly 45% of Bitcoin’s surging value.
*   One of the conceits of cryptocurrencies is that anyone can mine them using a computer with an Internet connection. However, mining popular cryptocurrencies requires considerable energy, sometimes as much energy as entire countries consume. The expensive energy costs coupled with the unpredictability of mining have concentrated mining among large firms whose revenues run into billions of dollars. According to an MIT study, 10% of miners account for 90% of its mining capacity.
*   Though cryptocurrency blockchains are highly secure, other crypto repositories, such as exchanges and wallets, can be hacked. Many cryptocurrency exchanges and wallets have been hacked over the years, sometimes resulting in millions of dollars worth of “coins” stolen.
*   Cryptocurrencies traded in public markets suffer from price volatility. Bitcoin has experienced rapid surges and crashes in its value, climbing to as high as $17,738 in December 2017 before dropping to $7,575 in the following months. Some economists thus consider cryptocurrencies to be a short-lived fad or speculative bubble.
*   Cryptocurrencies are a new paradigm for **money**. Their promise is to streamline existing financial architecture to make it faster and cheaper. Their technology and architecture decentralize existing monetary systems and make it possible for transacting parties to exchange value and money independently of intermediary institutions such as banks.
*   Cryptocurrencies are generated by mining. For example, Bitcoin is generated using Bitcoin mining. The process involves downloading software that contains a partial or full history of transactions that have occurred in its network. Though anyone with a computer and an Internet connection can mine cryptocurrency, the energy- and resource-intensive nature of mining means that large firms dominate the industry. The process of creating Bitcoin to spend or trade consumes around 91 terawatt-hours of electricity annually, more than is used by Finland or Norway a nation of about 5.5 million.

# [Permissioned Model of BlockChain](#toc.xhtml#s45a)

It is now needless to say that blockchain technology works on principles of Decentralized Ledger Technology (DLT). DLT has opened up a multitude of opportunities for companies that are looking to innovate. It is not new; blockchain technology is one of those technologies that have radically changed how industries work.

# [Permissioned Blockchains](#toc.xhtml#s46a)

A permissioned blockchain is a blockchain network that requires access to the chain[^(\[12\])](#c01.xhtml#ftn12). In this blockchain type, a control layer runs on top of the blockchain that governs the actions performed by the allowed participants. As you can see, permissioned blockchains work entirely different than that of private and public blockchains. They are crafted to take advantage of blockchains without sacrificing the authority aspect of a centralized system.

Basically, there is one big difference between a *permissioned* and a *permissionless blockchain in the manner* the participants are going to interact with the network. A permissioned system is also known to have a restriction on the consensus participants, making permissioned networks highly configured and controlled by the owners. However, it is not a bad thing, but it is all about building a suitable blockchain network.

There are many popular permissioned blockchain frameworks available in the market. The list includes ***Hyperledger, Quorum, Corda****,* and *others.*

# [Benefits of Permissioned Blockchains](#toc.xhtml#s47a)

Let’s understand the beneficial features of permissioned blockchains.

There are many benefits of permissioned blockchains, which makes it the most preferable to use as compared with permissionless blockchains:

*   Permissioned blockchains offer superior performance compared to permissionless blockchains due to a limited number of nodes, eliminating unnecessary computations for reaching consensus and enhancing overall performance. Additionally, permissioned networks have pre-determined nodes for validating transactions.
*   Permissioned networks have a structured governance system that allows for quicker rule updates compared to public blockchains. Public blockchains face consensus problems as not all nodes collaborate for implementing updates, leading to slower network updates. In contrast, permissioned blockchains have nodes that work together for faster updates.
*   Permissioned networks effectively utilize blockchain for decentralized data storage.
*   Permissioned blockchain technology offers cost-effective solutions for various industries.

# [Consequences of Permissioned Blockchains](#toc.xhtml#s48a)

Permissioned blockchains have the following drawbacks:

*   **Compromised security**: Because all nodes correctly participate in the consensus process, permissionless public or private blockchains have better security. However, this might not apply to permissioned blockchains. A permissioned network’s security is only as strong as the integrity of its members. This implies that a limited group within a permissioned system may collaborate to alter network-stored data. The network’s integrity may be jeopardized in this way. The system should have the right permissions set in order to prevent those bad actors from combining to produce the intended effect.
*   **Control, Censorship, and Regulation**: In a perfect world, permissioned blockchains would function similarly to public blockchains under strict guidelines. Regulating bodies have the ability to impose restrictions on transactions or prevent them altogether, thereby introducing censorship into the network. Any company or organization using the permissioned network is at risk from these. Additionally, this strategy prevents the permissioned network from maximizing the benefits of the entire blockchain ecosystem.

# [Difference Between Permissioned and Permissionless Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s49a)

When we take a stock of the working of these two types of blockchain technology, there are many differences. *[Table 1.2](#c01.xhtml#tab1_2)* gives you a quick insight into the permissioned blockchain vs. permissionless blockchain:

 
| **Criteria** || **Permissioned Blockchain** || **Permissionless Blockchain** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **General Description** || A user has to be permitted by the blockchain authority before she or he could access the network. The user might join only if she or he gets an invitation || An open network system where all the devices can freely access without any type of permission. The ledger is shared and transparent |
| **Speed** || Faster || Slower |
| **Privacy** ||  A Private membership holder can also become a member || Transparent and open to anyone |
| **Legitimacy** || Legal || Illegal |
| **Ownership** || Nodes pre-defined the network || Managed by a group of Public ownership - no one owns |
| **Decentralization** || Partially decentralized || Truly decentralized |
| **Cost** || Cost-effective || Not so cost-effective |
| **Security** || Less secure || More secure |

**Table 1.2:** Permissioned Blockchain vs. Permissionless Blockchain

*[Figure 1.12](#c01.xhtml#fig1_12)* shows the distributed network of Public (permissionless) vs. Private (Permissioned) Blockchain.

![](images/Figure-1.12.jpg)

**Figure 1.12:** Public (Permissionless) vs. Private (Permissioned) Blockchain Network

# [Blockchain Security](#toc.xhtml#s50a)

Decentralized security and trust have been accomplished via Blockchain technology in a number of ways. New blocks are initially always kept both chronologically and linearly. Put differently, they are continuously appended to the blockchain’s “end”. Unless the majority of the network has come to a consensus to do so, it is very difficult to go back and change the contents of a block once it has been added to the end of the blockchain. This is so that each block has its own hash, which is also accompanied by the hash of the block that came before it and the time stamp that was previously mentioned. Digital information is converted into a string of numbers and letters by a mathematical function to create hash codes. Should that data be modified in any manner, the hash code will also be altered. Imagine that a hacker who is also a node operator on a blockchain network aims to change a blockchain and pilfer cryptocurrency from other users. Imagine if they changed the one copy they had, it would no longer match the copies of everyone else. The hacker’s version of the chain would be rejected as illegitimate when the other copies are compared to each other, and they notice that this one copy stands out. For the hack to be successful, at least 51 percent of the blockchain copies would need to be controlled and altered concurrently by the hacker in order for their new copy to become the majority copy and the agreed-upon chain. Additionally, a massive amount of funding and resources would be needed for such an attack.

# [Benefits and Drawbacks of Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s51a)

Despite its intricacy, blockchain technology offers virtually infinite possibilities for decentralized data storage. Beyond the aforementioned uses, blockchain technology may find use in areas such as increased security, reduced processing costs, and reduced user privacy.

**Benefits:**

*   Increased accuracy through the elimination of human verification
*   Lower costs through the elimination of third-party verification
*   Decentralization increases the difficulty of tampering with transactions
*   Secure, private, and efficient transactions
*   Transparent technology
*   Offers a banking alternative and a means of protecting personal information for citizens of nations with unstable or underdeveloped governments

**Drawbacks:**

In addition to the aforementioned benefits, there are certain drawbacks associated with blockchain technology. These include:

*   High technology costs involved in mining bitcoin
*   Limited transactions per second
*   Past associations with illicit activities on the dark web
*   Uncertainty and variation in regulations across different jurisdictions
*   Restrictions on data storage capacity
*   Speed and Data Inefficiency

# [Unlawful Behavior](#toc.xhtml#s52a)

On the blockchain network, secrecy safeguards users’ privacy and shields them from hackers, but it also permits illicit behavior and trading. The Silk Road, an online dark web bazaar for illegal drugs and money laundering that was operational from February 2011 until October 2013, when it was shut down by the US FBI, is likely the most frequently cited example of blockchain being used for criminal activities.

Using the Tor Browser, users can buy and sell illicit things without being detected on the dark web. They can also make illicit Bitcoin or other cryptocurrency purchases. Financial service providers are required by current U.S. rules to collect data about their clients when they open an account, authenticate each client, and ensure that clients do not appear on any list of recognized or suspected terrorist groups.

We can see the benefits and drawbacks of this arrangement. Financial accounts are accessible to anyone, but it also makes it easier for thieves to conduct business. Many have claimed that the benefits of cryptocurrencies, such as banking the unbanked, outweigh its drawbacks, particularly given that the majority of illicit activity is still carried out using untraceable cash.

Although Bitcoin was initially utilized for these kinds of things, its transparency and development as a financial tool have actually caused criminal behavior to shift to other cryptocurrencies like Dash and Monero. Nowadays, just a very small percentage of Bitcoin transactions are the result of illicit activities.

# [Business Benefits of Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s53a)

Let’s examine the two distinct financial systems side by side. All members of legacy business networks have their own ledgers, which are prone to duplication and inconsistencies that lead to disagreements, longer settlement periods, and the requirement for middlemen and their corresponding overhead expenses. However, organizations can save time and money while lowering risks by utilizing blockchain-based shared ledgers, where transactions cannot be changed once they are written to the ledger and certified by consensus.

Blockchain consensus techniques also offer near-real-time reference data, a consolidated, consistent dataset with fewer inaccuracies, and the ability for users to modify the descriptions of the assets they possess.

Because no participating member owns the source of origin for information stored in the shared ledger, blockchain technologies promote trust and integrity in the flow of transaction information among the participating members. Blockchain technologies’ inherent immutability mechanisms reduce audit costs and increase regulatory compliance while improving transparency. Additionally, businesses gain from faster execution times, lower costs, and less risk due to contracts executed on business networks using blockchain technologies. These factors allow businesses to engage with clients and create new revenue streams.

![](images/Figure-1.13.jpg)

**Figure 1.13:** Some Applications of Blockchain

# [Evolution of Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s54a)

Although blockchain is a very recent new technology, it already boasts a rich and interesting history. The following is a brief timeline of some of the most important and notable events in the development of blockchain.

To understand the type of data system the blockchain is, let’s take a look at the kinds of data software and systems we deal with in everyday scenarios.

We have been dealing with the following database technologies in the past:

*   A spreadsheet on the user’s computer
*   A spreadsheet in the user’s company’s data management system
*   A remote cloud-based working model
*   Blockchain: a distributed and public data management system

Let us explore the pros and cons of all the four systems.

**1\. A spreadsheet on the user’s computer**

When you create a new spreadsheet, you are essentially constructing an electronic version of information so you can store it on your computer’s internal hard drive as data. The spreadsheet software, as well as your internal hard drive (the hardware), are designed for a single person to use at a time.

**Benefits:**

*   As long as your computer remains secure, you will be the only one who has access to your data.
*   The spreadsheet software allows you to use formulas to automatically calculate values based on data in another data cell or spreadsheet stored on your hard drive.

If your spreadsheet software is compatible with your database tool, you will be able to automate data interactions between the two.

**Drawbacks:**

*   If your computer is stolen or your hard drive is corrupted, all of your data will begone.
*   Your computer could get hacked, giving unauthorized parties access to your data.
*   If you wanted someone else to work collaboratively on the same spreadsheet, you would have to send them the file. They would then have to return the latest version of the file. The file will pass to and from, resulting in many different versions of the same spreadsheet.

**2\. A spreadsheet in your company’s Data Management System**

When you create a spreadsheet on the computer system at work, the electronic data will be stored on powerful computers (the server) located downstairs in the IT department. Most likely, your company will have backup servers keeping a duplicate of the entire database - either on or off-premise.

Since the company’s servers store all the data generated by each person and department, the entire database will be accessible to people with the correct permissions - if they know where to look.

**Benefits:**

*   Anyone who has access to the server can be granted permission to read and/or write the same spreadsheet to improve collaboration.
*   Depending on the sophistication of your company’s software (ERP system), you will be able to automate data interactions between a number of your software tools. For example, should the projected profit margins calculated by one of your programs fall below a certain level, your email software would automatically send a notification to a relevant individual.

**Drawbacks:**

*   Everyone with access to the data can write, rewrite, or erase data, with no record kept of the changes. Should an error slip in or a willful manipulation of data occur, it will be very difficult to detect and trace it back to its source to correct and hold accountable the person at fault.
*   When audit time comes, you will need a team of auditors to sift through data stored in various locations on your server to combine it into a single, coherent report. Because they have to rely on the latest version of the file with no record of the changes (legitimate or illegitimate) that have occurred along the way, it will be impossible to guarantee the accuracy of the data they report. Failing an audit could result in fines and/or other penalties for the company.
*   Other departments or regional offices might use different software systems that are incompatible with yours. This creates data silos that limit collaboration and prevent anyone from gaining a Big Picture overview of operations.
*   The company is responsible for the entire cost of ownership of the system, including computers, servers, software, security, and the cost of employing IT personnel.
*   Company servers can go down and cause operational downtime.
*   Company servers are a prime target for cyber-attacks. If your server is hacked, sensitive data like client’s personal information and corporate secrets might be compromised. A ransomware attack could encrypt your data and hold you ransom to get access to the decryption key.
*   If a cyber-attack occurs, it will be essential to detect it early to isolate the affected machines. However, because your database allows people to read, write, and erase data, hackers can erase traces of their access. This will let them lurk in your servers until they can do maximal damage to your systems.

**3\. A remote cloud-based working model**

In a remote working setup, you would create your spreadsheets via Cloud-based software that stores data on a Cloud server, such as Google Drive.

In a cloud computing model, the servers that run your software and store your data are made up of powerful computers located in special remote warehouses (data center). Hundreds or even thousands of computers combine their power and storage so many clients (that is, different companies) and users can access the database simultaneously. To access your data from this data center, you could use an Internet connection or a more secure direct line.

**Benefits:**

*   If your server is hooked up to the Internet, you can collaborate with anyone, wherever they find themselves in the world.
*   Depending on your software’s sophistication (Cloud-based ERP system), you will be able to automate data interactions between your software tools.

**Drawbacks:**

*   Everyone with access to the data can write, rewrite, or erase data, with no record kept of the changes. Should an error slip in or a willful manipulation of data occur, it will be very difficult to detect and trace it back to its source.
*   Company auditors have to assume that the data in your system is accurate, even
*   though changes could have occurred between the time the data was logged and the present.
*   If your company needs to collaborate with other organizations, the other parties will have to use the same software. Alternatively, you would need a software integrations specialist to build a bridge to allow different programs to communicate with each other.
*   Although the cloud provider bears the costs of the outlay, maintenance, and security of their infrastructure, you will have to pay a subscription fee to use it. There are different costs associated with different types of data. For example, a customer would pay ingress and egress fees for accessing their archival data. If it’s a free product, the provider is probably selling your data to fund their business model.
*   When your provider’s servers go down, your data will become inaccessible.
*   Cloud servers can get hacked or mistakenly share your data with unauthorized individuals.

**4\. Blockchain: a distributed and public data management system**

The blockchain model is derived from the invention of Bitcoin. So, we consider the Bitcoin blockchain as the archetype for a blockchain system. Refer to *[Chapter 4, Distributed Consensus in Open Environments](#c04.xhtml)*, for more information.

1.  If your company operates on a public blockchain system, exact copies of your database will be stored on a computer network (the server) distributed across the globe.
2.  Each time someone changes the data, a new record will be published to indicate the latest version. The latest version will not overwrite any of your data’s previous versions but show the entire history of changes.
3.  Additionally, the record will show which party was responsible for making each change and indicate the date and time.
4.  The blockchain database is also publicly accessible. Unless data is encrypted before being recorded, anyone who accesses the database (via a blockchain explorer over the Internet) will be able to sift through the data. They won’t be able to make sense of how the data relates to other data or who created or made changes to any particular data record. This is because the blockchain attributes pseudonyms to each person who interacts with it in the form of a blockchain address. And, unless they reveal their pseudonym to you, you would need incredibly sophisticated algorithms to decipher the identity behind a blockchain address.

![](images/Figure-1.14.jpg)

**Figure 1.14:** Isometric View of Universal Blockchain Technology

# [History of Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s55a)

*   **1990**: In the late 1990s, cypherpunk Nick Szabo advocated securing a digital payments system called bit gold (which was never implemented) with a blockchain.
*   **1991**: Mathematicians Stuart Haber and W. Scott Stornetta first proposed the concept of blockchain technology in 1991. They aimed to create a system in which document timestamps could not be altered.
*   **2008**: “Bitcoin: A Peer to Peer Electronic Cash System” is published by an anonymous individual or group going by the handle Satoshi Nakamoto.
*   **2009**: Computer scientist Hal Finney and the enigmatic Satoshi Nakamoto conduct the first Bitcoin (BTC) transaction that is successful.
*   **2010**: Two Papa John’s pizzas are the first items ever purchased with Bitcoin by Florida-based programmer Laszlo Hanycez. Hanycez sent 10,000 BTC, which at the time was equivalent to roughly $60. It is now valued at $80 million. Officially, Bitcoin’s market capitalization surpasses $1 million.
*   **2011**: The cryptocurrencies reach parity with the US dollar, with 1 Bitcoin equaling $1 USD. 
    
    Wikileaks, the Electronic Frontier Foundation, and other organizations have begun to accept Bitcoin donations.
    
*   **2012**: The introduction of blockchain and cryptocurrencies into popular culture with references to them in series like The Good Wife. Vitalik Buterin, one of the original Bitcoin developers, established Bitcoin Magazine.
*   **2013**: The market value of bitcoin reached $1 billion. For the first time, Bitcoin reached $100/BTC.
*   **2014**: Overstock.com, The D Las Vegas Hotel, and gaming company Zynga begin to accept Bitcoin as payment. Through an Initial Coin Offering (ICO), Buterin’s Ethereum Project is crowdfunded, raising over $18 million in Bitcoin and creating new opportunities for blockchain technology. To find new applications for blockchain technology, a consortium of more than 200 blockchain companies, called R3, is established. PayPal announces the incorporation of Bitcoin.
*   **2015**: There are more than 100,000 retailers that take Bitcoin. To test the technology for exchanging shares in private companies, the San Francisco blockchain startup Chain and the American stock market NASDAQ are collaborating.
*   **2016**: The announcement by IBM of a blockchain strategy for cloud-based enterprise applications. 
    
    The legitimacy of cryptocurrency and blockchain technology is acknowledged by the Japanese government. Bitcoin crossed $1,000/BTC for the first time in 2017. The market value of cryptocurrencies hits $150 billion.
    
    CEO of JP Morgan, Jamie Dimon, has expressed his belief in blockchain as a technology for the future, endorsing the ledger system with support from Wall Street. Bitcoin hits $19,783.21/BTC, its all-time high.
    
    Dubai declares that by 2020, its government will run on blockchain technology.
    
*   **2018**: Facebook says it will launch a group focused on blockchain technology and makes a suggestion that it might launch its own coin. IBM is creating a blockchain-based banking platform, and major institutions like Barclays and Citi are participating.
*   **2019**: As China’s central bank declares it is developing its own cryptocurrency, President Ji Xinping of China openly welcomes blockchain technology. CEO of Twitter and Square Jack Dorsey said that blockchain developers will be hired by Square to work on the company’s next cryptocurrency initiatives. Additionally, the New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) has announced the founding of Bakkt, a cryptocurrency trading and digital wallet startup.
*   **2020**: By the end of the year, Bitcoin nearly hits $30,000. PayPal declares that users will be able to purchase, sell, and hold cryptocurrency. 
    
    The Bahamas, aptly dubbed the “Sand Dollar,” introduced the world’s first digital money from its central bank. Blockchain emerges as a major weapon in the fight against COVID-19, namely for the safe storage of patient data and research data.
    
*   **2021**: The year 2021 was a roller coaster ride for crypto investors as bitcoin value surged to $64,829.14 (highest yet) and declined to $35,000 as well. 
    
    In March, the world’s richest man, Elon Musk, announced the acceptance of Bitcoins as payment for Tesla cars. Later, he withdrew his statement due to environmental hazards as bitcoin mining requires high computation power.
    
    El Salvador became the first country to accept Bitcoin as a legal currency.
    
    Afterward, a giant wave of (Non-fungible Token) NFTs came with great earning opportunities for creators. An NFT is a digital asset such as digital art, music records, videos, or collectibles that is unique and tradable. Platforms like OpenSea, one of the most popular NFT marketplaces, presented a stage for artists and traders. An intersection of Blockchain, AR/VR, and web3 gave rise to Metaverse. Several start-ups inspired by COVID-19 pandemic work from home culture started working on virtual world creation.
    
    On 28 October, Mark Zuckerberg CEO of social network giant Facebook, renamed the company, Meta. In contrast, the cryptocurrency bill in India banned the use of cryptocurrencies as a payment system. On the other hand, the idea of web3 was promoted worldwide.
    
*   **2022**: The ongoing year 2022 started with more fame towards NFTs and Metaverse. That eventually led to more trading options and earning opportunities. Several rich apparel companies like Nike, Adidas, Walmart, Puma, Gucci, Ferrari, and even Disney got on Metaverse to create and promote their product NFT designs for avatars. 
    
    In contrast, the Indian government announced 30% taxation on digital asset transfers. Yet, it doesn’t mean that it’s legal in the country.
    
    However, various other blockchain use cases are being explored, tested, and released, such as smart contracts for satellite communications in Space, the use of blockchain in the supply chain, entertainment, healthcare, transportation, real estate, automation, and many more industries.
    
    Meanwhile, due to recent war tension between Russia and Ukraine, the Bitcoin community showed financial help to Ukrainian injured soldiers and civilians.
    
*   **2023**: The leading blockchains are Bitcoin, Ethereum, Solana, Avalanche, and BNB Chain. Each offers unique attributes and advancements, distinguishing them in the decentralized technology space. Former FTX CEO Sam Bankman-Fried, a notable figure in the cryptocurrency industry, was found guilty on 3 November 2023 by a New York jury. The charges included two counts of wire fraud, two counts of wire fraud conspiracy, and three additional conspiracy charges.
*   **2024**: Blockchain technology, the force behind cryptocurrencies like Bitcoin, has moved far beyond its initial niche. Industries are rapidly recognizing the potential of this secure, decentralized ledger system to transform everything from finance to supply chain management.

# [Likely Future Trends](#toc.xhtml#s56a)

The blockchain technology is much more than a system for securely transferring cryptocurrencies. The industry has a staggering CAGR rate of over 69% between 2019 and 2025. During this period, blockchain is no longer only relevant to cryptocurrency, blockchain-based applications are emerging to support many other scenarios that require decentralized data storage and accessibility.

The fact that blockchain is ushering in major economic shifts. However, the cryptocurrency market is still a ‘wild west’ with little regulation. According to recent reports, it appears the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission is gearing up to more closely regulate the cryptocurrency industry in 2022.

Also, with many practical applications for the technology already being implemented and explored, blockchain is finally making a name for itself in no small part because of bitcoin and cryptocurrency. As a buzzword on the tongue of every investor in the nation, blockchain stands to make business and government operations more accurate, efficient, secure, and cheap, with fewer middlemen.

As we prepare to head into the third decade of blockchain, it’s no longer a question of if legacy companies will catch on to the technology — it’s a question of when. Today, that is, in 2022 start, we see a proliferation of NFTs and the tokenization of assets. The next decades will prove to be an important period of growth for blockchain.

# [Additional Information](#toc.xhtml#s57a)

*   **Numbers of Blockchains**
    
    As of 2022, there are more than 10,000 active cryptocurrencies based on blockchain, with several hundred more non-cryptocurrency blockchains.
    
*   **Blockchain Platform**
    
    Nevertheless, the blockchain platform allows users and developers to create novel uses of an existing blockchain infrastructure. One example is Ethereum, which has a native cryptocurrency known as ether (ETH).
    
    However, the Ethereum blockchain alternative also allows the creation of smart contracts and programmable tokens used in initial coin offerings (ICOs), and non-fungible tokens (NFTs). These are all built up around the Ethereum infrastructure and secured by nodes on the Ethereum network.
    

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml#s58a)

In this chapter, we have learned in detail about block technology, its inception, and partial adoption. Its application is predominantly in the financial sector and in generating and earning cryptocurrencies like bitcoins and Ethereum. We have also learned that this technology is beyond the control of several governments and other political powers. It involves many complications and requires a lot of skills, time, and huge electrical power.

In the next chapter, we shall learn about its core part, such as cryptographic hash functions, which help in keeping the transactions confidential to avoid misuse by miscreants.

# [CHAPTER 2](#toc.xhtml#c02)

# [Cryptographic Hash Function](#toc.xhtml#c02)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml#s60a)

Cryptography is utilized in blockchain to make message transmission very secret and secure. For this purpose, hash functions are used, which make the scripting very complicated by using mathematical functions and random probability. We will learn all of this in this chapter.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml#s61a)

In this chapter, we will cover the following topics:

*   Cryptographic Hash Function (CHF)
*   The Nonce
*   An Introduction to One-Way Hash Functions
*   Hash Pointer and Merkle tree (Hash Tree)
*   Digital Signature
*   Public Key cryptography or Asymmetric Cryptography

# [Introduction to Cryptographic Hash Function](#toc.xhtml#s62a)

The Greek term “cryptography” originally meant “secret writing.” The history of cryptocurrencies can be traced back to academic and military use cases, which were eventually taken away for private communication.

It is noteworthy that symbol **#** (known as *hash in information technology*) is mostly used for showing the numbering or counts of any item, but in modern digital cryptography, it is used for encrypted messages and vice versa.

To form a chronological single source of truth for the data, blockchain stores encrypted blocks of data, which are then chained together in blockchain technology. As such digital assets are distributed instead of copied or transferred to create an immutable record, the assets so created are decentralized to allow full real-time access and thus are transparent to the public using it. This transparency of ledger of changes maintains and preserves the integrity of the document, creating trust for users in the asset.

The inherent security measures provided in the blockchain system, along with its quality of being a public ledger, make it a prime technology for almost every single sector. Thus, blockchain is a promising and undoubtedly revolutionary technology as it helps to (i) reduce risk, (ii) stamps out fraud, and (iii) bring transparency. Thus, it is scalable and utilized in many ways for myriad uses.

The purpose of a hash function is to turn a random input of data (keys) into a string of bytes with a fixed length and structure **hash value**). In *[Figure 2.1](#c02.xhtml#fig2_1)*, the encrypted hash message contains 10 bytes in each case. Cryptographic hash functions generate a fixed-length character string from data records of any length (shown in left). A data record of any block can be a *word,* a *sentence,* a *longer text, or an entire file.*

The hash function has become the backbone of crypto security. The hash provides a manner in which it is easy to identify transactions on the blockchain.

![](images/Figure-2.1.jpg)

**Figure 2.1:** Hash Function Generated for Similar but Different Text Strings (The crypted message is vastly different)

It can be easily observed from *[Figure 2.1](#c02.xhtml#fig2_1)* that the output message so generated after hashing is vastly dissimilar if a slight change in the input message is done by the user. Always remember that a blockchain is a growing list of records, called blocks, that are linked together using cryptography.

Each block contains a:

*   Cryptographic hash of the previous block,
*   A timestamp, and
*   Transaction data (generally represented as a Merkle tree, we shall read about this in the section *The Nonce*).

The **timestamp** is adopted to prove that the transaction data existed when the block was published (posted) in order to get into its hash. As each block contains information about the block previous to it, they form a continuous chain, with each additional block reinforcing the ones before it.

The data in any given block cannot be altered retroactively without altering all subsequent blocks as blockchains are resistant to modification.

“Although blockchain records are not unalterable as forks are possible, blockchains may be considered secure by design and exemplify a distributed computing system with high Byzantine fault tolerance”^([\[1\]](#c02.xhtml#ftn1),[\[5\]](#c02.xhtml#ftn5)).

Blockchain uses peer-to-peer network technology that is used as a publicly distributed ledger in which blockchains are transmitted as bonafide transactions.

Researchers and other people involved in this movement refer to themselves as “**cypherpunks**” and have been advocating a shift to greater privacy and control over our data since the late 1980s.

# [Hashing](#toc.xhtml#s63a)

Hashing simply means passing some data through a formula that produces a result, called a **hash**. Any hash (sentence) so created is usually a string of characters and the hashes generated by a formula are always the same *length*, regardless of how much data you feed into it.

The term hash rate is a measure of how many calculations can be performed per second and can be measured in *billions, trillions, quadrillions, and quintillions*. For example, a hash rate of 1ST/s means one trillion (10⁹) hashed calculations can be performed every second. These calculations that are being measured are concerned with **mining**.

The cryptographic hashes are generated to secure data transferred on a public network.

# [Miners](#toc.xhtml#s64a)

**Miners** compete with their *peers to zero* in on a hash value generated by a crypto coin transaction, and the first miner to crack the code gets to add the block to the ledger and receive the *reward*.

Interested Bitcoin miners download the entire history of blockchain and assemble valid transactions into a block. If the block of assembled transactions is accepted and verified by other miners, then the miner receives a block reward.

Transaction fees provide another incentive for bitcoin miners to participate in the process.

Currently, Antminer S19 Pro-ASIC is one of the best Bitcoin mining hardware solutions. This hardware can also be used to mine other SHA-256 cryptocurrencies. The advantages of this hardware are as follows:

*   Highest hash rate
*   Efficiency and power consumption
*   It is said to generate almost US$12 on a regular basis, with an electricity cost of US$0.1/kilowatt.

Ethereum at a mining hash rate of 750.00 MH/s consumes 1,350.00 watts of power at $0.10 per kWh, and a block reward of 2 ETH.

Before proceeding further, let us learn about some definitions:

*   **Hash rate** is a measure of the computational power of a blockchain network, group, or individual. Hash rate is determined by how many guesses are made per second. The overall hash rate helps determine the mining difficulty of a blockchain network.
*   **Hashing power** (otherwise known as Hash power) is the power that your computer or hardware uses to run and solve different hashing algorithms. These algorithms are used for generating new cryptocurrencies and allowing transactions between them. This process is also called mining.
*   **Mining difficulty** is a unit of measurement used in the process of mining a cryptocurrency such as Bitcoin and refers to how difficult and time-consuming it is to find the right hash for each block.
*   ETH is short for Ethereum.

# [Non-Fungible tokens (NFTs)](#toc.xhtml#s65a)

Non-fungible tokens (NFTs) are blockchain-based tokens that may represent a unique asset like a piece of art, digital content, or media. NFT is assumed as an irrevocable digital certificate of ownership and authenticity for a given asset in both formats, either digital or physical.

# [Cryptographic Hash Function (CHF)](#toc.xhtml#s66a)

CHF is an acronym for **Cryptographic Hash Function** and is a mathematical algorithm that maps data of an arbitrary size to a bit array of a fixed size. It is a one-way function, that is, a function for which it is practically infeasible to invert or reverse the computation Brute force approach is an approach that finds all the possible solutions to find a satisfactory solution to a given problem. The brute force algorithm tries out all the possibilities till a satisfactory solution is found.

In an ideal situation, there is no other way to find a message that produces a given hash other than to attempt a brute-force search of possible inputs to see if they produce a match, or use a rainbow table of matched hashes.

We need the Rainbow tables for protecting passwords, which are actually tables of reversed hashes used to crack password hashes. In fact, computer systems require passwords typically to store the passwords as a hash value of the user’s password. When a computer user enters a password, the system hashes the password and compares it to the stored hash. Normally, when you crack a password hash, your computer computes a word, generates the hash, and then compares it to see if there is a match. If there is a match, the password is correct; if not, it will keep guessing. Rainbow tables work on the principle of a time-memory trade-off. From a modern password cracking threat perspective though, rainbow tables are mostly obsolete. At the rate of 200,000 hashes per second, it would take 3 minutes to generate a four-character rainbow table.

The cryptographic hash functions are a basic tool of modern cryptography. Values returned by a hash function are called **message digest** or simply hash values. *[Figure 2.2](#c02.xhtml#fig2_2)* illustrates the working of a hash function.

![](images/Figure-2.2.jpg)

**Figure 2.2:** Working of cryptologic hash function (Courtesy: Tutorial Point)

A cryptographic hash function is devised as an algorithm that takes an arbitrary amount of data input (that is, a credential). This generates a fixed-size output in the form of enciphered text, which is called a hash value, or just “hash in short”. The most important thing to note is that enciphered text can then be stored in place of the password itself, and later used to verify the user.

A hash algorithm (program) is designed to take an arbitrary-length data input and produce a fixed-length deterministic result, a digital fingerprint of the input. For any specific input, the resulting hash will always have to be the same and can be easily calculated and verified by any user implementing the same hash algorithm.

It is important to understand that the key characteristic of a cryptographic hash algorithm is that it is virtually impossible to find two different inputs that produce the same fingerprint. As a corollary, it is also virtually impossible to select an input in such a way as to produce a desired fingerprint, other than trying random inputs. Corollary to any theorem means forming a proposition that follows from one already proved in the concerned theorem.

As for Bitcoin is concerned, it uses the SHA-256 hash algorithm. The function of this algorithm is to generate verifiably random numbers in a way that requires a predictable amount of computer processing power. With the famous SHA256, the output is always 256 bits long, regardless of the size of the input. In Example 2.1, we will use the Python interpreter to calculate the SHA256 hash of the phrase, “I am Satoshi Nakamoto.”[^(\[2\])](#c02.xhtml#ftn2).

**Example 2.1: SHA256 Example**

`$ python`

`Python 2.7.1`

`>>> import hashlib`

`>>> print hashlib. sha256("I am Satoshi Nakamoto").hexdigest()`

`5d7c7ba21cbbcd75d14800b100252d5b428e5b1213d27c385bc141ca6b47989e`

*Example 2.1* shows the result of calculating the hash of `"I am Satoshi Nakamoto"`: `5d7c7ba21cbbcd75d14800b100252d5b428e5b1213d27c385bc141ca6b47989e`. This 256-bit number is the hash or digest of the phrase and depends on every part of the phrase. Adding a single letter, punctuation mark, or any other character will produce a different hash.

Now, if we change the phrase (part of the data/sentence inputted), we should expect to see completely different hashes. Let’s try that by adding a number to the end of our phrase, using the simple Python scripting in *Example 2.2*.

**Example 2.2**: **SHA256:** A script for generating many hashes by iterating on a nonce

\# example of iterating a nonce in a hashing algorithm’s input

`import hashlib`

`text = "I am Satoshi Nakamoto"`

`# iterate nonce from 0 to 19`

`for nonce in xrange(20):`

`# add the nonce to the end of the text`

`input = text + str(nonce)`

`# calculate the SHA-256 hash of the input (text+nonce)`

`hash = hashlib.sha256(input).hexdigest()`

`# show the input and hash result`

`print input, '=>', hash`

Running this code snippet will produce the hashes of several phrases, made different by adding a number at the end of the text. By incrementing the number, you will get different hashes, as shown in *Example 2.3.*

**Example 2.3:** SHA256 output of a script for generating many hashes by iterating on a nonce

`$ python hash_example.py`

`I am Satoshi Nakamoto0 => a80a81401765c8eddee25df36728d732…`

`I am Satoshi Nakamoto1 => f7bc9a6304a4647bb41241a677b5345f…`

`I am Satoshi Nakamoto6 => 4a2fd48e3be420d0d28e202360cfbaba…`

`I am Satoshi Nakamoto7 => 790b5a1349a5f2b909bf74d0d166b17a…`

`I am Satoshi Nakamoto11 => 7045da6ed8a914690f087690e1e8d66…`
