# [CHAPTER 4](#toc.xhtml#c04)

# [Distributed Consensus in Open Environments](#toc.xhtml#c04)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml#s141a)

Consensus is the process by which a group of peers (competitor’s or their authorized agents) — known as ‘nodes’ — on a network determine which blockchain transactions are valid and which are not. Consensus mechanisms are the methodologies used to achieve this agreement. By ensuring most users on the network agree on the state of the ledger, consensus mechanisms maintain the security and integrity of the blockchain. The consensus mechanism maintains the security of the blockchain by keeping a record of all legitimate transactions. Since crypto trading is a decentralized process, this becomes important to stop sellers from deliberately cheating a buyer.

This chapter delves into the world of distributed systems, where data and tasks are spread across multiple devices or computers. We’ll also explore different types of consensus mechanisms such as Proof of Work (PoW), Proof of Stake (PoS), and more, used in cryptocurrencies including Bitcoin. We will also understand how they contribute to reliable and secure data management in decentralized environments.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml#s142a)

In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:

*   Consensus in a Bitcoin Network
*   Public Blockchains Function
*   HashCash Protocol for PoW
*   Bitcoin and PoW
*   The Proof of Stake (PoS)
*   Attacks on PoW and the Monopoly
*   Proof of Burn (PoB)
*   Proof of Elapsed Time (PoET) Blockchain
*   Proof of History (PoH)
*   The Hardness of Mining and Mining Difficulty
*   Mining Pool

# [Consensus in a Bitcoin Network](#toc.xhtml#s143a)

Shared databases were created when computers and networks became popular in the 1980s and 90s. They were created so that multiple users could access the data they stored. Most of them had a centralized database with permissions that people could access from various workstations. This arrangement grew into centralized networks with administrators who gave user rights and ensured data integrity.

Programming for some of these shared databases spread processing and storage power across a network of storage devices located in multiple places. Preventing data tampering and unauthorized access — whether malicious or not — was one of the most important issues that needed to be addressed. To ensure that the information was not altered, a method was needed to automate distributed database management.

Distributed autonomous consensus refers to a method where network programs agree on state of a shared database using cryptographic techniques. This agreement was intended to be accomplished by using encryption algorithms to generate long strings of alphanumeric numbers, known as hashes, which were then validated by programs running on the network. Hashes only change if the hashing algorithm’s input is changed, so programs are designed to compare hashes to make sure they are similar in distributed systems.

Distributed consensus is a process to reach a common agreement in a distributed or decentralized multi-agent platform. It is crucial for the message transmission system.

![](images/Figure-4.1.jpg)

**Figure 4.1:** Blockchain Process and Attackers

An attack on a blockchain that uses a PoW algorithm for consensus is possible if the attackers have over 50 percent control of the network hash rate.

If this is the case, the controlling CPU power will allow an attacker to create a separate chain from any previous block in the blockchain. Given that it has the majority of computing power, its new chain will eventually overtake the accepted chain by the network, thereby defining a new transaction history.

In this new chain, the attackers are able to double spend virtual currency, meaning that the funds that have already been spent on the network’s chain could be spent again on the attackers’ chain.

With Bitcoin, staging a 51% attack on the network is seemingly unlikely, simply due to the size of the network and its hash rate. One estimate puts the cost of running a 51% attack on Bitcoin at just over 15 billion USD. Many altcoins, however, are much more at risk.

Numerous 51% attacks have taken place in recent years. In August 2021, Bitcoin SV (BSV) slid about 5%value after an attack. Another Bitcoin fork, Bitcoin Gold (BTG), suffered a 51% attack in 2019.

Because there is a central server in a typical software architecture, consensus is rarely an issue; instead, the other nodes just need to line up with the server. To reach a consensus, each node in a distributed network like blockchain serves as both a host and a server and must communicate with other nodes. The consensus process may occasionally be severely hampered or destroyed by malevolent nodes in addition to downed or unavailable nodes. As a result, a good consensus protocol can reduce the harm and accept the occurrence of these phenomena without affecting the consensus’s ultimate outcome.

Generally speaking, every new block that is added to the Blockchain is guaranteed by the consensus protocol to be the only version of the truth that is accepted by every node in the Blockchain.

Let’s reiterate: Blockchain is an immutable, private, secure, and transparent distributed decentralized network. Despite the lack of a central authority to validate and verify the transactions, each and every transaction on the Blockchain is thought to be totally secure. Only the consensus mechanism, an essential component of every Blockchain network, makes this feasible.

The process by which all of the peers in the Blockchain network come to a consensus regarding the current state of the distributed ledger is known as the algorithm (of consensus). Consensus algorithms accomplish this by building trust amongst strangers in a distributed computing environment and ensuring reliability in the Blockchain network. In essence, the consensus mechanism ensures that each new block added to the Blockchain represents the singular, accepted version of the truth accepted by all of the nodes in the Blockchain.

# [Consensus-Building Process](#toc.xhtml#s144a)

In computer and blockchain systems, a consensus mechanism should essentially be a fault-tolerant method that is used to get the required agreement on a single data value or a single state of the network across distributed processes or multi-agent systems, like with cryptocurrencies[^(\[1\])](#c04.xhtml#ftn1). This ensures that record-keeping benefits from its utilization. For example, the Proof-of-Work (PoW) consensus process in the Bitcoin blockchain necessitates the use of computer power to solve a challenging but arbitrary problem in order to maintain network integrity for all nodes.

# [The Mechanism of Consensus](#toc.xhtml#s145a)

A central administrator has the power to maintain and update any conventional centralized system, such as a database containing important data regarding driver’s licenses in a nation. Any adjustments, such as adding, removing, or amending the names of individuals who meet the requirements for a particular license, are carried out by a central authority that is still the only one responsible for keeping accurate records.

# [Public Blockchains Function](#toc.xhtml#s146a)

Public blockchains function as globally distributed, autonomous, decentralized networks that are not governed by a single entity. They are made possible by the participation of hundreds of thousands of users who work on block mining and the verification and validation of transactions that take place on the blockchain.

Due to the dynamic nature of blockchain technology, publicly accessible ledgers require a mechanism that is efficient, equitable, real-time, functional, dependable, and secure to guarantee that all network transactions are authentic and that all participants reach a consensus regarding the ledger’s status. The consensus mechanism, a collection of guidelines that determines the validity of contributions made by the various blockchain participants (that is, nodes or transactors), carries out this crucial duty.

An open Blockchain is a public network that keeps an unchangeable record of all transactions. By following specified regulations, anyone can publish a transaction and take part in the network. Examples of open blockchains are Litecoin and Bitcoin. In simpler words, it’s a shared digital event ledger accessible to all system members.

Open Blockchain is a shared record of digital events, or transactions, among many users who have an interest in the system. Information entered into the ledger cannot be removed after it has been recorded and may only be changed by consensus among the participants. Every event that is recorded can be cryptographically verified by providing evidence of participant agreement.

Technically speaking, Open Blockchain is a fabric architecture that enables companies to use the strength of distributed, peer-to-peer, immutable (append-only), cryptographically secure databases. These characteristics, pioneered by Ethereum and Bitcoin, make blockchains secure and reliable for recording transactions.

Blockchain is a distributed ledger system that operates on a peer-to-peer basis, catering to a new breed of transaction applications. It streamlines corporate procedures while establishing transparency, accountability, and trust. In contrast to public blockchains, some systems use a modular private blockchain architecture.

This approach is used to store and retrieve transactions on a private ledger. In this context, the term “transaction” can refer to many different things, including data, assets, identities, and instructions. For various domains, a system that integrates the information storage and the transactional processing protocol is quite beneficial. A key advantage of this modular design is that network administrators can specify their own limitations and then configure the protocol to meet those needs. This approach is beneficial for creating permissioned networks.

An endless number of distinct actors can build their own networks with the help of this open-source fabric. Communities establish a permissioned network in which known whitelisted entities run both validating and non-validating nodes. A network issuing authority grants access to these identities. This concept differs greatly from blockchains that are in use today. Traditional blockchain technology are expanded upon by Open Blockchain through the following:

*   **Logic (Chain Code):** Chain code is an extension of conventional smart-contracts, which are generally understood to be self-executing contracts expressed in code that can interact with one another and trigger other smart-contracts. Chain-code can communicate with other hlp-fabric-golang networks or the outside world while running in sandboxed Docker containers. Additionally, chain-code inherits confidentiality and privacy and the ability to store persistent data.
*   **Variable Confidentiality**: Networks have the ability to control who can see and engage with the environment at various levels. This extends to transactions, where specific privacy standard can be applied.
*   **Verifiable Identification:** Although the network has the ability to obfuscate identities, it is nevertheless feasible to have peers that are 100% anonymous and whose identities can be independently verified using safe cryptography techniques. An auditor can de-anonymize users and transactions on a network if the users provide authorization. This transparency is crucial for regulatory analysis and inspections.
*   **Private Transactions:** All transaction information is encrypted, including but not limited to peers, assets, volumes, and chain-code. This removes the possibility of identifying patterns or of private information being disclosed to unauthorized users on the network. Exclusive actors possess the ability to decode, observe, interact, and execute (using chain-code).
*   **Customizable Consensus Protocols:** Almost any consensus mechanism, like proof-of-work or proof-of-stake, can be used with the fabric to operate with ease. There are many applications for Open Blockchain, owing to its adaptable architectural architecture. For example, the Proof-of-Work (PoW) consensus process used in the Bitcoin blockchain demands the application of computational resources to solve a challenging but arbitrary puzzle in order to maintain the integrity of all nodes in the network

PoS mechanism is covered in length in section *Attacks on PoW and the Monopoly*.

# [Workproofing](#toc.xhtml#s147a)

In 2009, Satoshi Nakamoto, creator of Bitcoin, intended for it to be a decentralized, distributed consensus form of money. This innovation aimed to achieve decentralization in both the financial and technological aspects of money. For example, when you use a trusted system to transact money, the debit and credit transactions are handled by a third party (think banks, credit/debit cards, PayPal, and more).

The bank act as intermediaries, debiting the sender’s account (for example, Mark) and crediting the recipient’s account (for example, Sally) with the corresponding amount.

Until the transaction is complete, none of the transacted funds belong to Mark or Sally because they are all managed by and within the unidentified third party.

Bitcoin, on the other hand, disrupts this centralized model. It operates on a peer-to-peer network, eliminating the need for a trusted intermediary. This approach aims to empower users with greater control over their finances and potentially fosters transparency and trust in the financial system.

# [The Proof of Work (PoW)](#toc.xhtml#s148a)

Several consensus mechanism algorithms exist, each based on a distinct set of principles. These include:

*   Proof of Work (PoW)
*   Proof of Stake (PoS)

For example, the Proof-of-Work (PoW) consensus process used in the Bitcoin blockchain demands the application of computational resources to solve a challenging but arbitrary puzzle in order to maintain the integrity of all nodes in the network. PoS mechanism is covered in length in section *Attacks on PoW and the Monopoly*.

In Proof-of-Work, participating nodes must demonstrate that the work they have completed and submitted qualifies them to be granted permission to add new transactions to the blockchain. However, entire bitcoin mining process requires a lot of energy and takes a long time to process.

Cryptocurrencies like Ethereum and Bitcoin have embraced the PoW. In every round of consensus, PoW uses a competition between compute power to choose one node to produce a new block. The participating nodes in the challenge have to figure out a cryptographic riddle. The ability to add a new block may belong to the node that solves the puzzle first. *[Figure 4.1](#c04.xhtml#fig4_1)* shows the block formation process in PoW flow.

It is very difficult to solve a PoW puzzle. Nodes need to constantly adjust the random value (nonce) to get the correct answer, which requires much computational power. It is feasible for a malicious attacker to overthrow one block in a chain, but this amounts to increase the number of valid blocks in the chain. The workload gets accumulated, therefore overthrowing a long chain requires a huge amount of computational power. PoW belongs to the probabilistic-finality consensus protocols since it guarantees eventual consistency. In Blockchain, all nodes maintain and work on the same distributed ledger.

The following table illustrates the properties of atomic broadcast in distributed consensus:

 
| **Properties** || **Note** |
| --- | --- |
| **Validity** || This validity guarantees that if a message is broadcast by a valid node, it will be correctly included within the consensus protocol. |
| **Agreement** || Agreement provides the guarantee that if a message is delivered to a valid node, it will ultimately be delivered to all valid nodes. |
| **Integrity** || It is to ensure that a message is broadcast only once by a valid node. |
| **Total Order** || It is to ensure that all nodes agree to the order of all delivered messages. |

**Table 4.1:** Atomic broadcast Properties of Distributed Consensus Protocols

A consensus protocol should have the following three properties; namely consistency, availability, and fault tolerance[^(\[2\])](#c04.xhtml#ftn2). These properties are elaborated in *[Table 4.2](#c04.xhtml#tab4_2):*

 
| **Properties** || **Note** |
| --- | --- |
| Safety/ Consistency || A consensus protocol is considered safe (or consistent) only when all nodes produce the same valid output, according to the protocol rules, for the same atomic broadcast |
| Liveness/ availability ||  If all non-faulty participating nodes produce an output (indicating the termination of the protocol), the protocol is considered live. |
| Fault Tolerance ||  It exhibits the network’s capability to perform as intended in the midst of node failures |

**Table 4.2:** Properties of Distributed Consensus Protocols

# [Open and Closed Types of Blockchains](#toc.xhtml#s149a)

Unlike traditional financial institutions that rely on trust, Bitcoin operates as a trustless system. This might seem counterintuitive, but it actually allows for secure peer-to-peer transactions without the need for a middleman.

![](images/Figure-4.2.jpg)

**Figure 4.2:** Creation of a new block after getting verified by consensus algorithm

Here, miners take the position of the traditional mediators, and their job is to ensure that transactions involving Bitcoin are successfully completed on behalf of the cryptocurrency’s owners. Miners use the computing power of their computers to decipher the encrypted algorithms contained in each transaction in order to ensure that it is accepted. In a Proof of Work system, miners compete to confirm the validity of each and every transaction in the candidate block, or the block that is presently being constructed[^(\[3\])](#c04.xhtml#ftn3). They need to solve the encrypted puzzles that confirm the coins’ integrity in order to accomplish this. Block rewards, or a portion of the transacted currency, are given to the fortunate first miner who solves these problems. When the issue is resolved, the transactions form a block that is kept on the blockchain as a public record, and the miner notifies everyone on the network of the resolution.

PoW is determined by computational output and competition. Imagine there is an international math tournament, and the competitors (the miners) are handed a proof (the block) that has never been solved. The first person to solve this proof will receive a reward (a block reward), and once it is, it will be made public online, establishing a new blockchain block.

# [Advantages of Proof of Work](#toc.xhtml#s150a)

A Proof of Work system, as previously mentioned, requires computers to perform a small amount of additional work before carrying out a requested operation. After this additional work is completed, a solution is produced and shared with the other computers connected to the network. The accuracy of the answer and the approval of any action may be simply verified by the other computers.

One essential feature of a Proof of Work system is that solving a problem should be rather challenging, while confirming that a given solution actually solves the problem should be very simple. Cryptographic hash functions are essential to contemporary Proof of Work systems. Proof of Work systems are comparable to Merkle trees in this sense. With relatively little computational work, every other machine can confirm that a hash is a valid result after it has been formed.

Some key advantages of Proof of Work systems are elaborated as follows:

*   They work wonders in discouraging spammers. If sending one email requires a considerable amount of work, most spammers wouldn’t have access to enough computing power to send a significant volume of unsolicited emails.
*   Alternatively, even if a spammer were to possess the computational power to send a large volume of emails, the expenses associated with this processing power — such as purchasing hardware and paying for electricity — would probably outweigh the money gained from spamming. A Proof of Work system works in a way to deter spam and other harmful activity.
*   It’s noteworthy to mention that Proof of Work systems can also be utilized to secure a whole network. A single bad actor or even a small group of bad actors cannot get the computational power necessary to dominate and influence a network if enough nodes (computers or specialized mining equipment) are fighting to find a particular solution.

# [Technical Specifics of Blockchain Proof of Work](#toc.xhtml#s151a)

Proof of Work blockchains involve nodes on the network discovering a block hash that is less than or equal to a predefined number, called the “target.” This is the “work” or mathematical problem the nodes are attempting to solve. The block header data combined with a cryptographic hash algorithm is the block hash. Six pieces of information make up the block header itself, including:

*   The blockchain codebase version number that is in effect when a block is mined
*   The desired number in compressed form, or bits
*   The timestamp corresponding to the precise moment the block is mined
*   The Merkle Root, which is the hashed set of all transaction IDs in the block using a Merkle Tree
*   A cryptographic hash of the block hash from the previous block
*   The nonce

Thus, the header of a block is composed of these six pieces of data. The hash that results from hashing these six pieces of information collectively is referred to as the “block hash.” A block must have a block hash that is less than or equal to the goal in order for a miner to begin mining it.

It’s crucial to understand that the quantity targeted and what’s usually called the challenge are not the same. The difficulty and the target are really inversely correlated. As the target is reduced, the difficulty of finding a block increases.

This is true because miners are trying to find a block hash that is less than or equal to the target. If the target is extremely high, the chances that a miner can find a block hash less than the target are comparatively high. If the targeted amount is low, then there is a much more limited set of numbers that are less than or equal to that target. This makes it harder to find a block hash beneath the threshold.

Every Proof of Work blockchain has a target that is frequently changed to guarantee a block time that is comparatively constant. For example, the goal for Bitcoin is changed every two weeks. The goal of Bitcoin is to maintain block time at ten minutes. If blocks are discovered too quickly (less than ten minutes on average), the difficulty increases, making it harder for miners to find new blocks and slowing down block creation. Conversely, if block times consistently exceed ten minutes, the difficulty decreases, allowing miners to find blocks faster and restore the target block time.

Now remember that the cryptographic hash of the header of a block in a Proof of Work blockchain, which consists of six pieces of data, is the block hash of any block in the blockchain. You’ll see that five of the six data points are static, which means they can’t be changed and must stay the same.

The nonce is the only integer that is modifiable. Because it is a single-use number, the nonce is known as such. Every block is unique in its nonce.

As previously said, the goal of mining cryptocurrencies is to locate a block hash that is either less than or equal to the target. Miners attempt to mine blocks by repeatedly and incrementally changing the nonce because it is the sole number that is modifiable. Finding a nonce that, when combined with the other five pieces of information that comprise the block header, results in a block hash that is less than or equal to the target is the aim.

A miner broadcasts a nonce and its associated block hash to all other nodes on the network once it discovers one that meets these requirements. Every node confirms that the hash generated by the nonce is accurate and either less than or equal to the target. That block is added to the chain if the network reaches consensus. After the miner obtains their block reward, the competition recommences with a fresh block.

# [Disadvantages of Proof of Work Blockchains](#toc.xhtml#s152a)

There are two primary disadvantages to Proof of Work systems:

*   The first is that they waste energy, which is bad for the environment. As computers perform extra computational work, additional electricity is used. This can add up to an extremely large amount of excess electricity consumption. This has led to the rise of Proof of Stake chains as a greener alternative. 
    
    However, the simple statistic of how much energy the Bitcoin network consumes doesn’t tell the whole story. Environmentally speaking, Bitcoin mining isn’t as bad as you might think. A report on crypto mining released in November 2018 estimates that around 80% of the electricity used in mining is green energy. In some cases, Bitcoin mining operations are making use of excess electricity that a country’s power grid would be unable to store and distribute anyway.
    
*   The second major drawback to Proof of Work blockchains is about security. Proof of Work blockchains provide adequate security only if there is a large network of miners competing for block rewards. If the network is small, the possibility remains that a hacker could gain a simple majority of the network’s computational power and stage what is known as a 51% attack.

In today’s circumstances, manipulating the Bitcoin blockchain is practically impossible. However, there are dozens of smaller Proof of Work blockchains that remain extremely vulnerable to 51% attacks.

In fact, many of these small Proof of Work chains can be successfully attacked for just several hundred dollars an hour on NiceHash, a website that allows users to rent hash power to enable mining of cryptocurrencies. For more details, check out Crypto51, a website that lists in real time how much money it would cost to 51% attack every Proof of Work blockchain in existence.

While these two disadvantages of Proof of Work blockchains are serious concerns, Komodo’s Blockchain Security Service provides a solution to both.

As far as environmental issues are concerned, Komodo’s innovative delayed Proof of Work security mechanism recycles the incredible hash rate of the Bitcoin network to provide BTC-level security to all integrated blockchains. It leverages the gargantuan amount of electricity already being consumed to protect small Proof of Work blockchains.

As for the concern about 51% attacks and other types of blockchain manipulation, they are no longer an issue once a chain is secured with the power of the BTC network. A potential hacker would need to overpower both the KMD network and the BTC network at the same time in order to successfully attack a chain integrated to Komodo’s Blockchain security service.

# [HashCash Protocol for POW](#toc.xhtml#s153a)

In 1997, a member of the cypherpunk movement named Adam Back founded a protocol called HashCash, which laid the foundation of “Proof of Work” mechanisms. HashCash even included “*Double Spending Protection*,” a foundational concept in blockchain for keeping networks secure from double spend attacks.

Moreover, Satoshi Nakamoto cited HashCash as an influence in Bitcoin, writing that in order “*to implement a distributed timestamp server on a peer-to-peer basis, we will need to use a proof-of-work system similar to Adam Back’s Hashcash*.” The connection is not just assumed but made explicit by the founder of Bitcoin themself.

HashCash was a solution designed to combat spam by generating a proof of work that allowed verifying that a certain email was not spam. It introduced Proof of Work (PoW) that was used to minimize spam and denial of service attacks (known as DoS or DDoS). This technology gained wide popularity, thanks to its implementation in the Bitcoin and many others cryptocurrencies.

# [Bitcoin and PoW](#toc.xhtml#s154a)

Bitcoin makes use of the Hashcash function as part of its mining core. All bitcoin miners use GPU rather than CPU. The GPUs are graphics processing units that have a higher computing power than those of conventional central processing units (CPUs). Like many cryptographic algorithms, HashCash uses a hash function as a building block. Solving these puzzles creates new blocks and validates transactions on the Bitcoin network. Without this software, the current Bitcoin mining and security structure would not be possible.

# [The Proof of Stake (PoS)](#toc.xhtml#s155a)

Another popular consensus method that developed as a low-cost, low-energy substitute for the proof of work (PoW) algorithm is the proof of stake (PoS). It includes assigning a participant node a certain amount of tokens representing virtual currency in proportion to how many tokens it holds, which is the public ledger. One potential disadvantage of PoS is that it encourages storing cryptocurrency rather than spending it, which could affect overall circulation.

In this method, the age of a forger’s stake within the blockchain network determines which forgers—the proof-of-work equivalent of a miner—are selected to produce blocks. Let’s take an example where you own 500,000 QTUM. Let me begin by speculatively congratulating you on your fat stacks. Returning to the example, you would have a higher chance of creating the candidate block under the Proof of Stake mechanism than someone with 100,000 QTUM. Furthermore, you would be more likely to generate the following block if you had been holding your 500,000 QTUM in the same address for a year as opposed to someone who had the same amount of QTUM but had been storing it in a network address for half that time.

![](images/Figure-4.3.jpg)

**Figure 4.3:** Age of a forger’s stake within the blockchain network

Another way to think about it would be if your chances of winning the lottery were dependent on two things:

*   How much money you invested, and
*   How long you had been purchasing tickets.

Staking your preferred PoS currency won’t make you millions of dollars, but you can still increase the value of your investments and generate some nice passive income.

It is significant to remember that a stake can only be selected if it is kept on an address connected to the coin’s network.

Thus, you would have to keep your QTUM in the QTUM core wallet if you were holding it like in the preceding example. The PoS mechanism does not have any block rewards either. Forgers only receive transaction fees because there is no motivation to outcompete other miners based on their effort.

Additionally, there is a significant distinction between ordinary Proof of Stake and Delegated Proof of Stake, which will be discussed later. The list of coins utilizing the various technologies is as follows:

*   **PoW Coins**: Bitcoin, Ethereum, Litecoin, Monero
*   **PoW/PoS Hybrids**: Dash, Stratis, HShare, Pivx
*   **PoS Coins:** Cardano, OmiseGo, QTUM, Ardor

While PoW and PoS are by far the most prevalent in the blockchain space, there are other consensus algorithms like Proof of Capacity (PoC) which allow sharing of memory space of the contributing nodes on the blockchain network. The more memory or hard disk space a node has, the more rights it is granted for maintaining the public ledger.

Proof of Activity (PoA), used on the Decred blockchain, is a hybrid that makes use of aspects of both PoW and PoS.

# [Attacks on PoW and the Monopoly](#toc.xhtml#s156a)

A 51% attack, also known as a majority attack, occurs when a single person or group gains control of over 50% of a blockchain’s hashing power. That is usually achieved by renting mining hash power from a third party. The attackers consume enough power to control most events in the network. The term 51 is just symbolic, it means more than 50% control. They can monopolize generating new blocks and receive rewards since they are able to prevent other miners from completing blocks and can reverse transactions.

# [Proof of Burn (PoB)](#toc.xhtml#s157a)

Proof-of-burn (PoB) is another blockchain consensus process that uses less energy than proof-of-work (PoW). By burning currencies, decentralized platforms using the Proof-of-Battle technique make sure miners come to a consensus. The procedure of burning cryptocurrency is to remove it from use forever. Blockchains backed by Proof of Bases (PoBs) use this technique to validate transactions even though it lowers inflation[^(\[4\])](#c04.xhtml#ftn4).

In contrast to Proof-of-Work decentralized networks like Bitcoin, Proof-of-Burn verifies transactions using virtual mining machines rather than real ones. In simpler terms, PoB miners start coin burns to demonstrate their network participation and gain mining permission.

The fact that a miner can burn bitcoin demonstrates the strength of virtual mining. As a result, power increases with coin quantity and vice versa. Keep in mind that more mining power accelerates the process of discovering new blocks, exactly like in PoW systems. The miner receives greater payouts as a result.

Using proof-of-burn (PoB) networks, the coins are sent to an “*eater address”* as part of the coin burn procedure.

This address is unreachable but can be verified by the public. It is noteworthy that eater addresses are not secret keys; instead, they are randomized.

Proof-of-stake (PoS) and proof-of-burn (PoB) consensus procedures are comparable in that they both rely on currency interactions to safeguard the network. In contrast to PoB, coins held in PoS systems can still be accessed and sold by their owners should they wish to quit the network. Conversely, PoB causes a coin shortage as opposed to PoS.

Because the PoB method is a novel kind of consensus algorithm, its applicability to big networks has not yet been established. The PoB technique has a number of benefits, including a fully decentralized mining process and sustainability.

# [Proof of Elapsed Time (PoET) Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s158a)

Another network consensus technique that avoids excessive resource and energy consumption is the Proof of Elapsed Time (PoET) blockchain, which maintains process efficiency by employing a random lottery system.

Permissioned blockchain networks can choose who writes the next block thanks to Intel Corporation’s PoET consensus algorithm[^(\[5\])](#c04.xhtml#ftn5).

On a blockchain network, the PoET algorithm determines mining rights and block winners using a randomly generated elapsed time. Additionally, the PoET method increases transparency by guaranteeing that lottery outcomes may be independently verified by other parties by executing a trusted code in a safe environment.

One of PoET’s interesting features is that it uses a lottery approach to distribute winnings evenly among network users, providing each node an equal chance of winning. Every node in the blockchain network is assigned a random wait time by the PoET algorithm, and it is necessary for every node to sleep for that period.

# [Understanding Proof of Elapsed Time (PoET)](#toc.xhtml#s159a)

Blockchain networks employ consensus mechanisms to confirm transactions and add new blocks. On permissioned blockchain networks, the consensus method known as PoET is frequently employed to determine who has the right to mine or who wins a block. Blockchain networks that need identity verification before allowing anyone to join are known as permissioned networks.

Following two essential components were supposed to be guaranteed by the PoET network consensus mechanism:

*   In the first place, it guarantees that the nodes taking part actually choose a random time, rather than picking a shorter period of time on purpose to win.
*   It also certifies that the winner has served out their waiting period.

Evidence of a failing consensus process time consumes a lot less energy than proof of work since it chooses a node at random rather than pitting all of the miners on a network against one another.

The massive chip manufacturer Intel Corporation (INTC) came up with the PoET concept at the beginning of 2016. It provides a ready-made, cutting-edge solution to the computational issue of “random leader election.” Applications can run trusted code in a secure environment due to the embedded mechanism. This ensures that the two requirements are met: all participating nodes’ waiting times must be randomly selected, and the winning participant must actually finish the waiting period. The open-source Hyperledger Sawtooth project, which employs the PoET consensus mechanism, was supported by Intel in collaboration with other groups like the Linux Project and IBM. A distributed ledger system that is both scalable and flexible for a wide range of business applications is called Hyperledger Sawtooth.

# [Operation of PoET](#toc.xhtml#s160a)

By distributing the odds of winning across the greatest number of network users, the PoET mechanism follows the idea of a fair lottery system in which each node has an equal chance of being selected. Every node in the network that takes part in the PoET scheme has to wait for a predetermined amount of time; the first node to finish the waiting period wins the next block. In the blockchain network, every node creates a random wait time and goes to sleep for that amount of time.

It is simple to understand that the person who wakes up first, or who has the lowest wait time, broadcasts the required information to the whole peer network when they wake up and commit a new block to the blockchain. The next block is then discovered by repeating the same procedure.

# [Distinguishing PoET from PoW](#toc.xhtml#s161a)

The proof of work (PoW) algorithm’s workflow is essentially the same as the consensus method used by Bitcoin, although it doesn’t require as much power. It improves the efficiency of the network by enabling a node to sleep and transition to other duties for the predetermined amount of time.

As a result, unlike Proof-of-Work, which encourages decentralization and transparency, Proof-of-Elapsed Time necessitates the issuance of certificates to all network participants.

Numerous other network requirements are met in this case by the trusted code execution mechanism operating inside a secure environment. It guarantees that the trusted code operates in a secure setting and cannot be changed by any party involved. Additionally, it makes sure that the outcomes can be independently verified by users or other authorized parties, which increases the network consensus’s transparency.

PoET also maintains the consensus process’s vitality, or nimbleness, in order to keep the cost under control and proportionate to the value generated by the process—a crucial need for the cryptocurrency economy to keep growing. Hyperledger Sawtooth is an enterprise-focused distributed ledger, akin to a blockchain, intended for supply chain and logistics applications. It might even be used to bookkeeping, inventory control, and other time-consuming and error-prone company operations.

PoET is mostly utilized in Sawtooth Hyperledger. There isn’t a cryptocurrency that uses Proof of Elapsed Time (PoET) as of January 2022, hence there isn’t a Proof of Elapsed Time Coin yet.

Proof-of-Work and Proof-of-Elapsed Time differ significantly. PoW is a competitive work method, while PoET relies on random selection.

# [Proof of History (PoH)](#toc.xhtml#s162a)

Similar to Proof-of- Elapsed Time (PoET), another consensus technique known as Proof of History (PoH) was created by the Solana Project. It achieves consensus without requiring a lot of resources by cryptographically encoding the passage of time. In order to reach the required consensus across distributed processes or multi-agent systems, like cryptocurrency systems, on a single data value or a single state of the network, any consensus method used in computer and blockchain systems must be fault-tolerant[^(\[6\])](#c04.xhtml#ftn6). Among other aspects, record-keeping benefits from its utilization.

# [The Hardness of Mining and Mining Difficulty](#toc.xhtml#s163a)

A cryptocurrency like Bitcoin grows in popularity in proportion to the number of computers involved in its peer-to-peer network. Miners fight for the few benefits offered by each block. The so-called “hashpower” of the entire network rises as the number of users and computer power do[^(\[7\])](#c04.xhtml#ftn7).

This process of competition is referred to as the **mining difficulty** or in simple words **difficulty** (felt in competing), which is easier to understand once you grasp the basics of Bitcoin mining. It refers to the effort required to solve a complex cryptographic puzzle. Since Bitcoin transactions are stored in blocks, they are added to the blockchain every 10 minutes (= 600 seconds). To maintain the time, it takes to process one block at around 10 minutes, difficulty has to be adjusted periodically.

Mining difficulty is a measurement unit used in the process of Bitcoin mining.

The mining difficulty of a cryptocurrency such as Bitcoin indicates how difficult and time-consuming it is to find the right hash for each block.

The difficulty of mining new units increases or decreases over time, depending on the number of miners in the network.

It is noteworthy that the mining difficulty in the Bitcoin network is adjusted automatically after 2,016 blocks have been mined in the network. An adjustment of difficulty upwards or downwards depends on the number of participants in the mining network and their combined hashpower.

# [Evolution of Mining Equipment](#toc.xhtml#s164a)

Mining equipment has evolved considerably since the beginnings of Bitcoin. In the early days, the first miners used the CPUs of their PCs to mine Bitcoin. Miners eventually realized that graphics cards are better suited for mining Bitcoin. However, graphics cards also need more energy.

In recent years, special “ASICs” (application-specific integrated circuit chips) have been developed specifically for Bitcoin mining.

Presently, Bitcoin and other digital currencies are mined via **mining pools**, where lots of miners join forces and combine their hash rates in the quest for block rewards. Solving the mathematical puzzles for valid block creation requires huge amounts of computational power. Because the difficulty is rising continually, miners join forces in Bitcoin mining pools and solve the mathematical puzzles together. The first individual miner or the mining pool that finds the right hash gets the block reward.

Usually, block rewards consist of new coins or tokens native to a blockchain network such as Bitcoin. In a mining pool, block rewards are split among participants in proportion to their share of computing power in the mining pool. This way each participant is adequately invested in the process.

# [Mining Pool](#toc.xhtml#s165a)

A mining pool is the pooling of resources by miners, who share their processing power over a network, to split the reward equally, according to the amount of work they contributed to the probability of finding a block[^(\[8\])](#c04.xhtml#ftn8).

A “share” is awarded to members of the mining pool who present a valid partial proof-of-work. Mining in pools began when the difficulty for mining increased to the point where it could take centuries for slower miners to generate a block[^(\[9\])](#c04.xhtml#ftn9). The solution to this problem was for miners to pool their resources so they could generate blocks more quickly and therefore receive a portion of the block reward on a consistent basis, rather than randomly once every few years[^(\[9\])](#c04.xhtml#ftn9).

Usually, the blocks in the cryptocurrency network contain transactions. Transaction fees are paid to the miner (mining pool). Different mining pools could share these fees between their miners or not.

Every cryptocurrency pool will require you to pay mining fees, hence it is best to opt for pools that have a high payout and low fees.

Mining pools are groups of cooperating miners who agree to share block rewards in proportion to their contributed mining hash power.

While mining pools are desirable to the average miner as they smooth out rewards and make them more predictable, they unfortunately concentrate power to the mining pool’s owner. It’s important to note that most mining pools are in China. Many only have Chinese websites and support. One of Bitcoin’s biggest concerns currently is mining centralization in China. With roughly 20 major mining pools, estimates suggest Chinese pools control a whopping 65% of the network’s hash rate. Other major players include Malta (10%), USA (3%), Czech Republic (3%), and Japan (0.5%).

![](images/Figure-4.4.jpg)

**Figure 4.4:** Share of World’s Mining Pools \[Courtesy reference 9\]

Miners can, however, choose to redirect their hashing power to a different mining pool anytime. Here is a look at some popular options:

*   **Slush Pool**: It is the oldest mining pool currently in use, operational since 2010. This current mining pool is accessible to the public and is known for its accuracy and stability. It offers a simple registration process, and to learn how the dashboard functions, you can even build a demo miner. There are two options on the website: one for novice miners and another for seasoned ones.
*   **AntPool**: The largest mining pool globally is AntPool. About 11% of all blocks are mined by it, and its servers are located all over the world. During setup, AntPool’s novel peer-to-peer mining mechanism can connect your device to the closest one for a dependable connection.
*   **BTC.com**: If you are acquainted with Bitcoin.com, the same business also owns BTC.com. Before launching the mining section, BTC.com was well-known for its cutting-edge blockchain explorer and Bitcoin wallet. Litecoin, Ether, and other cryptocurrencies are supported by its medium-sized mining pool, which is located in multiple countries including the US and Germany.
*   **KanoPool:** This pool, which has been operational since 2014, is smaller than the others but offers inexpensive mining costs and a simple startup procedure. The only prerequisites are a username and a Bitcoin wallet address; registration is completely voluntary. There is a comprehensive help page with all the necessary FAQ answers, and the interface is straightforward and easy to use.
*   **Binance Pool**: One of the biggest cryptocurrency exchanges in the world, Binance, powers Binance Pool. Binance Pool is an up-and-coming, well-liked platform that presently mining roughly 9% of all blocks, following the success of its exchange platform and native currency, Binance coin.

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml#s166a)

This chapter explored various mechanisms that underpin the operation of secure and decentralized blockchains. We started by examining the concept of consensus in a Bitcoin network, highlighting the crucial role it plays in maintaining network integrity. We then delved into the inner workings of public blockchains, followed by a deep dive into the Proof-of-Work (PoW) system utilized by Bitcoin and its reliance on the HashCash protocol. The chapter further explored alternative consensus mechanisms like Proof-of-Stake (PoS), Proof-of-Burn (PoB), and Proof-of-Elapsed Time (PoET), each offering advantages and disadvantages compared to PoW. Finally, we discussed potential vulnerabilities and centralization concerns associated with Proof-of-Work. By understanding these core concepts, you gain valuable insights into the foundation of various blockchain technologies.

The next chapter is solely devoted to the deployment of Blockchain technology for different types of enterprises all over the world.

# [CHAPTER 5](#toc.xhtml#c05)

# [Understanding Blockchain for Enterprises](#toc.xhtml#c05)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml#s168a)

This chapter is solely devoted to deployment of Blockchain technology for different types of enterprises all over the world. We will discuss about permissioned blockchain model, its use cases and permissioned blockchain platforms. Readers will also learn about the challenges of permissioned solutions and state machine replication in detail.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml#s169a)

In this chapter, we will cover the following topics:

*   Permissioned Blockchain Model and Their Use Cases
*   List of Best Permissioned Blockchain Platforms
*   Challenges of Permissioned Solutions
*   State Machine Replication

# [Permissioned Blockchain Model and Use Cases](#toc.xhtml#s170a)

A blockchain is a distributed ledger that is based on cryptographic protocols that allow data transmission and storage on a peer-to-peer basis. Network consensus protocol maintains this distributed ledger and provides notable security features. Blockchain is designed to eliminate the need for a third-party intermediary for the transfer of value, assets, or data between two parties.

Although in it is nascent development stages, blockchain offered significant promise. But considering the diverse needs of businesses and individual users, blockchain is forced to devise several models that have emerged in due course.

Over the past several years, blockchains have rapidly evolved in several ways, offering a diverse range depending upon their build and configuration. The contents stored on the blocks of the blockchain as well as the activities performed by the various participants on the blockchain networks depends on two key factors:

*    How the blockchain is configured and
*    How it is expected to fulfill the desired business purpose.

# [Types of Blockchains](#toc.xhtml#s171a)

Generally, two types of blockchain models are used as follows:

*   Permissionless Blockchains which can be further divided into two types:  
    *   Public blockchains
    *   Private blockchains
*   Permissioned blockchains are also known as private blockchains or permissioned sandboxes have also gained attention and momentum.

In the next section, we will discuss in detail about these two categories of blockchains.

# [Permissionless Blockchains](#toc.xhtml#s172a)

A permissionless blockchain is devised to be a type of blockchain network (as the name suggests) that allows anyone to become a part of the network and to contribute to its upkeep. In other words, a permissionless blockchain is a decentralized ledger that is open to the public. The vast majority of cryptocurrencies, including Bitcoin, are using this type powered by permissionless blockchain networks. The key characteristics of permissionless blockchains are enumerated as follows:

*   **Transparent**: The permissionless blockchains transactions are transparent, allowing users to access all information except private keys. Users have access to view all transactions in the network. Because blockchain networks must incentivize users to trust the network, the transparency is necessary.
*   **Anonymous**: Most permissionless blockchains do not ask users to provide any personal information when creating an address. Anonymous means that the whereabouts of the concerned person is unknown to the public. Let us understand this in another way sighting an example, as in the case of an alcoholics group takes its name from the fact that it’s a confidential group - people use only their first names and do not identify one another as members of the group.
*   **Decentralized**: Permissionless networks tend to be fully decentralized. This means that:  
    *   No single entity can change the network protocols,
    *   Shut down the network, or
    *   Edit the ledger in a permissionless blockchain.

***Note:*** *This blockchain model is based on a ‘majority’ consensus protocol, having a requirement that a change to the blockchain must have a “consensus” among more than 50% of the users.*

Permissioned blockchains have enabled new applications that depend on *privacy and security*, including:

*   Supply chain provenance tracking;
*   Claims settlement; and
*   Identity verification.

In case of supply chain, **provenance** is the validated history of ownership, custody and origin of a specific product instance such as a lot, a batch, or a serial number. It also applies to a pallet that may contain more than one product as well.

To illustrate, let’s consider a scenario and the key considerations that ensue from it.

In case of any large healthcare organization, with many subsidiaries and ecosystem partners need to evaluate their system using Distributed Ledger Technology (DLT). Decentralizing health records contains its several value propositions for the organization including its employees, patients, partners and)medical research. By distribution of this information the organization achieves the following:

*   Lowers the risk of data tampering, fraud, human error and singular attack-point vulnerability; Improves records integrity, access, privacy and auditability; and
*   Streamlines information portability across service providers, compliance adherence, insurance settlement, latency, cost reductions, even medical research efficiencies.

However, the organization ought to be aware of following risk factors:

*   Diverse risks involved in still-immature blockchain technology,
*   Unclear regulatory requirements,
*   Cultural and political objections involving sensitive information, business models and stakeholder adoption,
*   Risk of failure.

In spite of having a broad grasp of potential applications as detailed here, most of the organizations may not be able to choose correct architecture.

# [Difference Between Permissioned Blockchain and Permissionless Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s173a)

*[Table 5.1](#c05.xhtml#tab5_1)* summarizes the difference between permissoned blockchain systems and permissionless blockchain systems:

 
| **Permissioned Blockchain versus Permissionless Blockchain** |
| **Category** || **Permissioned** || **Permissionless** |
| **Speed** || Faster || Slower |
| **Privacy** || Private membership || Transparent and open – anyone can become a member |
| **Legitimacy** || Legal || Illegal |
| **Ownership** || Managed by a group of pre-defined nodes || Public ownership - no one owns the network |
| **Decentralization** || Partially decentralized ||  Truly decentralized |
| **Cost** || Cost-effective || Not so cost-effective |
| **Security** || Less secure || More secure |

**Table 5.1:** Permissoned blockchain systems as compared to permissionless[^(\[1\])](#c05.xhtml#ftn1)

# [Permissioned Blockchains and Tokens](#toc.xhtml#s174a)

The permissioned blockchains are also known as private blockchains or permissioned sandboxes and are closed networks in which previously designated parties, which may be sometimes members of a consortium, interact and participate in consensus and data validation. They are required to be partially decentralized in the sense of being distributed across known participants rather than unknown participants, as in permissionless blockchains. Generally, here also tokens and digital assets are possible but less common as compared to permissionless.

Characteristic features of permissioned blockchains and tokens are:

*   Permissioned blockchains allow for a mixed implication bag between the public and private blockchains and support many customization options. These include allowing anyone to join the permissioned network after suitable verification of their identity, and allocation of select and designated permissions to perform only certain activities on the network. For example, Ripple’s XRP, one of the largest cryptocurrencies, supports permission-based roles for participants. This is detailed in the section titled *Permissioned blockchains and Tokens.*
*   In such scheme of blockchains are built in a manner so that they grant special permissions to each participant. At the same time this allows participants the ability to perform specific functions such as read, access, and write information on the blockchains. People in different businesses are increasingly opting for permissioned blockchain networks, because it allows them to:  
    *   Selectively place restrictions while configuring the networks, and
    *   Control the activities of the various participants in the desired roles.

For example, if a blockchain network is used for managing dealings, the process involves multiple entities, that is the dealing with the farm produce from its origin (the farm) to the end customer (the market). Say a farmer cultivates a medicinal plant that he/she ships to multiple markets across the world. In this case, multiple parties are involved:

*   The customs authorities which clear the produce to enter their respective nation,
*   The shipping companies who move the produce, and
*   The warehouse operators that are supposed to maintain the product within a specified temperature range, all serve a vital but specific function in the farmer’s transaction. In this case, permissioned networks may offer the best fit.
*   The farmers may finalize a particular price and quantity for selling her/his or their produce to a buyer in America and another price and quantity to another buyer in India. The other involved entities such as the warehouse operator may not necessarily need information about the agreed prices between the farmer and his various buyers. They may simply need access to limited information—like quantity and quality specifications—to perform their function in supporting such deals.

***Note****: Most permissionless blockchains allow for the use of utility known as monetary tokens, depending on the purpose that they serve. These tokens or digital assets are intended to incentivize users to become a part of the network. The value of the tokens can increase or decrease in value depending on the relevancy and state of the blockchain in the market.*

# [Advantages and Disadvantages of Permissioned Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s175a)

Being closed to outsiders gives permissioned blockchains clear advantages, but there are downsides as well.

**Advantages can be enumerated as:**

*   Decentralization can be incremental, which allows multiple businesses to participate without all the risks of highly centralized models
*   Strong privacy, because permission is needed to access transaction information
*   Customizability for specific uses, because it allows diverse configurations, modular components and hybrid integrations
*   Performance and scalability because fewer nodes manage transaction verification and consensus

**Disadvantages can be enumerated as:**

*   Increased risk of corruption and collusion because there are fewer participants than in a permissionless blockchain
*   Consensus is more easily overridden because the owners and operators can change the rules of consensus, immutability and mining
*   Less transparency to outside oversight because the number of participants is limited and the network’s operators determine privacy requirements

# [Characteristics Associated with Permissioned (or Private Blockchain Networks)](#toc.xhtml#s176a)

The characteristics features associated with permissioned or private blockchain networks are set explained as follows:

*   **Well-Defined Governance Structures**: Unlike permissionless blockchain networks, permissioned blockchains are not governed on consensus-based protocols. Needless to say, that decisions are made on a central, pre-defined level by members of the network.
*   **Possessing Varying Degree of Decentralization**: Permissioned blockchains can have varying degrees of decentralization. Permissioned blockchains can be partially decentralized or fully centralized. Usually, members of the blockchain decide the level of decentralization that the network should have as well as the consensus algorithms it should deploy.
*   **Being Non-Transparent**: In contrast to their permissionless counterparts, permissioned blockchains do not have to provide transparency. They can be transparent if they choose to, but most are not intended to be.

# [The Ripple and XRP](#toc.xhtml#s177a)

A technology to process international money transfers is known as Ripple, a payment protocol that uses blockchain. It has extremely fast processing times, and offers low transaction fees. It has partnered with hundreds of financial institutions that use this technology.

There’s a common misconception that Ripple is a cryptocurrency. Although Ripple itself is not a cryptocurrency, but it does have a native cryptocurrency called XRP (CRYPTO:XRP).

The purpose of RippleNet is to provide fast, cheap, and convenient cross-border transactions for banks[^(\[2\])](#c05.xhtml#ftn2). That makes it an alternative to SWIFT, the current international payments system that most banks use.

There are several advantages Ripple offers for international transactions, including:

*   The average transaction processes in five seconds.
*   The transaction fee is 0.00001 XRP, which was much less than a fraction of a cent even at the token’s all-time high in price.
*   The XRP cryptocurrency can be used as a bridge currency. This gives financial institutions a cheaper way to trade currencies. They can hold XRP instead of various types of fiat money

The main pros and cons of Ripple and its XRP cryptocurrency are given in *[Table 5.2](#c05.xhtml#tab5_2)*:

 
| XRP transactions are fast and cheap. || Ripple’s consensus protocol is arguably less secure than other methods of processing crypto transactions. |
| --- | --- |
| Ripple’s payment network is already being used by many financial institutions. || Most of Ripple’s banking partners only use RippleNet and not its XRP cryptocurrency. |
| XRP can be used by small business owners and consumers for secure money transfers. || Ripple has attracted controversy because it’s run by a private company and because of the SEC lawsuit. |
| XRP can be used as a bridge currency for international currency transfers. || XRP is difficult to purchase in the U.S. |

**Table 5.2:** The main pros and cons of Ripple and its XRP cryptocurrency

# [Layers of Blockchains](#toc.xhtml#s178a)

Blockchains can be abstracted into three different layers[^(\[3\])](#c05.xhtml#ftn3), as illustrated in *[Figure 5.1](#c05.xhtml#fig5_1).* At the core of blockchain is layer 1.

# [Layer 1 of Blockchain: BFT Consensus](#toc.xhtml#s179a)

BFT consensus — also known as *state machine replication* — which is a generic approach to tolerate failures. BFT consensus has different forms, ranging from conventional BFT protocols to PoW-based consensus. Despite fundamental differences in how consensus is achieved, any form must solve the same problem: how to enable nodes to reach consensus on the total order (that is, consistency) of transactions submitted by clients in the form of requests. After nodes reach a consensus about the order, the data/operations of the transactions are then processed according to the order of the transactions. As a result, distributed nodes functionally behave as if there were one centralized node. This ensures that there is only one sequence of client transactions, known as “*the longest chain.”*

# [Layer 2 of Blockchain: Smart Contracts](#toc.xhtml#s180a)

Layer 2 of blockchain is the smart contract, which is essentially software code. A smart contract provides an interface for blockchain developers to implement new functions. Smart contracts can then facilitate, verify, or enforce the execution of business transactions. A smart contract can be viewed as a program that connects the underlying consensus protocols with layer 3, applications and use cases.

# [Layer 3 of Blockchain: Applications and Use Cases](#toc.xhtml#s181a)

**Figure 5.1:** Three layers of three different types of blockchain
