These nodes make transactions with each other and send information on the transactions (for example, rating about other mobile nodes at a specific location and time) to the nearest blockchain node. Here, each grid refers to a specific location.

# [List of Best Permissioned Blockchain Platforms](#toc.xhtml#s182a)

The Blockchain platforms allow the development of blockchain-based applications. The permissioned networks are crucial to the different industries out there. Industries such as the finance industry, supply chain, and more can benefit immensely through permissioned networks.

The key permissioned blockchains include:

*   Ethereum
*   Quorum
*   Parity
*   MultiChain
*   Hyperledger Fabric 1.0
*   BigchainDB
*   InterPlanetary File System
*   Corda

*Quorum, Corda,* and *Hyperledger Fabric* are the top-notch solutions when it comes to permissioned solutions. Let’s look briefly into each of the permissioned blockchains that falls in the “best permissioned blockchain” category.

# [Ethereum](#toc.xhtml#s183a)

Ethereum is one of the oldest and most established blockchain platforms that was introduced as early as in 2013. One interesting fact about Ethereum is that it provides a truly decentralized blockchain almost comparable to the that of Bitcoin blockchain network. Ethereum possess a key strength which enables it to be truly decentralized having support for smart contracts. Low processing times and higher transaction processing costs as compared to other platforms are the key weakness points. It has its own cryptocurrency called ether, along with its role of being a blockchain platform that underpins enterprise applications[^(\[4\])](#c05.xhtml#ftn4).

The Ethereum platform is getting very popular and getting widespread adoption by technologists developing decentralized applications (dApps) based on the Ethereum network. Ethereum has a mature ecosystem of tools for writing smart contracts using the solidity programming environment. Solidity programming environment runs on the Ethereum Virtual Machine. However, some more alternative blockchain networks can process transactions much faster at a potentially lower cost than Ethereum. It is correct to say that many observers expect (April 2022) this ought to change after Ethereum adopts a more efficient security mechanism.

Nevertheless, it also has an active developer community orchestrated by the Enterprise Ethereum Alliance. This Alliance which has more than 250 members, including Intel, JPMorgan and Microsoft.

The Ethereum community is in the process of migrating from the existing proof of work (PoW) consensus mechanism to proof of stake (PoS), which is more energy-friendly. This migration process requires an elaborate process to flourish up a separate, new type of blockchain called a Beacon chain. Beacon chain seems getting merged into the existing, main Ethereum blockchain. By adopting this change the Ethereum Foundation estimated to reduce energy use by 99.95%.

# [IBM Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s184a)

IBM had developed Blockchain tool, a private, decentralized blockchain network that has been the most successful with enterprise clients who are less risk-averse. IBM thus sees the biggest opportunities in using it to link into enterprise cloud and legacy technologies more seamlessly than is possible in other decentralized networks.

This is a Blockchain developer tool introduced by IBM and was designed to be flexible, functional and customizable. Also, by this venture, IBM has invested in creating a user-friendly interface to simplify critical tasks, such as setting up, testing and rapidly deploying smart contracts. It is noteworthy that Alex-Paul Manders is a prominent industry thought leader on blockchain with hands-on technology experience and is sought out as a subject-matter expert presenter.

# [Hyperledger Fabric](#toc.xhtml#s185a)

Hyperledger Fabric championed by the Linux Foundation, is a set of tools that help in creating blockchain applications (refer to *[Chapter 6: Distributed Consensus in Closed Environment](#c06.xhtml)* for more details). Hyperledger Fabric is designed with rich ecosystem of components that can be plugged to your system in modular architecture fashion. Working well in closed blockchain deployments, it can improve security and speed and also supports an open smart contract model that can support various data models, such as account and unspent transaction output, or UTXO, models.

Hyperledger Fabric improves data privacy by two methods, one by isolating transactions in channels and another by enabling the sharing of private data on a need-to-know basis in private data collections. According to its proponents, it also enables high-speed transactions at the same time, with low latency of finality and confirmation.

# [Hyperledger Sawtooth](#toc.xhtml#s186a)

Launched by the Linux Foundation and contributed by IBM and Digital Asset, this technology is an enterprise-grade and modular platform, designed for creating, deploying, and executing distributed ledgers which enable digital records to be maintained without a central authority.

Hosted by Hyperledger and the Linux Foundation, this application is another open source blockchain initiative. Hyperledger Sawtooth uses a novel consensus mechanism called Proof of Elapsed Time (PoET) that can integrate with hardware-based security technologies to enable “trusted execution environments” of program code to run in secure enclaves, which are protected areas of computer memory. We covered PoET in the previous chapter.

Its mechanism enables Hyperledger Sawtooth to integrate with hardware security solutions, known as “*trusted execution environments*”. Intel’s newly launched Xeon processor is one of its applications.

Sawtooth is Hyperledger’s second open-source blockchain platform to integrate with an enterprise-ready 1.0 version. The launch of 1.0 software solutions is essential for every software developer. This version indicates that the maintainers are still dedicated to the core features and it gives blockchain companies a sense of confidence that whatever they build, won’t be disrupted by future upgrades.

# [R3 Corda](#toc.xhtml#s187a)

It is debatable that R3 Corda is technically a blockchain or an alternative type of distributed ledger. The official Corda site describes it as “*both a blockchain and not a blockchain.*” Corda uses a novel innovative consensus mechanism in which transactions are cryptographically linked but does not periodically batch multiple transactions into a block. However, one of the key benefits of this approach is that all transactions are processed in real time, which can improve performance compared to other types of blockchains.

R3 consortium has a strong following in the financial industry as Corda provides an attractive approach for financial transactions and smart contracts with strong security. Leading proponents of R3 Corda include Bank of America, HSBC, Intel and Microsoft. It supports tools that automate business logic that can execute across company boundaries.

# [Tezos](#toc.xhtml#s188a)

Although a very old application, Tezos is still in development stage since 2014. Tezos is a decentralized blockchain platform that is self-governing and establishes a true digital commonwealth. Just like Ethereum, Neo, Waves and QTUM, Tezos is a platform for smart contracts and dApp applications. However, its self-amending cryptographic mechanism makes it unique from other platforms. It is a platform linked to a digital token, called a Tezzie or Tez. But the platform is not based on the mining of Tez. Rather, token holders get a reward for participating in the proof-of-stake consensus mechanism. Its noteworthy that Tezos supports decentralized applications, smart contracts and novel financial instruments, such as NFTs. NFT can be thought of as a modern variation on trading cards that are tied to digital assets. This platform supports a dynamically upgradable protocol and modular software clients that facilitates its adaption to new uses.

Since then, Tezos has improved performance and increased the size limit on smart contracts. It has also developed tools to help automate the process of weaving NFTs into enterprise supply chains.

# [EOSIO Blockchain Platform](#toc.xhtml#s189a)

This blockchain platform was first launched as an open-source project in 2018. It is optimized for developing decentralized applications and smart contracts. According to its proponents it uses a complex consensus mechanism based on PoS that provides better performance than older mechanisms, such as Ethereum. Main difference is that it also includes support for a governance feature for voting on changes to the platform.

Let us understand first that EOS (Enterprise Operating System) — the first (of many) deployed network running EOSIO software. EOS is a global digital cryptocurrency that enables the exchange of value across the EOS Network’s ecosystem of dApps and smart contracts. It facilitates many functions, including transacting on decentralized finance (DeFi) protocols, buying and selling of verifiably owned digital art through NFTs. EOSIO is said to be a highly performant open-source blockchain platform, built to support and operate safe, compliant, and predictable digital infrastructures. EOSIO is an open-source platform that provides industry-leading transaction speeds and a sub-second block time latency rate, allowing developers to perform transactions at a lower cost through efficient resource management.

Needless to say, EOSIO and EOS both are part of the same ecosystem, but have different functionalities. EOSIO enables cryptographically secured transaction-layer infrastructure through programmable smart contracts.

The redesigned structure permits EOS to dispense with the requirement for transaction expenses and offers the capacity to more transactions per second (TPS). The most elevated recorded TPS of EOS is 3,996 contrasted with 15 TPS of Ethereum. The concept of Decentralized Finance (DeFi) has appeared as people are looking for ways to explore their possibilities in finance. Decentralization has entered into a new trustless and transparent financial services domain.

EOS may replace Ethereum as the dominant smart contract platform. Although, EOS has improved many of Ethereum’s issues such as transaction fees and scalability but remains controversial due to its more centralized model.

In 2021, Google Cloud joined the EOS community, a leading open-source platform for blockchain innovation and performance, and is taking steps to support the EOS public blockchain by becoming a block producer (BP).

Its noteworthy that the blockchain technology behind the EOS.IO protocol has the potential to drastically change the future of computer resources and applications, and improve upon several businesses and industries. Due to the powerful impact EOS could have, it’s long-term value could increase exponentially.

Not to forget, EOS was initially built on Ethereum as an ERC20 token, but has since split off onto its own mainnet. Now, like Ethereum, other dApps can live on the EOS protocol.

Ethereum (ETH) and EOS are two altcoins (acronym for alternative coins) that provide benefits beyond what Bitcoin provides and among the most popular cryptocurrencies today. Decentralized applications (dApps) can run on these blockchain “*supercomputers”* and power the next generation of decentralized internet. EOS in essence replicates all of the elements of a supercomputer, emulating computer processing hardware, storage, and more.

Key strengths of EOS include fast transactions and advanced account permission features for deploying applications. Over 400 applications have been developed on the platform, including identity management, supply chain management and gaming. The community also provides various tools for customizing blockchain implementations for various decentralized use cases in supply chain management, healthcare and DeFi.

# [Stellar](#toc.xhtml#s190a)

Stellar is a newer blockchain platform optimized for various kinds of DeFi applications evolved around two years back. Its protocol is Stellar Consensus, which purportedly can speed the time required to process and finalize transactions on a public blockchain network. The menace of shutting out bad or questionable actors in a financial transaction is also handled by Stellar that also includes security mechanisms for dealing with. It is being used by several companies for international trade and exchanging money across borders. The following are the examples of applications built on the Stellar blockchain and adopted as enterprise applications:

*   MoneyGram for money transfer,
*   Circle for payments and treasury infrastructure, and
*   Flutterwave for integrating payment processing

# [ConsenSys Quorum](#toc.xhtml#s191a)

Developed by financial services company JPMorgan, the Quorum is a customized version of Ethereum. Taking and utilizing the advantage of the core work on the Ethereum blockchain platform and repackages it into a hardened environment suitable for banks. It has been then optimized to support high-speed transactions between institutions such as insurance companies and banks on a private network. Various privacy enhancements to Ethereum are also added to improve support for regulations such as CCPA in California and GDPR in Europe.

The California Consumer Privacy Act (CCPA) is a piece of data privacy legislation that applies to most businesses that process the personal data of California residents.

The General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) is the toughest privacy and security law in the world. Though it was drafted and passed by the European Union (EU), it imposes obligations onto organizations anywhere, so long as they target or collect data related to people in the EU. The regulation was put into effect on May 25, 2018.

In contrast to the above, Central Consumer Protection Authority has the following powers:

If the commission finds violations of rights of consumers or in notice of trade practices which is unfair, it can inquire or cause an inquiry, either on receipt of complaint or suo moto or as directed by the Central Government.

In late 2021, ConsenSys acquired the intellectual property rights to the Quorum platform from JPMorgan. They then integrated these assets into their existing offerings, creating the ConsenSys Quorum open-source protocol layer. This layer aims to accelerate enterprise development of applications compatible with other Ethereum-based tools. ConsenSys offers development services for the combined platform, catering to enterprise customers like JPMorgan and the South African Reserve Bank.

# [Hedera Hashgraph](#toc.xhtml#s192a)

Another Blockchain Platform, Hedera Hashgraph provides a new form of distributed consensus, and is lightning secure, fast and fair platform that does not need to compute a heavy proof of work algorithm. Following changes have been made:

*   Hedera Platform empowers and enables developers to build an entirely new class of decentralized applications that are scalable.
*   Smart contracts in the Hedera Hashgraph platform can be made dynamic just like any other software component. Since it offers an optional mechanism which allows “*binding arbitration*”, the smart contracts deployed with a list of the public key of arbitrators can be edited to fix bugs or add new features.
*   If a designated arbitrator agrees on the amendment of a smart contract, the transaction having a new bytecode is approved by the arbitrator’s keys and the change is implemented.
*   The Hedera Hashgraph Council, the platform’s governing body, ensures no single member or small group can exert undue control through its governance structure.

# [Emergence of OpenChain](#toc.xhtml#s193a)

Coinprism, a company behind the colored coins standard Open Assets, developed OpenChain. This system is an open-source distributed ledger technology, highly suited for organizations willing to manage the digital assets in a secure and scalable manner. Unlike the consensus mechanism used in Bitcoin, it uses Partitioned Consensus where one instance will only have a single authority for validation of transactions.

There are three obvious advantages of using OpenChain:

*   **Eliminates Mining Competition:** OpenChain removes the need for miners, eliminating competition associated with proof-of-work systems.
*   **Free Transactions:** Transactions on OpenChain incur no fees, making it a cost-effective option.
*   **Efficient Validation:** Asset administrators can validate transactions on OpenChain, streamlining the process compared to other platforms.

# [Challenges of Permissioned Solutions](#toc.xhtml#s194a)

Implementing permissioned blockchain solutions can be a tough task. Permissioned blockchains are not free from operational challenges. These challenges encompass data storage, messaging and integration, identity, smart contracts management, and more.

For new startups considering a permissioned network, several key decisions need to be taken. Here are two crucial initial questions:

*   It needs to decide which blockchain platform to use.
*   It needs to determine the blockchain runtime to use.

Needless to say, the answers to these two questions will provide them the development stack to build the permissioned network.

Let’s have a detailed look at the challenges that one might come across while developing permissioned solutions:

*   **Integration:** Biggest challenges that a business or organization encounter are integration challenges, especially when using APIs for communication. To make it easy, company can use Rhombus, Chain Link, and Oraclize. These are great tools that will help you connect and integrate services more efficiently.
*   **Data Privacy:** In cases of regulated industries, privacy becomes a big concern. To ensure proper privacy, they are required to meet specific requirements. It is indicated by industry experts that Quorum and Aztec are a good pick when it comes to developing permissioned blockchains that offer great privacy.
*   **Data Access**: Accessing information in a permissioned blockchain can be rather a slow process. The right solution is to use the Graph which can expose data to the Application Programming Interface (APIs) and smart contracts for easy access.
*   **Data Storage:** It is possible to store data in many ways and formats on a blockchain. However, not all solutions are optimal, considering that a huge amount of data is stored on the blockchain. To ensure proper storage, it is advised to use data storage solutions such as IPFS Private Network, Big chain DB, and AWS Quantum Ledger.
*   **Identity:** The participant’s identity is also a problem. Being permissioned network, the identity of the peers is already known, this may cause issues during the consensus computations. To simplify the issue, it is advised to use Azure BaaS or uPort that will help the businesses to better leverage their identity protocols and solutions for decentralized applications.

# [State Machine Replication](#toc.xhtml#s195a)

A state machine is a mathematical abstraction used to design algorithms. A state machine reads a set of inputs and changes to a different state based on those inputs.

A state is a description of the status of a system waiting to execute a transition. A transition is a set of actions to execute when a condition is fulfilled or an event received.

One state is marked as the initial state; this is where the execution of the machine starts. A state transition defines for which input a state is changed from one to another. Depending on the state machine type, states and/or transitions produce outputs.

The initial state is not a separate state, but more like an indication of which state that the machine should start in by default. A transition from a different state can target a substate of a compound state, thereby bypassing the state’s initial state.

A state machine is a concept storing a state of the system at the moment[^(\[5\])](#c05.xhtml#ftn5). As per this concept, it receives a set of inputs (also referred to as commands). The state machine concept apply these inputs in a sequential order using a transition function to generate an output and an updated state. A brief description of the state machine is as follows:

`state = init`

`log = []`

`while true:`

`on receiving cmd from a client:`

`log.append(cmd)`

`state, output = apply(cmd, state)`

`send output to the client`

The `state` machine is initialized to an initial state `init`. On receipt of an input `cmd` from a client, it first adds the input to a log. It then execute the `cmd` by applying the transition function `apply` to the state. As a result, it obtains an updated state and a resultant `output`. The `output` is then sent back to the client as a response. For the subsequent discussion, a State Machine will be defined as the following tuple of values:

*   A set of States
*   A set of Inputs
*   A set of Outputs
*   A transition function (Input × State → State)
*   An output function (Input × State → Output)

# [The State Machine Approach](#toc.xhtml#s196a)

The state machine approach is a powerful technique for ensuring consistency in distributed systems. It relies on two key properties:

*   **Safety**: This property guarantees that if any two non-faulty replicas (copies) of the system store the same sequence of commands in their logs, they will always be in the same state. This ensures consistency across the system.
*   **Liveness:** This property ensures that honest server replicas will eventually execute any command proposed by a client. This guarantees that the system makes progress and doesn’t stall indefinitely.

# [Consensus on a Sequence of Values](#toc.xhtml#s197a)

Our preceding discussion provided the simple technique for implementing a fault-tolerant service in terms of a state machine. The user/programmer is required to:

*   Place copies of the state machine on multiple, independent servers.
*   Receive client requests, interpreted as inputs to the state machine.
*   Choose an ordering for the inputs.
*   Execute inputs in the chosen order on each server.
*   Respond to clients with the output from the state machine.
*   Monitor replicas for differences in state or output.

We have seen that the Bitcoin ledger is the most prominent example of state machine. The state consists of the set of public keys along with the associated Bitcoins. The input (or `cmd`) to the state machine is a transaction between parties corresponds to the Bitcoin ledger. And the transition function is the function that determines whether a transaction is valid, and if it performs the desired bitcoin script operation.

# [Fault Tolerant State Machine Replication (FT-SMR)](#toc.xhtml#s198a)

While using a client-server network setting, the servers simulate a state machine in a fault tolerant manner. In such situation, clients send commands as if there is a single state machine. But alternatively maintaining a single server is prone to crashes or Byzantine faults. A Byzantine fault is any fault presenting different symptoms to different observers. Instead of maintaining a single server, a state machine replication (SMR) system uses multiple server replicas, some of which can be faulty. The group of servers presents the same interface as that of a single server to the client.

In continuing process, the server responds and replicates all initially start with the same state. As such honest replicas first need to agree on the sequence of client commands received by them when they receive concurrent requests from a client. This problem called log replication is a multi-shot version of consensus. The replicas apply the commands in the log in case the sequence is agreed upon, one by one, using the apply transition. Suppose that the transition is deterministic, all honest server replicas maintain an identical state at all times.

Thus, a Fault Tolerant SMR system needs to perform log replication efficiently and then execute the commands received on the log. Specifically, it needs to guarantee the following:

It is indicated here that FT-SMR needs log replication, or multi-shot consensus. One can sequentially compose single-shot consensus protocols conceptually. As per available practice, SMR protocols may optimize without necessarily relying on a sequential composition.

**Who are the learners?**

According to dictionary, Byzantine is a system or situation excessively complicated, and typically involving a great deal of administrative detail. In the so-called Byzantine Broadcast (BB) and Byzantine Agreement (BA), the parties executing the protocol are the ones learning the result. In FT-SMR, application, the replicas engage in the consensus protocol but eventually need to convince clients of the result. In the presence of Byzantine faults, in an FT-SMR protocol, the clients may communicate with multiple replicas before learning about the committed value. If there are of Byzantine faults, the client needs to communicate with at least f+1 replicas, where f represents the maximum number of Byzantine faults the system can tolerate. This is done to ensure that it has communicated with at least one honest replica.to obligate or pledge oneself

Fault tolerance means an essential consequence of clients not participating in the protocol is the fault tolerance one can obtain. The two terms Byzantine agreement (BA) and Byzantine broadcast (BB) are fundamental problems in distributed computing, and one of the most important practical applications of BA and BB is to implement Byzantine fault tolerant (BFT) state machine replication (SMR). With BB, we know of protocols such as Dolev-Strong that can tolerate f<n−1 faults among n replicas. SMR protocols that obtain safety and liveness cannot tolerate more than a minority corruption.

Finally, the definition does not explicitly state a validity property as in BB and BA. Fault Tolerant SMR protocols generally satisfy external validity, that is, a command is said to be valid so far as the client signs the value.

# [Optimizing for a Sequence of Values](#toc.xhtml#s199a)

Since FT-SMR protocols need to agree on a sequence of values, practical approaches for SMR (such as PBFT, Paxos, and more) use a steady-state-and-view-change approach to architect log replication. There is a designated leader that drives consensus in the steady-state. Typically, the leader does not change until it fails (for example, due to network delays) or Byzantine behavior is detected. In case when the leader fails, the replicas vote to de-throne the leader and elect a new one. This process of choosing a new leader is called view-change. The presence of a single leader for more extended periods yields simplicity and efficiency when the leader is honest. But however, it also reduces the amount of decentralization and can cause delays if Byzantine replicas are elected as leaders.

This section explores the concept of representing algorithms using finite state machines. We’ll use a crowdfunding platform implemented as a smart contract as an example.

This chapter explored permissioned blockchains, a powerful technology for controlled and secure data sharing within a defined group of participants. We examined various use cases where permissioned blockchains offer significant advantages, such as supply chain management, financial transactions, and identity verification. We then delved into a curated list of leading permissioned blockchain platforms, highlighting their unique features. However, the chapter also acknowledged the challenges associated with implementing permissioned solutions, including decisions around platform selection and operational hurdles. # [CHAPTER 6](#toc.xhtml#c06)

# [Distributed Consensus in Closed Environment](#toc.xhtml#c06)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml#s203a)

In the previous chapter, we explored distributed consensus in open environments. This chapter delves into its counterpart: closed environment. Open blockchains allow large audiences or the public to consume all the ledger data. Closed blockchains attempt to restrict read access. Bitcoin is an open source that relies on the community to maintain and develop its underlying code. Any user can review the Bitcoin protocol and verify whether all processes are running correctly. The blockchain technology prevents a user from changing the code to benefit from it. Public blockchains allow unrestricted access; private blockchains are available to selected or authorized users; permissioned blockchains have different levels of user permissions or roles. Many cryptocurrencies are built on open-source, public blockchains. Ether is open-source software, among cryptocurrencies.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml#s204a)

In this chapter, we will cover the following topics:

*   Distributed Consensus
*   Consortium Blockchain
*   Consensus Models for Permissioned Blockchain
*   Types of Distributed Consensus Algorithm
*   The Paxos Algorithm
*   The Raft Consensus Algorithm
*   Byzantine Fault Tolerance (BFT)
*   Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance (PBFT, often written as pBFT)
*    Asynchronous Byzantine Fault Tolerance (ABFT)
*   Lamport-Shostak-Pease BFT Algorithm
*   Working Style of pBFT

# [Distributed Consensus](#toc.xhtml#s205a)

The consensus is a general agreement on a decision made by the majority of those involved. For example, the problem may be as simple as some friends trying to decide which restaurant has multiple options to choose from or complex as decisions on distributed systems.

In blockchain technology, a distributed consensus ensures a consensus of data among nodes in a distributed system or reaches an agreement on a proposal.

The consensus may be reached in two ways:

*   Open systems
*   Closed Blockchain

The Open systems refer to systems that interact with other systems or the outside environment, whereas closed systems refer to systems having relatively little interaction with other systems or the outside environment.

Also, an open blockchain is a public network that maintains an immutable record of transactions. Anyone can publish a transaction and participate in the network by adhering to a set of published rules. Examples include Bitcoin and Litecoin.

In effect an open blockchain is a ledger of digital events, called transactions, shared among different participants, each having a stake in the system. The ledger can only be updated by consensus of the participants, and, once recorded, information can never be altered. Each recorded event is cryptographically verifiable with proof of agreement from the participants.

Closed Blockchain is defined as a private network that maintains a shared record of transactions. Here, the network is accessible only to those who have permission and transactions can be edited by administrators. Examples include: Hyperledger, Ripple and Eris.

The balance between public and private blockchains is essentially established based on which participants on the network are able to write data onto the blockchain or ledger. The distinction between the open and closed blockchain models takes into consideration who’s able to read that data.

An open virtual currency can be substituted for real money using online exchange systems or ATMs that are designed for virtual to real currency exchanges. An example of an open virtual currency is Bitcoin, the most popular decentralized cryptocurrency online and peer-to-peer databases.

As there is a role for both the Internet and Intranets, there’s probably a role for both open and closed blockchains. We know the fact that blockchain is decentralized, the network as such cannot be shut down by one government. Consortium blockchain is a hybrid system in which all better qualities of both the open and closed systems have been recognized and utilized but it can be controlled.

# [Consortium Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s206a)

Consortium blockchain or Federated blockchain is a hybrid blockchain that combines private and public characteristics. However, it is different because several organizational members work together on a decentralized network. Basically, a consortium blockchain is a private blockchain that is only accessible to a group. This eliminates the risks associated with having a single entity controlling the network on a private blockchain.

The consensus processes in a consortium blockchain are managed by specific nodes. It has a node that is responsible for initiating, receiving, and validating transactions. Member nodes are able to accept or initiate transactions.

*   **Advantages**: A consortium blockchain tends to be more efficient, secure, and scalable than public blockchain networks. It also provides access controls such as private and hybrid blockchains.
*   **Disadvantages**: Compared to public blockchains, consortium blockchains are more transparent. Still, it can be compromised if a member node is breached; the blockchain’s own rules can make it difficult for the network to work properly.
*   **Examples:** This kind of blockchain is used in two areas: banking and payments. Different banks can band together and form a consortium, deciding which nodes will validate transactions. Similar models can be developed by research organizations or food tracking organizations. It is perfect for supply chains, especially for food and medicine applications.

In simpler terms, blockchain consensus algorithm is a procedure via which all the peers of a blockchain network reach a common acceptance or consensus about the real-time state of the distributed ledger.

Moreover, a consensus mechanism enables the blockchain network to attain reliability and build a level of trust between different nodes, while ensuring security in the environment.

There are some conditions that need to be followed in order to achieve distributed consensus every non-faulty process must eventually decide. The final decision of every non-faulty process must be identical, and every non-faulty process must begin and end with the same value.

# [Overview of Consensus Models for Permissioned Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s207a)

Not everyone is able to join a permissioned blockchain. To join the network, each person will require specific authorization from the owner or network administrator. For businesses, banks, and other establishments that would rather follow the law and have total control over their data, this is helpful. Each participating node in a permissioned blockchain is selected and known in advance. We still need consensus, though, as we cannot take it for granted that every node is reliable.

Permissioned blockchains cannot use the same type of consensus models that are seen in permission less blockchains. There are numerous variants of such consensus algorithms that are available today for permissioned blockchains.

By using a blockchain consensus mechanism, all participants in the network can reach a consensus on the current data state of the ledger. This fosters confidence in a distributed computing environment amongst strangers. Before any update is permanently added to the blockchain, it is essential that the distributed peers of the blockchain evaluate and approve it. This applies to everyone wishing to add information to the blockchain.

To preserve fairness and transparency on a blockchain, several consensus mechanisms are employed. For blockchains that are permissionless or permissioned, these models differ.

Every consensus model is constructed with a set of goals in mind. Among them are:

*   **Reaching a consensus**: Every model seeks to gather as many group agreements as it can.
*   **Cooperation:** Every blockchain participant strives to reach a consensus that takes the group’s interests into account as a whole.
*   **Equal Rights**: Each group member’s vote will be worth the same amount. This implies that each person’s vote counts.
*   **Engagement**: Every user on the blockchain is required to take part in the voting process.
*   **Activity**: Every blockchain user needs to continue being as active as possible. There won’t be any hierarchy of authority within the network.
*   **Cooperation**: Each member will set aside their personal interests in order to function as a team.

# [Types of Distributed Consensus Algorithm](#toc.xhtml#s208a)

Here are some common Distributed Consensus Algorithms:

*   The Paxos Algorithm
*   The Raft Consensus Algorithm
*   Byzantine Fault Tolerance (BFT)  
    *   Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance (PBFT, often written as pBFT)
    *   Asynchronous Byzantine Fault Tolerance (ABFT)
*   Lamport-Shostak-Pease BFT Algorithm

# [Paxos Algorithm](#toc.xhtml#s209a)

Although it is also “notorious” in terms of complexity, Lamport’s Paxos algorithm is one of the most well-known distributed consensus methods. Lamport put out this inventive approach, which is workable, engineering-implementable, and capable of providing the highest-level of consistency for distributed systems. Many distributed systems, such as Chubby and ZooKeeper, employ Paxos extensively. The applications for big data are the latter two.

*   A Proposer can handle a client request and actively suggest a proposal value in the event that a Basic Paxos has a single decree, meaning that it only agrees on a value once.
*   An Acceptor casts votes on proposals made by Proposers, quietly replies to the information they receive, and upholds the states’ principles.

If we are to simplify the model, then the Learner role can be ignored. This does not affect the decision-making in the model.

![](images/Figure-6.1.jpg)

**Figure 6.1:** Basic Paxos RPC communication and decision-making process

As shown in the *[Figure 6.1](#c06.xhtml#fig6_1)*, the consensus decision-making process uses the two-phase commit protocol:

*   • In the first stage, broadcast the Prepare RPC command to block incomplete previous proposals and determine the final value determined by the protocol.
*   • In order to force Acceptors to accept a predetermined value, broadcast the Accept RPC command during the first phase of the second phase.

Multiple instances of Basic Paxos combine to form Multi-Paxos, which can choose from a range of values.

The practical implementation of Paxos is likewise contingent upon numerous presumptions and limitations. Byzantine failures cannot be processed by Paxos; it can only handle Crash Fault Tolerance (CFT). It is not a Byzantine fault tolerance method as a result.

From the standpoint of FLP, Paxos gives up on liveness and implements fault tolerance and safety (safe but not live). That is to say, although it is quite rare, this algorithm might never finish or come to an agreement. From CAP’s point of view, Paxos reduces availability while just guaranteeing C (consistency) and P (partition tolerance). In order to enhance Paxos system availability, we might augment the Learner count.

The reason Paxos works so well in reality is that most of the situations that could compromise the availability and liveness of Paxos systems are difficult to trigger. Even if those circumstances materialize, the result is not very unsatisfactory.

![](images/Figure-6.2.jpg)

**Figure 6.2:** RAFT protocol Summary

# [Raft Consensus Algorithm](#toc.xhtml#s210a)

Owing to Paxos’ complexity, Ongaro introduced Raft, a less complicated algorithm, in 2014. Raft is far simpler to comprehend and used in engineering. This is Raft’s original goal as well. Many clear-cut design cues have been added, provided that functionality remains unaffected.

An asymmetric model based on leaders is the Raft algorithm. In a system, a node can only be in one of the following three states at any given time: candidate, follower, or leader. Every node is a follower in the early stages. A node, or follower, needs to run for office and start a round of electoral votes in order to take the lead. The node reverts to being a follower in the event that it does not collect enough votes. But the node becomes the leader if it gets the majority of the votes. The original leader immediately reverts to the follower state if it experiences setbacks and discovers that a new leader is chosen once it has recovered.

In order to quickly identify information that has expired, Raft additionally presents the Term notion. In Zookeeper, a term is comparable to an epoch file. A maximum of one leader may be elected in a given period, and the number of terms increases monotonically over time. The log containing the later phrase is more recent if the logs have a different last entry.

Raft additionally presents the timeout and heartbeat packet. An elected leader needs to deliver heartbeat packets to all other nodes in the cluster in order to stay in power. A follower considers the leader to have crashed and initiates a leader election if it does not receive the heartbeat packet within the allotted election timeout. In this case, the follower modifies its status to candidate.

The heartbeat and random timeout are used in Raft to conduct the leader election. Effective leadership is the key to implementing log replication. After receiving a client command, the leader adds it to their log and distributes the log to other followers. Raft limits the authority to commit logs to that of a leader, ensuring security.

Take note of how straightforward the leader election process and the leader’s regular activities are. In Raft, the procedure of changing leaders is actually a little more intricate.

Though Raft and Paxos don’t operate on precisely the same concept or mechanism, their approaches to solving issues and choosing trade-offs can be compared. In other words, Raft emphasizes fault tolerance but is limited to solving crash defects, emphasizing fault tolerance, safety, and consistency and weakens the level of liveness and availability.

# [Byzantine (Military) Generals Problem](#toc.xhtml#s211a)

The problem was discussed and explained aptly in a paper by Leslie Lamport, Robert Shostak, and Marshall Pease at Microsoft research in 1982.

Imagine that several divisions of the Byzantine army are camped outside an enemy city, each division commanded by its own general. The generals can communicate with one another only by messenger. After observing the enemy, they must decide upon a common plan of action. However, some of the generals may be traitors, trying to prevent the loyal generals from reaching an agreement. The generals must decide on when to attack the city, but they need a strong majority of their army to attack at the same time. The generals must have an algorithm to guarantee that:

*   All loyal generals decide upon the same plan of action, and
*   A small number of traitors cannot cause the loyal generals to adopt a bad plan.

The loyal generals will all do what the algorithm says they should, but the traitors may do anything they wish. The algorithm must guarantee the first condition regardless of what the traitors do. The loyal generals should not only reach agreement, but should agree upon a reasonable plan.

A Byzantine fault (also Byzantine Generals Problem, interactive consistency, source congruency, error avalanche, Byzantine agreement problem, and Byzantine failure) is a condition of a computer system, particularly distributed computing systems, where components may fail and there is imperfect information on whether a component has failed. The term takes its name from an allegory, the “Byzantine generals problem”[^(\[1\])](#c06.xhtml#ftn1), developed to describe a situation in which, in order to avoid catastrophic failure of the system, the system’s actors must agree on a concerted strategy, but some of these actors are unreliable.

Satoshi devised a means to use cryptographic security and public-key encryption to answer the Byzantine general problem in a digital electronic network. To prevent data tampering, cryptographic security uses hashing, a process of encoding. The identity of a network user is verified via public key encryption.

Byzantine Generals Problem describes the difficulty decentralized systems have in agreeing on a single truth. The Byzantine Generals Problem plagued money for millennia, until the invention of Bitcoin. Bitcoin uses a Proof-of-Work mechanism and a blockchain to solve the Byzantine Generals Problem.

***Note:*** *Byzantine Empire, the eastern half of the Roman Empire, which survived for a thousand years after the western half had crumbled into various feudal kingdoms and which finally fell to Ottoman Turkish onslaughts in 1453. But the Byzantine Romans differed from the Western Romans in two important ways: they spoke primarily Greek instead of Latin, and they were Eastern Orthodox Christians rather than Roman Catholic Christians. The Byzantines viewed the emperor not just as the head of the government but as the living representative of God and Jesus Christ. This meant that church and state were combined into one all-powerful body.*

In a Byzantine fault, a component such as a server can inconsistently appear both failed and functioning to failure-detection systems, presenting different symptoms to different observers. It is difficult for the other components to declare it failed and shut it out of the network, because they need to first reach a consensus regarding which component has failed in the first place.

# [Analogy](#toc.xhtml#s212a)

In its simplest form, a number of generals are attacking a fortress and must decide as a group only whether to attack or retreat. Some generals may prefer to attack, while others prefer to retreat. The important thing is that all generals agree on a common decision, for a halfhearted attack by a few generals would become a rout, and would be worse than either a coordinated attack or a coordinated retreat.

![](images/Figure-6.3.jpg)

**Figure 6.3:** Analogy Byzantine Generals Problem

The problem is complicated by the presence of treacherous generals who may not only cast a vote for a suboptimal strategy, they may do so selectively. For instance, if nine generals are voting, four of whom support attacking while four others are in favor of retreat, the ninth general may send a vote of retreat to those generals in favor of retreat, and a vote of attack to the rest. Those who received a retreat vote from the ninth general will retreat, while the rest will attack (which may not go well for the attackers). The problem is complicated further by the generals being physically separated and having to send their votes via messengers who may fail to deliver votes or may forge false votes.

**Resolution**

Byzantine fault tolerance can be achieved if the loyal (non-faulty) generals have a majority agreement on their strategy. There can be a default vote value given to missing messages. For example, missing messages can be given a “null” value. Further, if the agreement is that the null votes are in the majority, a pre-assigned default strategy can be used (for example, retreat).

The typical mapping of this story onto computer systems is that the computers are the generals and their digital communication system links are the messengers. Although the problem is formulated in the analogy as a decision-making and security problem, in electronics, it cannot be solved simply by cryptographic digital signatures, because failures such as incorrect voltages can propagate through the encryption process. Thus, a component may appear functioning to one component and faulty to another, which prevents forming a consensus as to whether the component is faulty or not.

# [Byzantine Fault Tolerance (BFT)](#toc.xhtml#s213a)

Byzantine Fault Tolerance (BFT) is the feature of a distributed network to reach consensus (agreement on the same value) even when some of the nodes in the network fail to respond or respond with incorrect information. The objective of a BFT mechanism is to safeguard against the system failures by employing collective decision making (both – correct and faulty nodes) which aims to reduce to influence of the faulty nodes. BFT is derived from Byzantine Generals Problem.

Byzantine fault tolerance can be achieved if the correctly working nodes in the network reach an agreement on their values. There can be a default vote value given to missing messages, that is, we can assume that the message from a particular node is ‘faulty’ if the message is not received within a certain time limit. Furthermore, we can also assign a default response if the majority of nodes respond with a correct value.

Leslie Lamport proved that if we have 3m+1 correctly working processors, a consensus (agreement on same state) can be reached if at most m processors are faulty. This means that strictly more than two-thirds of the total number of processors should be honest.

# [Characteristics/Objective of Byzantine Fault Tolerance](#toc.xhtml#s214a)

A Byzantine fault is any fault presenting different symptoms to different observers. A Byzantine failure is the loss of a system service due to a Byzantine fault in systems that require consensus.

The objective of Byzantine fault tolerance is to be able to defend against failures of system components with or without symptoms that prevent other components of the system from reaching an agreement among themselves, where such an agreement is needed for the correct operation of the system.

The remaining operationally correct components of a Byzantine fault tolerant system will be able to continue providing the system’s service as originally intended, assuming there are a sufficient number of accurately operating components to maintain the service.

Byzantine failures are considered the most general and most difficult class of failures among the failure modes. The so-called fail-stop failure mode occupies the simplest end of the spectrum. Whereas fail-stop failure mode simply means that the only way to fail is a node crash, detected by other nodes, Byzantine failures imply no restrictions, which means that the failed node can generate arbitrary data, including data that makes it appear like a functioning node. Thus, Byzantine failures can confuse failure detection systems, which makes fault tolerance difficult. Despite the analogy, a Byzantine failure is not necessarily a security problem involving hostile human interference: it can arise purely from electrical or software faults.

# [Types of Byzantine Failures](#toc.xhtml#s215a)

There are two categories of failures that are considered:

*   One is fail-stop (in which the node fails and stops operating) and
*   Other is arbitrary-node failure.

Byzantine Fault Tolerance (BFT) is of two types:

*   Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance (PBFT, often written as pBFT)
*   Asynchronous Byzantine Fault Tolerance (ABFT)

# [Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance](#toc.xhtml#s216a)

Since Lamport first introduced the Byzantine Generals Problem in 1982, there have been several talks regarding BFT solutions; however, many of the solutions to these difficulties are laborious, slow, and inefficient. When Castro and Liskov introduced the Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance (PBFT) method in 1999, things started to get better. The PBFT algorithm is the first of its kind to have its complexity lowered from an exponential to a polynomial level. Several thousands of transactions per second (TPS) and workable solutions to malicious nodes in practice are made possible by PBFT. It has been demonstrated that if there are no more than 1/3 of malicious nodes in a system, the PBFT algorithm will function as intended.

In a PBFT system, each node is arranged in a sequential order, with one node serving as the leader and the rest as backup nodes. In a system, every node talks to every other node and uses the majority principle to come to decisions. We refer to each PBFT consensus round as a perspective. Every view involves a change in the leading node, which can be substituted by a protocol known as a view change if a predetermined period of time elapses without the leading node broadcasting the request. This replica timeout technique makes sure that a new view begins by re-electing a new leader and that the crashed or malicious leader may be identified.

![](images/Figure-6.4.jpg)

**Figure 6.4:** pBFT normal operations

As shown in *[Figure 6.4](#c06.xhtml#fig6_4)*, five phases are experienced from the client launching requests to receiving responses. The consensus process adopts the three-phase protocol. The following content briefly describes the five phases:

1.  **Launch:** The client (`client c`) initiates the service request m to the cluster.
2.  **Pre-prepare**: The leader node (`replica 0`) verifies the validity of the request message m, assigns the sequence number n to the request m in the view and broadcasts the assigned pre-prepare message to all the backup nodes (`replica 1-3`).
3.  **Prepare:** The backup nodes verify the validity of the request message m and accept the sequence number n. If a backup node accepts the assignment scheme, it broadcasts the corresponding prepare message to the other nodes. In this phase, all the replicas are required to reach a globally consistent order.
4.  **Commit:** Once the assignment agreement message is received from the cluster, all the nodes (primary and secondary nodes) broadcast the commit message to all the other nodes. In this phase, all the replicas have agreed on the order and confirmed the received request.
5.  **Execute and Reply:** After receiving the commit message from the cluster, the nodes execute the request m and send replies to the client. The client awaits the same reply from `f+1` different nodes and considers that the request has been executed successfully. *f* represents the maximum number of potential faulty nodes in the clusters. All the nodes directly return messages to the client is also to prevent the primary node from having problems during the request.

PBFT implements safety based on an asynchronous network model, but it depends on message timeouts to perform periodic synchronization. Because the leader-plan solution is adopted, the message synchronization process is very fast and the sequential writing is also implemented. However, the leader re-election is difficult. A malicious leader may start to send a message when the time is very close to the timeout window, causing the system to be seriously slow. This disadvantage can be used to launch attacks against the network and make correctly running nodes look incorrect, causing endless leader election.

Compared with *Paxos and Raft, PBFT* can process more problems. In addition to crash faults, it can process Byzantine problems that may cause troubles and unauthorized changes. However, from the perspective of the trade-off policy adopted in PBFT, PBFT is still similar to Paxos and Raft. From the perspective of FLP, PBFT also emphasizes the fault tolerance and safety and weakens the level of liveness. From the perspective of CAP, PBFT emphasizes the tolerance to network partition faults and consistency and weakens the level of availability.

Despite these shortcomings, PBFT is still effective and feasible in practice if the number of faulty or malicious nodes is no more than 1/3 of the total nodes. PBFT is not the only BFT algorithm. Other BFT-like algorithms are also evolving, for example, Lamport once suggested BFT Paxos (an enhanced version of Paxos) to process Byzantine faults. Recently, the BFT Raft algorithm was presented based on the combination of PBFT and Raft. However, from the perspective of the problem scope and mechanism, these algorithms are still similar to previous ideas and frameworks.

We know that a permissioned blockchain is a secure distributed ledger maintained by a number of trusted validation nodes. However, if during operation a validator may become compromised then it may send inconsistent messages to different nodes. This problem can be countered by consensus protocols like Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance can be used.

PBFT provide consensus mechanisms in permissioned blockchains. A BGP protocol allows the network to detect the behavior of malicious nodes, and all working nodes can reach a consensus if the number of compromised nodes is less than one-third of the total number of nodes, or `n >= 3f +1`*,* where n is the number of nodes and *f* is the number of compromised nodes.

Border Gateway Protocol (BGP) is a standardized exterior gateway protocol designed to exchange routing and reachability information among autonomous systems. Partition Attack on Bitcoin Nodes[^(\[2\])](#c06.xhtml#ftn2).

The partition attack includes isolation of a victim Bitcoin node from the Bitcoin network. Traffic from/to the attacked Bitcoin nodes is deliberately blackholed by an attacker in a certain point of the Internet. The attacker effectively splits the Bitcoin network into two isolated components that have no connectivity between each other. As a result, the block is solved in both components (the new block is solved approximately every 10 minutes) and it is broadcasted toward other Bitcoin nodes within components. Afterwards, the attacker stops the attack and connectivity between components is regained. However, all blocks mined within the smaller component (with less mining power) will be discarded, along with all transactions and the miner’s revenue. Therefore, the consequences of partition attacks are Denial of Service (DoS) for the Bitcoin clients in a smaller component and the loss of revenue. Moreover, transactions in a smaller component can be reverted (double-spending).

In order to blackhole traffic of the targeted Bitcoin nodes, the attacker exploits the weakness of the BGP protocol. As a result, the attacker effectively hijacks IP prefixes where Bitcoin nodes are located. As we know, BGP does not validate whether an origin AS is eligible to announce a prefix. Therefore, an attacker who gains access to an ISP router can announce any prefix via BGP. Let’s say that the attacker announces a prefix with the same length (partial BGP hijacking) as the owner of the prefix but AS\_PATH attribute is shorter (fewer AS hops) than the AS\_PATH in the original BGP announcement. As a result, the newer prefix, announced by the attacker AS, is installed into routing tables.

However, it is more effective for the attacker to announce a more specific prefix. In that case a complete BGP hijacking situation occurs, as BGP routers install more specific routes into their routing tables. As Maria Apostolaki in her paper claims, the vast majority of Bitcoin nodes are hosted in prefixes less than /24. Therefore, an attacker is announcing prefixes with length /24. Prefixes longer than /24 would be very likely filtered by upstream providers. Perfix length /24 means that the first 24 bits are the network address.

The *delay attack* is another type of network attack against the Bitcoin network using BGP hijacking. The attack effectively slows down propagation of blocks towards a victim. The goal is to delay a delivery of a new block to a victim, keeping the victim uninformed for almost 20 minutes about a new block. Contrary to the partition attack, the attacker does not blackhole a victim’s connection. Instead, BGP hijacking is used to conduct a Man-in-the-Middle attack (MITM) between a victim and its peer. The attacker also needs to modify Bitcoin messages sent from the victim to the peer in order to perform a successful delay attack.

# [A Simplified Three-Phase pBFT Process](#toc.xhtml#s217a)

PBFT distributed consensus algorithm has three phases, and each node maintains its own view of the network. The protocol is initiated by a leader in the network. Even if the leader failed, other nodes take over the responsibility and resume the operation.

A simplified three-phase process is as follows:

1.  A leader sends out pre-prepare message to all the nodes;
2.  Each node receiving the message will produce a response, and send the response, that is, prepare, to every node;
3.  After receiving sufficient such as 2/3 prepare messages from the nodes, each can commit to the agreement, and send out the commit message to everyone.

Thus, to reach a consensus, there will be three rounds of messaging, one for each pre-prepare, prepare, and commit message. The PBFT is more complicated as it also handles the case when a leader is crashed. In permissioned BCs, a Byzantine consensus will be needed only when a new block is to be created, and all working nodes need to agree with the contents of the new block.

However, for an enterprise system, due to asynchronous message arrival and large volume of data, each node may receive different messages since the previous block creation, and thus it can be difficult for all the nodes to agree with all the contents of the new block even if the nodes are working properly.

If the system has few transactions per second, this is not an issue, but this is an issue for an enterprise system. Thus, the goal of consensus protocol is modified as follows:

*   Most of working nodes can reach a consensus on most of items in a new block;
*   Detection of those failed nodes so that they can be safely ignored during consensus building;
*   Detection of those compromised nodes that sent out different messages to different nodes to disrupt the operation;
*   Those items that should be in the BC, but not voted in the current block, will be placed in the next block to be voted in the future;
*   Those items that should not be in the BC should be clearly identified and should not be placed in a queue to be considered in the next block.

**Issue 1** is a relaxed version of a common BC. Instead of having identical contents, one can allow each node to have slightly different contents in a new block yet to be voted on. This is to allow each node to receive different messages from different sources due to asynchronous messaging. However, as a part of the voting process, all working nodes must decide on the contents of the new block. In this way, after voting, all the nodes will have identical content in the newly created block.

**Issue 2** is important in a permissioned BC as failed nodes should not be involved in future voting until they are fully recovered. The recovered node also needs to retrieve any missing data during the absent period.

**Issue 3** is important as compromised nodes should not be involved as a compromised node can cause significant troubles for a BC. In fact, they should be excluded from the BC operation completely as soon as they are identified as the damage done by compromised nodes is far greater than the damage done by failed nodes.

**Issue 4** is a new problem in a permissioned BC as each second numerous data may enter the BC, and those valid data must be stored to maintain the trust of the BC. For example, a lottery machine may send out ticket information to a permissioned BC. Due to message delays, some messages did not get into every node, and they were not included in the new block. As they are valid tickets, they should be stored in the next block. This scenario is considered as false negative as a valid message was excluded due to various reasons.

**Issue 5** is a new problem due to Issue 4 as data not included in the previous block can be included in the next new block to be created, thus potentially an increasing number of data can be postponed and continued to be considered for inclusion. But some of these data may not be valid at all, and should be removed for good rather than to be re-considered in future blocks. This scenario is considered as false positive as invalid messages continued to be considered in block creation.

# [Applicable Scenarios](#toc.xhtml#s218a)

Distributed consensus algorithms have been developing, improving and evolving since the introduction of Paxos, Raft, and PBFT. Many big companies have also developed distributed consensus algorithms that meet their business scenarios. Although these algorithms are not very perfect, they play an important role in specific business practices.

Non-BFT algorithms such as Paxos and Raft can only process machine hardware faults and cannot handle situations where malicious nodes are present. These non-BFT algorithms can only run in very reliable network environments, for example, the internal network of a company. In such relatively closed networks, access requires strict authorization, ensuring that the identities of individual nodes are known and reliable. This serves to eliminate malicious nodes, and to allow the algorithms to run effectively.

BFT algorithms do have very strict requirements on the network environment. Even if malicious nodes are present, the entire system is still secure only if the malicious nodes are no more than one-third of the total nodes. However, this brings about new problems. How do you know exactly the number of malicious nodes in the network? What is the proportion of the malicious nodes to the total nodes? If access to the network requires permissions, it is relatively easy to solve this problem. For example, in a private network consisting of 10 affiliates, only the 10 authorized companies can access the network. Even if some companies (less than 3 companies) act maliciously and attempt to change data without authorization, the whole system is still reliable and secure. In such a permissioned network, the number of nodes that may act maliciously has been estimated. When some nodes do act maliciously, their real identities can be quickly located. This indirectly improves the safety of the network.

# [Limitations](#toc.xhtml#s219a)

However, BFT algorithms may cause problems in a permissionless (open permission, without permission control) open network. If a distributed network is open and can be accessed by anyone and the cost of the network access is low, it is unknown how many potentially malicious nodes may be in the network. Even when some nodes act maliciously, determining their identities is also a difficult issue. A typical attack scenario is a Sybil attack where attackers can forge identities to control a large number of nodes and then the entire distributed network.

In addition, the biggest limitation of BFT algorithms is that they can only coordinate a small number of nodes (for example, no more than 100 nodes). If the nodes are in the thousands, the system shows very poor performance or even fails to reach consensus, affecting the liveness and availability of the system. You may have noticed that multicast is required throughout the three-phase protocol of PBFT. In the pre-prepare phase, the primary node broadcasts the request to all the secondary nodes; in the prepare phase, the secondary nodes broadcast to all other nodes; in the commit phase, individual nodes (primary and secondary) broadcast to all other nodes. From this process, we can know that the number of communication times is the number of nodes squared. When a system has a large number of nodes, this broadcast mechanism will be a disaster. The system is almost unable to reach consensus in a short period of time.

From this explanation, we can draw a conclusion that traditional distributed consensus algorithms such as Paxos, Raft, and PBFT are generally applicable to reliable distributed networks that require permission control and have a small number of nodes.

# [Advantages of pBFT](#toc.xhtml#s220a)

The following are the advantages of using PBFT:

*   **Energy Efficiency**: PBFT can achieve distributed consensus without carrying out complex mathematical computations (like in PoW). **Zilliqa** employs pBFT in combination with PoW-like complex computations round for every 100th block.
*   **Transaction Finality**: The transactions do not require multiple confirmations (like in case of PoW mechanism in Bitcoin where every node individually verifies all the transactions before adding the new block to the blockchain; confirmations can take between 10-60 minutes depending upon how many entities confirm the new block) after they have been finalized and agreed upon.
*   **Low Reward Variance**: Every node in the network takes part in responding to the request by the client and hence every node can be incentivized leading to low variance in rewarding the nodes that help in decision making.

# [Working Style of pBFT](#toc.xhtml#s221a)

pBFT tries to provide a practical Byzantine state machine replication that can work even when malicious nodes are operating in the system.

Nodes in a pBFT enabled distributed system are sequentially ordered and used with one node being the primary (or the leader node) and others referred to as secondary (or the backup nodes). Note here that any eligible node in the system can become the primary by transitioning from secondary to primary (typically, in the case of a primary node failure). The goal is that all honest nodes help in reaching a consensus regarding the state of the system using the majority rule.

A practical Byzantine Fault Tolerant system can function on the condition that the maximum number of malicious nodes must not be greater than or equal to one-third of all the nodes in the system. As the number of nodes increase, the system becomes more secure.

pBFT consensus rounds are broken into four phases: (refer to *[Figure 6.5](#c06.xhtml#fig6_5)*):

1.  The client sends a request to the primary (leader) node.
2.  The primary (leader) node broadcasts the request to the all the secondary (backup) nodes.
3.  The nodes (primary and secondaries) perform the service requested and then send back a reply to the client.
4.  The request is served successfully when the client receives ‘m+1’ replies from different nodes in the network with the same result, where m is the maximum number of faulty nodes allowed.

![](images/Figure-6.5.jpg)

**Figure 6.5:** pBFT consensus rounds

The primary (leader) node is changed during every view (pBFT consensus rounds) and can be substituted by a view change protocol if a predefined quantity of time has passed without the leading node broadcasting a request to the backups (secondary). If needed, a majority of the honest nodes can vote on the legitimacy of the current leading node and replace it with the next leading node in line.

# [Limitations of pBFT](#toc.xhtml#s222a)

The pBFT consensus model works efficiently only when the number of nodes in the distributed network is small due to the high communication overhead that increases exponentially with every extra node in the network.

*   **Sybil Attacks**: The pBFT mechanisms are susceptible to Sybil attacks, where one entity (party) controls many identities. A Sybil attack is a type of attack on a computer network service in which an attacker subverts the service’s reputation system by creating a large number of pseudonymous identities and uses them to gain a disproportionately large influence. As the number of nodes in the network increase, sybil attacks become increasingly difficult to carry out. But as pBFT mechanisms have scalability issues too, the pBFT mechanism is used in combination with other mechanism(s).
*   **Scaling**: pBFT does not scale well because of its communication (with all the other nodes at every step) overhead. As the number of nodes in the network increase, so does the time taken to respond to the request.

# [Platforms Using pBFT Variants](#toc.xhtml#s223a)

*   **Zilliqa**: pBFT in combination with PoW consensus
*   **Hyperledger Fabric**: permissioned version of pBFT
*   **Tendermint**: pBFT + DPoS (Delegated Proof-of-Stake)

# [Asynchronous Byzantine Fault Tolerance (ABFT)](#toc.xhtml#s224a)

When a decentralized network is Byzantine fault tolerant, it means that the honest members, or nodes, of a network can be guaranteed to agree on the timing and order (consensus) of a set of transactions. Regardless as to whether there are some nodes maliciously trying to prevent that consensus — even if as many as one-third of nodes are trying to negatively affect consensus by delaying transactions or otherwise corrupting things. This is the ‘fault tolerance’ of the network, meaning how many nodes can the network tolerate acting maliciously, but still come to an honest consensus.

The ‘asynchronous’ property of Byzantine fault tolerance overcomes a challenge of fault tolerance, which is that of timing. Many forms of Byzantine fault tolerance assume there is a maximum threshold of message latency when coming to a consensus. An ABFT network eliminates this assumption and allows for some messages to be lost or indefinitely delayed.

An ABFT network allows for messages to be lost or indefinitely delayed and assumes only that at some point an honest node’s messages will eventually get through. It is much more challenging for an honest node to assess whether another node is not following the rules, if that node’s messages can be indeterminately delayed, but this scenario much better reflects that network reliability in the real world.

# [Lamport-Shostak-Pease BFT Algorithm](#toc.xhtml#s225a)

Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance (pBFT) is a consensus algorithm introduced in the late 90s by Barbara Liskov and Miguel Castro. pBFT was designed to work efficiently in asynchronous (no upper bound on when the response to the request will be received) systems. It is optimized for low overhead time. Its goal was to solve many problems associated with already available Byzantine Fault Tolerance solutions. Application areas include distributed computing and blockchain.

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml#s226a)

This chapter delved into the fundamental concept of distributed consensus, the backbone of achieving agreement in permissioned blockchains. We explored various consensus models suitable for controlled environments, including the popular Paxos and Raft algorithms. We then contrasted these with Byzantine Fault Tolerance (BFT) algorithms like PBFT and ABFT, designed to handle malicious actors.

By understanding the strengths and limitations of different consensus algorithms, developers can choose the most appropriate approach for their specific consortium blockchain application. While PBFT offers robust fault tolerance, its communication overhead might limit scalability in large networks. On the other hand, Paxos and Raft provide efficient consensus but require a more controlled and trusted environment.

The ongoing evolution of consensus algorithms promises even more efficient and secure solutions for permissioned blockchains. As these technologies mature, they will continue to play a vital role in driving innovation and trust in distributed ledger systems.

In the next chapter, we will focus on the enterprise application of blockchain.

# [Cross-Border Payments](#toc.xhtml#s230a)

It is very much clear that safe transfers between an endless number of bank ledgers can be made possible by blockchain technology in cross-border payments. By doing this, we can avoid using financial middlemen, who act as go-betweens when transferring money between banks[^(\[1\])](#c07.xhtml#ftn1).

Since blockchain technology employs encrypted distributed ledgers to provide reliable real-time transaction verification without the need for middlemen like correspondent banks, it has a bright future for cross-border payments. It makes verification possible without relying on other sources. Since anyone can utilize the technology to integrate their product to their advantage, it also preserves the idea of banking. A few banks in Korea and Japan have already begun to use this technology for their payment processes.

*   **AntPool**: The largest mining pool globally is AntPool. About 11% of all blocks are mined by it, and its servers are located all over the world. During setup, AntPool’s novel peer-to-peer mining mechanism can connect your device to the closest one for a dependable connection.
*   **BTC.com**: If you are acquainted with Bitcoin.com, the same business also owns BTC.com. Before launching the mining section, BTC.com was well-known for its cutting-edge blockchain explorer and Bitcoin wallet. Litecoin, Ether, and other cryptocurrencies are supported by BTC.com’s medium-sized mining pool, which is located in multiple countries including the US and Germany.

![](images/Figure-7.1.jpg)

**Figure 7.1:** Systematic diagram of Broadcasting of a transaction

*[Figure 7.1](#c07.xhtml#fig7_1)* is to show a transaction created gets converted into a block for further processing and the same gets broadcasted. Immediately the network nodes approve the block, the same gets added to the chain and finally gets entered on the ledger.

One utility known as *Oracle’s FLEXCUBE Blockchain adapter* enables users to interface Blockchain systems to transform data between traditional applications. Besides, this adapter can be used for any version and configured at the module level. The adapter helps to process information with minimal human intervention.

FLEXCUBE is a real-time, online, comprehensive universal banking solution which supports the changing landscape of retail, corporate, and investment banking needs with strong conventional banking and Islamic banking capabilities. FLEXCUBE is a microservices-based platform – developers can work with a single functionality without a significant impact on other modules. It provides fast integration with third-party applications to meet the needs of large banking ecosystems.

Oracle’s FLEXCUBE Blockchain technology can be used for the following features:

*   Automation of loan creation
*   Automating the liquidation of bills and loan contract
*   Reducing the cost of SWIFT transfer

To put the preceding scenario into clearer perspective, Blockchain technology can be utilized to make the disbursement of a buyer’s credit loan much more accessible. The importer’s bank generates a dummy import bill with all the information from the actual bill under a letter of credit, including shipment and goods details, when it accepts the offer for buyer’s credit made by the foreign bank. The information will be sent to the Blockchain network for verification, and the Blockchain network will notify the foreign bank of the contract’s specifics.

A Nostro Correspondent will receive an MT 202 shortly after the loan contract has been approved. The importer’s bank will get the loan disbursement details via the Blockchain.

MT202 message informs each of the banks in the chain of the payment amount, currency and recipient bank. It is mentioned here that *MT202 COV* is a SWIFT message format for financial institution (FI) funds transfer between financial institutions. Prior to MT202 COV the message format, MT202, were used primarily for two purposes, bank-to-bank payments (that is, interest payments and settlement of FX trades) and cover payments.

***Note:*** *SWIFT is an acronym for the Society for Worldwide Interbank Financial Telecommunication. It allows banks to relay information about financial transactions to one another. SWIFT is a cooperative company under Belgian law and is owned and controlled by its shareholders (financial institutions) representing approximately 3,500 firms from across the world except Russia. Just to mention here, Russia has already developed its own transaction system known as the System for Transfer of Financial Messages (SPFS), currently used only for domestic transactions.*

A SWIFT payment generally takes 1–4 working days. The transaction time taken varies based on the destination, time zones and different banking procedures. SWIFT transfers aren’t instant. Before your funds are credited to the recipient, they will undergo anti-fraud and anti-money laundering checks, which takes time.

When the import bill is due for liquidation, the user will do so, and the Blockchain will notify the lending bank that the loan has been repaid. Here, FLEXCUBE can interface with Blockchain systems, owing to the Blockchain adaptor, making it simple to convert data between Blockchain datasets and standard applications.
