A blockchain’s infrastructure is made up of nodes. They disseminate, store, and maintain the Blockchain data.

Through the adaptor, which serves as a messenger, the transaction recorded in the importer’s bank’s FLEXCUBE platform will be sent to the lending bank. Through the adapter’s APIs (Application Programming Interface), the transaction details are translated into nodes. The same will be sent by the API principle to the lending bank via an additional adaptor, assisting in the creation of a record in FLEXCUBE between the lending bank and itself. The adapter and node will facilitate communication between the two parties.

![](images/Figure-7.2.jpg)

**Figure 7.2:** Business Uses of Blockchain

The second most common use case for cryptocurrency and/or blockchain is cross-border payments, which 37% of businesses currently do and another 13% want to begin doing. Other common ways that multinational firms utilize cryptocurrency and blockchain include asset management and other payments.

# [Blockchain and Cross-Border Money Transfer](#toc.xhtml#s231a)

Given the current state of the art’s deficiencies with regard to cross-border payments, blockchain technology and the notion of a distributed ledger have found favor with the banking and financial industry. Banks also frequently discuss the distributed ledger blockchain, which has been the subject of much discussion for some years now. Blockchain is an open ledger that exists in a distributed network and is available to all users.

As a result, every node in the network will have a complete copy of the entire database or ledger, and any changes made to it will require proper verification by other nodes or parties in order to confirm the changes made. As such, for the ledger to be considered genuine, all nodes must concur on its current status. This would imply that since there are no middlemen or correspondent banks involved, direct transfers can happen instantaneously, without concern about manipulation, even for cross-border payments. The settlement agency will decide on a bilateral, visible, and irreversible transfer of value between the banks thanks to the fundamental idea of distributed ledger.

![](images/Figure-7.3.jpg)

**Figure 7.3:** Money transfer from bank A to bank D through blockchain eliminating the third party as highlighted

The following are some advantages that blockchain offers, as seen in *[Figure 7.3](#c07.xhtml#fig7_3)*:

*   It stipulates that all intermediaries, central agencies, and correspondents will be excluded from the payment proceedings. Transactions can happen between parties that have signed a bilateral agreement, guaranteeing mutual trust.
*   A lower total cost with little fees applied throughout the payment process. Furthermore, if the messages are routed through SWlFT, there is a processing fee. Due to these fees levied by the central agency and correspondent banks, processing payments becomes more expensive for tasks like receiving, compiling, and netting payment messages before retransmitting data and confirmations to the appropriate institutions. Since there is no need for central agencies or message movement, there is a possibility of a very short turnaround time for settlement.
*   The nodes of the network maintain a copy of the balances because they are maintained in the settlement accounts with the other banks, so there is no need to ensure intra-day liquidity.
*   Since the transaction details are encrypted and hashed, there is very little chance that the data will be modified.
*   The challenges associated with standardization are also reduced.
*   There is increased payment transparency with distributed ledger since sender and receiver are the nodes of the network or chain. A private or permissioned blockchain, in which users are unable to join the network anonymously and become nodes, may help to mitigate some of the privacy and data protection issues. In order to access transactions involving a private key among the network’s trusted parties, the parties will need to register or sign a bilateral agreement. Everyone is also aware of how hard it is to hack the block’s underlying hashed transactions.

About 11 banks have contracted on a private peer-to-peer distributed ledger supported by the R3 group in order to test the distributed ledger on a worldwide private network. Ethereum technology is housed on a virtual private network built on the public cloud platform Microsoft Azure.

But rather than working together, the R3 consortium is facing some issues in light of the recent announcement that some of the big companies are leaving the group. Blockchain is turning into a battlefield where rivalry is now the main focus.

This is evident from the fact that there are currently no established criteria or regulations for the game, yet the majority of the important aspects appear to be amenable to a just settlement. The regulatory reporting aspect presents the most obstacle. Numerous blockchain use cases advocate for the elimination of intermediaries, such as regulatory bodies. These proposals aim to foster collaboration to minimize disruption in this sector, given the known conservative stance taken by banks and other financial institutions. Every regulatory reporting and transaction monitoring activity seeks complete payment transparency and the specifics of the parties involved in order to perform customer due diligence, sanction screening checks, FATF, Dodd-Frank, AML/KYC, Basel III, and other requirements. An industry study has shown that the two main factors that are thought to contribute to internal resistance to blockchain adoption are regulatory and compliance issues, which must be appropriately addressed.

# [Drawbacks of Cross-Border Money Transfer](#toc.xhtml#s232a)

Transferring money from one account to another within the same financial institution or any other financial institution is known as remittance. All that SWlFT does in a cross-border payment is manage the flow of messages across the payment chain. On the other hand, the correspondent banks assist in passing the value to the final beneficiary by performing the real debits and credits between accounts in accordance with the message.

As an illustration, bank A is transferring money in euros to a German bank D account. The following is the workflow:

1.  Bank A in the US receives an MT103 (a SWIFT message format) in $US.
2.  Bank A uses Fedwire to transmit the payment request to Bank B, its correspondent bank, along with a debit/credit instruction for further transmission.
3.  Bank B makes the necessary modifications and uses the SWlFT network to communicate with Bank C, its correspondent bank in Brussels.
4.  Bank D in Germany receives the value from Bank C via the Single Euro Payments Area (SEPA).
5.  Bank D credits the supplier account in EUR.

![](images/Figure-7.4.jpg)

**Figure 7.4:** Payment ﬂow chart - Bank A sending euro amount to a euro account bank D in Germany

As shown in the payment ﬂow chart of *[Figure 7.4](#c07.xhtml#fig7_4)*, the banks charge fees for processing of each transaction, thus increasing the costs involved for all parties concerned. The SWIFT bank charges for transmitting the messages and thus adds to the cost. Since the ledgers are local to the banks, the SWIFT messages ensure the debit entry of one bank’s ledger is communicated to another bank so as to pass/post the corresponding credit entry in its ledger. Due to this increase in the number of payment messages in the chain, fees on SWIFT messages also increase.

The following drawbacks are observed in the current process of international payments transfer system with involvement of correspondent banks has:

*   Each transaction is subject to processing fees levied by the banks, which raises the overall cost to all participants. The cost is increased by the SWIFT bank, which charges for message transmission. Due to the local nature of the bank ledgers, SWIFT messages make sure that a debit entry from one bank’s ledger is shared with another bank so that the other bank can pass along or post the corresponding credit record in its ledger. Fees for SWIFT communications also rise as a result of this growth in the chain’s payment messages.

The existing international funds transfer system, which involves correspondent banks, has the following observed drawbacks:

*   SWIFT came into being to guarantee and conﬁrm message transmission as no two banks can agree on a transaction based on their own ledger, Central banks operated as settlement agents to guarantee payments.
*   Also SWIFT charges the bank for processing the payment orders irrespective of whether the bank is at both the receiving and sending end of the instruction.
*   In case a single cross-border payment has to traverse through certain correspondent banks which are involved in activities like receiving, collating, and netting payment messages before retransmitting conﬁrmations/denials to the respective banks. This increases the time to settle presence of a trusted third party with powers to overwrite and overturn ledger activities needed to have a unified view.
*   It is expected that a Central bank typically may insist for banks to maintain sufficient liquidity in their settlement accounts or nostro accounts maintained with the Central banks.
*   Note that in case of a cross-border payment for pooled account in certain banks, the originator of the message is modiﬁed and populated by an internal bank account number. This raises concern around the data protection and security in the receiving bank.

# [Know Your Customer (KYC)](#toc.xhtml#s233a)

Financial institutions are particularly susceptible to illegal criminal activity in an increasingly global economy. Financial institutions are shielded against fraud, corruption, money laundering, and terrorism financing by Know Your Customer (KYC) regulations.

KYC is the process of identification and verification of the identity of a client in which a series of controls are applied to avoid having commercial relations with people related to terrorism, corruption or money laundering, among others.

The KYC process is involved in verifying that the client is actually who she/he says she/he is and giving her/him access to the services or products he/she needs. This verification is carried out through different methods, although not all comply with legal requirements.

The KYC process takes place in such a way that the user who wants to become client of a company demonstrates with legal and binding evidence his identity. For this, methods such as video identification by streaming video and videoconferencing are used, in which the user shows and validates his identity documents, the authenticity of them, and their face, in addition to other biometric tests and security checks.

Know Your Customer process can be carried out both remotely online and in-person at a commercial office or store. When it is done remotely and online, or the process has been digitized, we talk about eKYC (electronic Know Your Customer) process.

Financial services professionals are required by KYC rules to attempt to confirm the identity, suitability, and risks associated with sustaining a business relationship. The protocols are under the larger purview of an institution’s anti-money laundering (AML) guidelines. Businesses of all sizes also use KYC procedures to make sure that potential clients, representatives, advisers, or distributors follow anti-bribery laws and are who they say they are. Customers are being asked to submit more and more thorough due diligence information by banks, insurance, export creditors, and other financial organizations.

A reasonable firm or individual would often be required to do due diligence before engaging into a contract or agreement with another party, or before acting with a specific standard of care.

The purpose of the KYC guidelines is to stop criminal elements from using enterprises as a means of money laundering. Businesses can also gain a deeper understanding of their clients’ financial transactions through related procedures. This enables them to prudently manage their risks. Nowadays, banks and various internet companies must adhere to KYC guidelines. Typically, they incorporate the following four essential components within their KYC policies:

*   The policy for accepting customers;
*   The processes for identifying customers;
*   Transaction monitoring; and
*   Risk control.

KYC is an essential and required process for both financial and non-financial companies due to the strict regulatory environment. Because it recognizes suspicious features early in the client-business interaction, it reduces the chance of fraud.

# [Steps Involved in KYC](#toc.xhtml#s234a)

KYC involves several steps:

1.  For Establishing customer identity;
2.  To understand the nature of customers’ activities and qualify that the source of funds is legitimate; and
3.  Assess money laundering risks associated with customers.

A customer/user may be defined as (for the purposes of a KYC policy):

*   Entity or a person or that maintains an account or has a business relationship with the reporting entity;
*   Agency on whose behalf the account is maintained (that is, the beneficial owner);
*   Any or all Beneficiaries of transactions conducted by professional intermediaries such as stockbrokers, chartered accountants, or solicitors, as permitted under the law; or
*   Any unknown person or entity connected with a financial transaction that can pose significant reputational or other risks to the bank, for example, a wire transfer or issue of a high-value demand draft as a single transaction.

Blockchain analytics and crypto compliance experts can help perfect the execution of your KYC lifecycle, allowing you to onboard more customers while feeling safe in the knowledge that you’re meeting regulatory requirements.

This involves a unique combination of:

*   Artificial intelligence,
*   Optical character recognition,
*   Natural language processing and automation,

This unique combination of technologies provide a single source of truth for compliance teams on all of their corporate, SME and individual clients.

KYC process includes:

1.  ID card verification,
2.  Face verification,
3.  Documents for verification, such as utility bills as proof of address and biometric verification.

In addition, real-time cross-border payments depend heavily on KYC compliance, which reduces risk and promotes increased levels of trust, transparency, and cooperation. In order to expedite the compliance process and develop novel, cooperative strategies for addressing financial crime, a community approach is needed.

# [KYC Registry for SWIFT](#toc.xhtml#s235a)

Currently, around 6,000 financial institutions publish and receive KYC data from their correspondent banks via the SWIFT KYC registry. The acknowledged benchmark for correspondent banking due diligence is SWIFT KYC. In an effort to streamline the KYC procedure between banks and corporations, the registration has now been expanded to include corporate SWIFT clients. Customer due diligence and KYC are essential to protect the global financial system from money laundering and financing of illegal and criminal activities.

As of 2018, around half of all high-value cross-border payments worldwide used the SWIFT network, and in 2015, SWIFT linked more than 11,000 financial institutions in over 200 countries and territories, who were exchanging an average of over 32 million messages per day.

The SWIFT’s KYC registry is a secure global platform providing predefined data fields and document types to standardize and streamline the data collection process.

To date, almost 6,000 financial institutions are using SWIFT’s KYC Registry to both publish their KYC data and receive data from their counterparties. SWIFT’s KYC is recognized as the accepted standard for correspondent banking due diligence. The registry has now been extended to corporate customers of SWIFT to help simplify the KYC process between banks and corporates.

# [India: The Reserve Bank of India KYC Guidelines for Banks in 2002](#toc.xhtml#s236a)

The goal of KYC guidelines is to stop criminal elements from using banks for money laundering activities, whether on purpose or accidentally. Banks can better know and comprehend their customers and their financial transactions thanks to these KYC procedures, which also help them manage their risks. The following essential components should be incorporated by banks into their KYC policies:

*   Customer Acceptance Policy
*   Customer Identification Procedures
*   Monitoring of Transactions

# [Blockchains Applications](#toc.xhtml#s237a)

As far as we know, the blockchain of Bitcoin stores information about financial transactions in blocks. More than 10,000 other cryptocurrency systems are currently operating on blockchain technology. By 2022, a number of businesses, including Walmart, Pfizer, AIG, Siemens, Unilever, and many more, will have used blockchain technology. For instance, IBM developed the Food Trust blockchain to track the path taken by food goods as they are delivered to their destinations.

History tells us that Blockchain was first introduced in 2009 to the market as the technology underpinning Bitcoin exchanges, but its practical uses in the world of business extend far beyond cryptocurrency transactions. For example, in finance, blockchain networks allow securities trades to be settled in minutes rather than days. In supply chains, blockchain networks allow the flow of goods and payments to be tracked and logged in real time.

Following questions may be asked to determine whether your use case is a good fit for blockchain questions:

*   Is a business network involved?
*   Is consensus used to validate transactions?
*   Is an audit trail, or provenance, required?
*   Is the record of transactions be immutable, or tamper proof?
*   Should dispute resolution be final?

Even if you answered yes to the first question and to at least one other, then your use case would benefit from blockchain technology. A network always needs to be involved for blockchain to be the right solution, but the network can take many forms. The network can be between organizations, such as a supply chain, or the network can be within an organization. Within an organization, a blockchain network could be used to share reference data between divisions or to create an audit or compliance network, for example. The network can also exist between individuals, who might need to store data, digital assets, or contracts on the blockchain, for example.

# [Blockchain in Food Industry](#toc.xhtml#s238a)

If a food is found to be contaminated, then it can be traced all the way back through each stop to its origin. Not only that, but these companies can also now see everything else it may have come in contact with, allowing the identification of the problem to occur far sooner and potentially saving lives. This is one example of blockchain in practice, but there are many other forms of blockchain implementation.

In the food industry, there have been several reports of *E. Coli* infections, salmonella outbreaks, and listeria outbreaks in addition to inadvertent exposure to dangerous substances in food. It has previously taken weeks to identify the cause of these outbreaks or the reason why people are getting sick from their meals. Blockchain technology allows brands to track a food product from the point of origin to every stop it makes, all the way to delivery.

# [Blockchain in Mortgage Processing](#toc.xhtml#s239a)

Loans against property are another term for mortgage loans, or just mortgages. One can refinance or purchase or build a home with a mortgage loan. Obtaining a new loan for a property while the previous loan is still being paid back is known as refinancing. Usually, the goal is to obtain a loan with favorable conditions.

Blockchain accelerators and blockchain technology are still relatively new in the mortgage market, but they have potential. Understanding every step of the standard mortgage process and how long it takes overall will help you fully appreciate the advantages of blockchain mortgages[^(\[2\])](#c07.xhtml#ftn2).

# [Conventional Loan Procedure](#toc.xhtml#s240a)

The length of time it takes to obtain a mortgage through the traditional route might range from 30 to around 60 days, depending on the time of year and the number of people looking to purchase a property at any particular moment. The normal stages in obtaining a mortgage are listed as follows:

*   **Prequalification**: A mortgage lender evaluates your credit and provides you with an estimate throughout this process.
*   **Application**: Submitting an application is the next action to take. A buyer of a house will often fill out the uniform residential loan application. The application includes all the pertinent information a mortgage lender would need to approve or reject your application, including your income, assets, and work history.
*   **Processing**: The following action involves verifying all the information on the application or processing the loan. Appraising the house you want to buy to make sure its value is at least as much as what you’re paying for it is another step in the loan approval process.
*   **Underwriting**: A mortgage is not yet ready even after the loan processor has examined everything and approved it. The underwriter then receives the file, reviews it all, and determines whether to approve or reject the loan. In particular, the underwriter is assessing the degree of risk and figuring out if a certain applicant would be a problematic borrower.
*   **Settlement**: The last stage is settlement, also known as closing, if the underwriter approves the mortgage. The buyer can move into their new house once the mortgage is finalized following the settlement.

# [The Mortgage Process and Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s241a)

By increasing productivity and lowering costs associated with the mortgage process, blockchain technology can help simplify the mortgage sector.

The mortgage business can gain from blockchain technology in a number of ways. It may contribute to accelerating transaction times, resulting in quicker settlements. It can also result in more accurate documentation and lower costs for all parties participating in the process.

Blockchain accelerators and blockchain technology are still relatively new in the mortgage market, but they have potential. Understanding every step of the standard mortgage process and how long it takes overall will help you fully appreciate the advantages of blockchain mortgages. Gaining a rudimentary understanding of blockchain technology can assist you in seeing how it can simplify the mortgage application process.

**A Blockchain Mortgage Process Example**

A prospective homeowner is already pre-approved for a mortgage. They’ve located a house they wish to purchase.

After they finish the mortgage application, it turns into a block. All participants involved in the mortgage process have access to the block, and the information submitted in the application is validated almost immediately.

The buyer gets an encrypted key to sign the offer after the financing is approved. The buyer’s signature record is added to the chain as a distinct block. Transferring the loan cash to the buyer is the next stage. Both the title transfer and the money transfer become into links in the chain. All things considered, utilizing blockchain technology reduces the time it takes to obtain approval for a mortgage and settle on a loan from several weeks to a few days.

Blockchain technology is still relevant after a mortgage is approved and closed. Blockchain technology can be used by mortgage servicers to monitor borrower payments. Blockchain can assist in proving property ownership for homeowners looking to sell or refinance their property. Blockchain can assist mortgage businesses in verifying that the original servicer has ownership rights to the loans in case one wishes to sell its loans to another mortgage company.

# [Blockchain Mortgages’ Advantages](#toc.xhtml#s242a)

For all parties participating in the mortgage process, blockchain technology has advantages. The following are some possible benefits of the technology:

*   **More precise record-keeping**: As everyone who has ever bought a house knows, the conventional mortgage application process requires an amazing volume of documentation. Buyers must substantiate their assets, income, and work history. Underwriters and loan processors may find themselves engulfed in piles of paperwork. Blockchain gathers all required papers and data into digital records that can be accessed at any time by simply clicking a button.
*   **Lower closing costs for buyers**: Blockchain technology can help cut expenses by doing away with the requirement for third parties throughout the settlement process.
*   **Easier to confirm the chain of title**: When purchasing a home through a traditional manner, purchasers are frequently required to acquire title insurance in case the property is subject to liens. Title insurance is no longer necessary because blockchain technology greatly simplifies the chain of title verification process.
*   **Smart contract implementation**: Smart contracts are coded sets of instructions that start acting upon the occurrence of a predetermined event. For instance, the mortgage procedure can proceed automatically to the next stage if the buyer purchases homeowner’s insurance.

# [Blockchain-Enabled Trade](#toc.xhtml#s243a)

The value of the world’s cryptocurrency industry peaked at $3 trillion at the end of 2021. Blockchain technology is the foundation of cryptocurrencies like Ethereum and Bitcoin. The use of blockchain technology and the products and services it enables will continue to have a significant impact on corporate operations.

However, blockchain technology[^(\[3\])](#c07.xhtml#ftn3) is much more than just a cryptocurrency transfer method that is safe. It can be applied in areas other than finance, such as voting, healthcare, insurance, welfare benefits, gaming, and artist royalties.

The blockchain revolution is coming, and the world economy is getting ready thanks to the way technology is already affecting society and business on many fronts. If you think the word “revolution” sounds dramatic, consider this: eight of the top ten corporations in the world are developing a range of blockchain-based products.

# [Blockchain in Finance and Banking](#toc.xhtml#s244a)

Blockchain helps banks and financial services firms to make loan applications and payment record-keeping more efficient.

The banking sector is one that may stand to gain the most from incorporating blockchain technology into its operations. Financial institutions are only open for business five days a week, during regular business hours. This implies that you will probably have to wait until Monday morning for the money to appear in your account if you try to deposit a check on Friday at 6 p.m.

Because banks have a large volume of transactions to settle, even if you make your deposit during business hours, it may still take one to three days for the transaction to be verified. Blockchain, however, is a 24/7 system.

**Banking**: Blockchain is being used by banks and other financial services organizations to automate payment record-keeping and loan processing. Regardless of holidays, the time of day, or the week, customers can get their transactions processed in as little as 10 minutes by integrating blockchain into banks. This is essentially the same amount of time it takes to add a block to the blockchain. Banks may now transfer money more swiftly and securely between organizations because of blockchain technology. For instance, in the stock trading industry, the money and shares are frozen for up to three days throughout the settlement and clearing procedure (or longer, if trading overseas). Even the few days that the money is in transit can be very costly and risky for banks because of the scale of the amounts involved.

Blockchain is most usually associated with cryptocurrencies like Bitcoin, although it has a wide range of other possible uses.

**Currency**: Without the assistance of a central bank, cryptocurrencies run independently. Like any other currency, they are used by people to make purchases—mostly online. While Bitcoin is among the most well-known cryptocurrencies, there exist other others as well.

The following are some of the existing and possible applications of blockchain technology.

# [The Use of Blockchain Technology in Logistics](#toc.xhtml#s245a)

Businesses can use blockchain to confirm the origin of materials they employ in their products or to reassure consumers that an item comes from a verified source.

# [Blockchain in Real Estate Documents](#toc.xhtml#s246a)

Blockchain technology can be used to confirm or establish the identity of a property’s owner. It can also assist in lowering the price of title searches and property insurance.

Mortgages: There are various ways in which blockchain technology might help the mortgage sector. It may contribute to accelerating transaction times, resulting in quicker settlements. It can also result in more accurate documentation and lower costs for all parties participating in the process.

# [Blockchain in Medical Field](#toc.xhtml#s247a)

Blockchain technology can be used by healthcare professionals to safely preserve patient medical records. Patients can have the assurance that their medical records are unchangeable by writing them onto the blockchain after they are created and signed. To ensure privacy, these private health records might be encrypted and kept on the blockchain using a private key, making them available to only specific people.

Concerns about patient privacy and security are paramount when it comes to electronic patient data storage. Patient information can be kept secret thanks to blockchain technology’s security. The procedure of exchanging patient data with other physicians and healthcare providers is also made easier by blockchain technology.

Other primary applications for blockchain in healthcare include:

*   Protection of healthcare data
*   Personal health record data management
*   Point-of-care genomics management
*   Electronics health records data management

These are some innovative blockchain applications in this space:

*   **MedicalChain:** Offers a blockchain solution to create a user-centric digital health record that can be shared with doctors easily. All information is recorded in an auditable, secure, and transparent format.
*   **Guardtime:** This company is helping healthcare organizations and governments implement blockchain in their cybersecurity systems to keep essential data secure.
*   **Curisium:** This blockchain platform is designed to streamline rebate negotiation and contract management through the use of innovative digital contracts.
*   **Robomed:** This platform uses telemedicine sessions, chatbots, and wearable devices to collect patient data, recording it on blockchain so it can be shared securely with healthcare professionals.
*   **The MediLedger Project:** Initially launched by Chronicled in 2019, the MediLedger Project aims to improve the track-and-trace capabilities for prescription medicine. Built to meet the requirements set out by the U.S. Drug Supply Chain Security Act (DSCSA), the blockchain healthcare project meets the legal requirements as well as supporting the operational needs of the pharmaceutical supply industry.

# [Blockchain in Elections and Voting](#toc.xhtml#s248a)

Election officials can use blockchain technology to capture and count people’s votes immediately. Since the technology is more secure than other options, it would also cut down on the risk of voter fraud and tampering.

As previously mentioned, blockchain could be used to facilitate a modern voting system. Voting with blockchain carries the potential to eliminate election fraud and boost voter turnout, as was tested in the November 2018 midterm elections in West Virginia. Using blockchain in this way would make votes nearly impossible to tamper with. The blockchain protocol would also maintain transparency in the electoral process, reducing the personnel needed to conduct an election and providing officials with nearly instant results. This would eliminate the need for recounts or any real concern that fraud might threaten the election.

# [Enterprise Blockchain Requirements](#toc.xhtml#s249a)

We believe that blockchain is a truly disruptive technology that can transform business networks. We also believe that this innovation has to happen in the open, collaborating with other technology companies and industries. To this end, IBM continues to contribute code to several active Hyperledger projects.

From IBM’s perspective, industrial-grade blockchain technologies have the following characteristics:

*   A shared, permissioned ledger is the append-only system of record (SOR) and single source of truth. It is visible to the authenticated members in the business network channels.
*   A consensus protocol agreed to by all participating members of the business network ensures that the ledger is updated only with network-verified transactions.
*   Cryptography ensures tamper-proof security, authentication, and integrity of transactions.
*   Chaincode (also called smart contracts) encapsulates participant terms of agreement for the business that takes place on the network; chaincode is stored on the validating peer nodes in the blockchain.
*   In addition to these attributes, enterprise blockchain technology needs to meet key industry requirements such as performance, verified identifies, and private and confidential transactions. Hyperledger Fabric has been architected to meet these needs. It is also designed with a pluggable consensus model, allowing businesses to select an optimal algorithm for their networks.

# [Currency](#toc.xhtml#s250a)

Blockchain forms the bedrock for cryptocurrencies like Bitcoin. The U.S. dollar is controlled by the Federal Reserve. Under this central authority system, a user’s data and currency are technically at the whim of their bank or government. If a user’s bank is hacked, the client’s private information is at risk. If the client’s bank collapses or the client lives in a country with an unstable government, the value of their currency may be at risk. In 2008, several failing banks were bailed out — partially using taxpayer money. These are the worries out of which Bitcoin was first conceived and developed.

By spreading its operations across a network of computers, blockchain allows Bitcoin and other cryptocurrencies to operate without the need for a central authority. This not only reduces risk but also eliminates many of the processing and transaction fees. It can also give those in countries with unstable currencies or financial infrastructures a more stable currency with more applications and a wider network of individuals and institutions with whom they can do business, both domestically and internationally.

Using cryptocurrency wallets for savings accounts or as a means of payment is especially profound for those who have no state identification. Some countries may be war-torn or have governments that lack any real infrastructure to provide identification. Citizens of such countries may not have access to savings or brokerage accounts — and, therefore, no way to safely store wealth.

# [Property Records](#toc.xhtml#s251a)

If you have ever spent time in your local recorder’s office, you will know that the process of recording property rights is both burdensome and inefficient. Today, a physical deed must be delivered to a government employee at the local recording office, where it is manually entered into the county’s central database and public index. In the case of a property dispute, claims to the property must be reconciled with the public index.

This process is not just costly and time-consuming — it is also prone to human error, where each inaccuracy makes tracking property ownership less efficient. Blockchain has the potential to eliminate the need for scanning documents and tracking down physical files in a local recording office. If property ownership is stored and verified on the blockchain, owners can trust that their deed is accurate and permanently recorded.

In war-torn countries or areas that have little to no government or financial infrastructure, and certainly no recorder’s office, it can be nearly impossible to prove ownership of a property. If a group of people living in such an area is able to leverage blockchain, then transparent and clear timelines of property ownership could be established.

# [Supply Chains](#toc.xhtml#s252a)

As in the IBM Food Trust example, suppliers can use blockchain to record the origins of materials that they have purchased. This would allow companies to verify the authenticity of not only their products but also common labels such as “*Organic*,” “*Local*,” and “*Fair Trade*.”

As reported by Forbes, the food industry is increasingly adopting the use of blockchain to track the path and safety of food throughout the farm-to-user journey.

Any industry or organization involved in the recording and overseeing of transactions of any kind stands to benefit from moving its operations onto a blockchain-based platform. Take a look at the following predictions of how blockchain technology will influence various sectors of the global landscape.

# [Blockchain Technology Disrupting Financial Services](#toc.xhtml#s253a)

One area predicted to continue evolving with blockchain technology is cross-border payments. These recent developments are leading transformation on this front:

*   **IBM’s Blockchain World Wire:** This blockchain-enabled conduit used the Stellar protocol and allowed for banks to clear and settle cross-border payments almost immediately. The World Wire API integrated into banks’ payment systems, with World Wire then converting the digital asset into currency, completing the transaction. In light of the COVID-19 pandemic, IBM ceased running the network and made the code open source, inviting the development community to build on their learnings.
*   **Paystack:** Paystack creates payment infrastructures and connects payment processors with each other with the goal of making it quicker and easier to facilitate online payments. In October 2020, financial services company Stripe acquired Pay stack for $200 million. The service expanded to South Africa in May 2021, by that point it was powering 50 percent of online payments in Nigeria, where it was founded. In September, it became the first Nigerian payment gateway to become an Apple Pay partner, opening it up to 380 million users across 60 countries.
*   **Ripple and Pyypl:** San Francisco-based software vendor Ripple has been specializing in global real-time payments, based on blockchain technology, since 2014. They recently partnered with Dubai-based technology company Pyypl, which has created a blockchain-based platform offering non-bank financial services via smartphone. Because the platform doesn’t require the pre-funding needed in traditional cross-border payments, where additional funds must be kept in the user’s bank account, this should help increase the liquidity of companies that use it.
*   **AZA Finance:** This company uses blockchain technology for small businesses to send and collect payments to and from Africa. By converting the relevant fiat currencies into stablecoins rather than US dollars when trading, AZA says it can reduce reliance on a dollar-dominated system, trade during banking holidays, and increase trade efficiency.

Blockchain technology has applications outside of payments too. Following are additional examples of fintech innovations using blockchain:

*   **Securrency:** This is a trading platform for cryptocurrencies and any kind of asset. It is exchanged through Securrency tokens, which allows cryptos to be traded outside of their dedicated exchanges.
*   **ABRA:** This global app and cryptocurrency wallet allows you to buy, invest, and store up to 100 cryptocurrencies. The company recently raised $55 million in funding to help develop a comprehensive product range to help people manage their money.
*   **Numerai:** This company aims to build an open-source hedge fund by sending encrypted datasets to thousands of decentralized quantitative analysts, who build predictive models. The best are rewarded with Numerai’s token and used to create a trading meta-model.
*   **Bloom:** This startup is applying blockchain to credit scoring by building a protocol that manages risk, identity, and credit scoring.

If you want to learn more about cryptocurrencies, UCT offers the Blockchain and Digital Currency: The Future of Money online short course. This six-week program reveals how crypto assets are set to shape the future of the financial industry, while imparting a practical working knowledge of blockchain and cryptocurrency assets.

Also focusing on cryptocurrencies is the SDA Bocconi School of Management’s Bitcoin and Blockchain Program. This five-week online course explores the technical pillars that underpin these technologies, and is ideally suited to individuals driven to remain relevant by upskilling to address this new business need.

# [Adopting Blockchain Technology in Business](#toc.xhtml#s254a)

Blockchain technology has huge implications for business, with the main drivers for adoption being higher revenue, lower costs, and more efficient use of time. Examples of how blockchain is being adopted by corporate companies include:

*   **ConsenSys Quorum:** Originally developed by JP Morgan, Quorum is an enterprise-grade service that helps companies grow and manage large-scale blockchain networks.
*   **LVMH:** The luxury goods company is tracking goods and fighting counterfeiting through blockchain. It has registered more than 10 million products on a platform created in conjunction with Prada and Cartier.
*   **MediaChain:** This blockchain database was acquired by Spotify to manage copyrights and royalty payments, and resolve rights holder issues.

# [Application of Blockchain in Legal Field](#toc.xhtml#s255a)

In law, blockchain technology can be used to create smart contracts, as well as validate ownership documents such as title deeds. If used to validate, authenticate, and secure courtroom evidence, blockchain could improve efficiency in the criminal justice system.

Smart contracts are digital contracts that uphold the nature of a legal contract. The terms of the contract are written in code and automatically executed when the agreed conditions have been met, without the need for any involvement or intermediary. When this happens, the blockchain is updated, and only parties with permissions can see the results.

Some states in the U.S. have begun to permit the use of smart contracts in certain legal contexts. These include Arizona, where parties can create enforceable legal agreements through smart contracts, and California, where marriage licenses can be issued via blockchain.

# [Use of Blockchain in Real Estate](#toc.xhtml#s256a)

Real estate transactions are usually expensive, paperwork-heavy, and require the assistance of agents. Blockchain has the potential to ease the process of finding and buying real estate through tokenization, using digital real estate tokens to represent physical assets. This would:

*   Ease transfer between buyer and seller by cutting out the need for a middleman.
*   Provide irrefutable proof of ownership via a distributed digital ledger.
*   Facilitate smaller real estate investments, as a token can be divided in the same way as a bitcoin.
*   Improve market security and transparency because every transaction is processed and approved by others.

Examples of real estate using blockchain technology include Deedcoin, which connects real estate agents with home buyers and reduces commission to 1 percent, and Harbor, which enables the tokenization of private securities, REITs, land titles, and land registry records.

# [Logistics and Supply Chain Through Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s257a)

**Supply chains:** The links between the creation and distribution of goods – have always been disrupted by innovation. Today’s supply chains are extremely complex, threading multiple continents, including huge numbers of invoices and payments, involving myriad entities, and potentially extending over months.

Due to this complexity, blockchain is an attractive means to transform the supply chain and logistics industry. When goods are transferred to a new step in the supply chain, the process can be logged securely and permanently, creating an immutable, auditable history from its point of origin to that of sale.

Following are some examples of innovative logistics and supply chain-focused blockchain applications:

*   **TradeLens:** Developed by IBM, this blockchain solution for logistics and supply chain provides transparency and improved efficiency through blockchain, offering a holistic view of shipment data and documents. More than half the world’s container ships are registered on the platform, and in 2020 it processed 1 billion shipments.
*   **Provenance:** Consumers are increasingly demanding transparency regarding the products they purchase to ensure the ethical sourcing and production of products. Provenance uses blockchain to prove chain-of-custody and certification of supply chains.
*   **Honeywell:** The US conglomerate has partnered with iTRACE and SecureMarking to secure, track, and trace its aerospace parts throughout the world. It does so using laser-etching and invisible ink on the parts, encoding this information onto a blockchain ledger for digital authenticity.
*   **De Beers:** De Beers mines, trades, and markets more than 30% of the world’s supply of diamonds. The company uses a blockchain ledger to trace diamonds from the mine to the customer purchase, and helps confirm diamonds are free from conflict.

# [Impact of Blockchain Innovation on Business](#toc.xhtml#s258a)

The tools and platforms described are just a few examples of how blockchain technology has been applied in various industries. This highlights the implications this technology will have for businesses, trade, and entire economies and how important it is for business leaders, tradespeople, and decision-makers to become familiar with the technology.

Deloitte’s 2021 Global Blockchain Survey found that 81 percent of business leaders believed blockchain was broadly scalable and had achieved mainstream adoption. Blockchain skills have become one of the most sought-after proficiencies in the world, with the demand increasing by 59 percent from the end of 2020 to the beginning of 2021.

# [Trade](#toc.xhtml#s259a)

Blockchain in financial trading means transparent pricing, new alternative markets, faster payment processing and immutable transaction recordkeeping. Blockchain’s ledger techno Blockchain enables data to be recorded in a secure digital format by providing real-time information on transactions between different parties, be they corporations, supplier networks, investment pools, or an international supply chain. Technology is enabling people to trade for lower costs and at faster speeds than ever before.

It provides all parties with a record that is secure, encrypted, transparent, easy to access, and impossible to tamper with. Although blockchain emerged within the financial system with the launch of cryptocurrency Bitcoin, today it is used in a wide range of activities, including ones that are directly or indirectly related to foreign trade.

The long value chain tied to international trade includes vast, complex areas like logistics, transportation, customs administration, financing, and administrative procedures between firms, all of which could be streamlined by adopting this technology.

Blockchain optimizes processes, makes goods traceable, guarantees the security of payments and financing, facilitates the verification of digital quality and origin certifications, enables real-time sharing of information on the different stages of trade, and helps improve how related public and private services operate, among other benefits.

Blockchain provides solutions for trade operations by simplifying cross-border trade, contributing to competitive improvements, and reducing transaction costs. Although blockchain has been used within foreign trade for several years, its significance has increased since the start of the COVID-19 health crisis and it is expected to play an even more prominent role in the post-pandemic world.

A concrete example of how blockchain is being used today comes from French retailer Auchan, which has implemented an international food traceability system using blockchain throughout its supply chain. After piloting this solution in Vietnam, it has applied this technology to track goods and services originating in France, Italy, Spain, Portugal, and Senegal, working in partnership with Te-Food. The system enables the tracking of different products from farm to fork and the recording of food quality data (and related logistical information) via QR codes.

# [International Trade](#toc.xhtml#s260a)

International trade is a $16 trillion market that accounts for the exchange of capital, goods, and services across international borders or territories. It is broadly split into two categories:

*   75% various goods typically shipped by shipping containers or ground transportation
*   25% commodities

From a shipping and transportation viewpoint, the trade and financing industry primarily suffers from a lack of trust and coordination between exporters and importers, particularly within emerging to developed markets. Additionally, the industry maintains various operational inefficiencies due to the complex nature of operational processes in the international trade of goods and commodities. For instance, shipping and trading still heavily rely on human resources and are affected by manual and paper-based processes which are very costly, slow and error-prone.

Exporters and importers face challenges to finance or guarantee their transactions, which stymies growth and limits the benefits from globalization. Historically, this space has been very resistant to advances in technology and digitization although some technologies like Commodities Trading and Risk Management (CTRM) solutions have proved useful.

# [Benefits of Blockchain in Global Trade and Commerce](#toc.xhtml#s261a)

Over the past 10-15 years, many start-ups and technology companies have attempted to develop products with mixed success — until the emergence of blockchain technology for which international trade is identified as a primary use case[^(\[4\])](#c07.xhtml#ftn4). The potential impact of blockchain technology on international trade finance has spurred many companies and consortiums to update their outdated technology. Beyond ushering in the era of digitization, blockchain enables the tokenization of existing documents, letters of credit, and more. Smart contracts will improve coordination between exporters and importers through the automation of agreements, business events, and other manually intensive processes. The global adoption of blockchain technology will create even greater benefits for cross-border coordination, trade settlement, and standardization.

Blockchains are effective in scenarios where a confirmed sequence of events is business critical. And they work very well where predetermined business logic needs to be respected by multiple nodes in a network, with no scope for tampering. So far, several startups have attempted to address trade finance issues with blockchain. However, we have found their approach to be defective in two key regards.

Firstly, they usually aim to map existing processes into a blockchain, rather than fundamentally rethinking the underlying business model and information flows.

This approach has led to multiple solutions providing a “digital letter of credit”. What these solutions fail to do is a question whether such a letter is needed, when credit can be issued and confirmed and payment initiated instantly.

Secondly, they tend to map only a single, isolated piece of the value chain, focused usually on letters of credit or bills of lading. They are much less concerned with the issuance and advisory aspects that are part of the wider process. Importers and exporters today pay for services in these areas and they are expensive. Yet in practice, they are often neglected and the customer experience is poor. By contrast, along the total trade finance transaction, we foresee blockchain addressing two fundamental challenges:

*   **Automating issuance:** Assume the various network players have adopted the proposed platform. Now they will record events such as issuance of credit, shipment of goods or the payment for a transaction in Realtime to the blockchain, which in turn broadcasts this out to the network. Given this public record of events, a paper-trail-chasing operations team is no longer required. And subsequent steps in the workflow can be reliably initiated on an automated basis.
*   **Full audit transparency**: Shipments are prone to fraud or illegal activity. A shipping container labelled as carrying bananas may in reality be freighting weapons. Effective control is clearly needed.

Blockchain can very effectively meet this need through a network-controlled mechanism, where a “*shared truth*” is established by participation of the majority. In addition, all salient events, such as term agreements, shipments or payments can be traced back and the likelihood of erroneous events is minimized. Access to this information can be limited if desired to approved network participants, resulting in a hybrid that shares traits of both private and public blockchains.

As well as addressing the areas referenced above, blockchain can enable cost-effective development of client endpoints across the globe. This gives key actors the physical ability to feed information directly into the system in real time. If we assume the platform champions decide to adopt open development, and to make the blockchain’s logic accessible, we see much more than a workflow solution emerging. We witness the creation of an entire ecosystem of trade finance applications. These will all be backed by the same internal logic, but are closely tailored to the needs of different actors, such as a shipping coordinator, a bank employee or a buyer of goods.

![](images/Figure-7.5.jpg)

**Figure 7.5:** The trade finance transactional environment workflow interpreted as a blockchain sequence

Just as most of the protocols on internet like TCP/IP, HTML, and HTTP provide shared and open standards and protocols that enabled the internet to become what it is today, so too can blockchain and related technologies create a flatter, smarter, more connected, and overall better world for global trade and commerce.

# [Trade Finance](#toc.xhtml#s262a)

Trade Finance is the financing of goods or services in a trade or transaction, from a supplier through to the end buyer[^(\[5\])](#c07.xhtml#ftn5). It accounts for 3% of global trade, worth some $3tn annually. The ‘*Trade Finance*’ is an umbrella term, which includes a variety of financial instruments that can be used by an importer or exporter.

These include:

*   Purchase Order Finance
*   Stock Finance
*   Structured Commodity Finance
*   Invoice Finance (Discounting and Factoring)
*   Supply Chain Finance
*   Letters of Credit (LCs)
*   Bonds and Guarantees

The two terms *Import Finance and Export Finance* are used interchangeably with **Trade Finance.**

# [Trade Finance Benefit to Business](#toc.xhtml#s263a)

In business trade finance facilitates the growth of a business by securing funds required to purchase *goods and stock*. Managing cash and working capital is critical to the success of any business. Trade finance is a tool which is used to unlock capital from a company’s existing stock or receivables or add further finance facilities based on a company’s trade cycles.

To sum up a trade finance facility may allow you to offer more competitive terms to both suppliers and customers, by reducing payment gaps in your trade cycle. It is beneficial for supply chain relationships and growth as follows:

*   Small to medium-term working capital, using the underlying products or services being imported/exported as security/collateral. It increases the revenue potential of a company, and earlier payments may allow for higher margins.
*   Trade finance allows companies to request higher volumes of stock or place larger orders with suppliers, leading to economies of scale and bulk discounts.
*   Trade finance helps in strengthening the relationship between buyers and sellers, increasing profit margins. It allows a company to be more competitive.
*   The work of managing the supply chain is critical for any business. Trade and supply chain finance helps ease out cash constraints or liquidity gaps – for suppliers, customers, third parties, employees or providers. Earlier payments also mitigate risk for suppliers.
*   It is important to note that trade finance focuses more on the trade rather than the underlying borrower, that is, it is not balance sheet led. Therefore, small businesses with weaker balance sheets can use trade finance to trade significantly larger volumes of goods or services and work with stronger end customers.

Due to the factor of embedded risk mitigants that surround trade finance lending and instruments, it leads to the potential of a diversity of supplier base for trading companies. A more diverse supplier network increases competition and efficiency in markets and supply chains.

The trading companies can also mitigate business risks by using appropriate trade finance structures. Late payments from debtors, bad debts, excess stock and demanding creditors can have detrimental effects on a business. External financing or revolving credit facilities can ease this pressure by effectively financing trade flows.

***Note :*** *The International Chamber of Commerce (ICC) is the world’s largest business organization with a network of over 6 million members in more than 100 countries.*

# [*we.trade*: The Largest Blockchain-Enabled Trade Networks](#toc.xhtml#s264a)

Until the launch of we.trade, the universal solution for open account trading or documentary trading was to use paper. But the slow process led to a lot of replication, with business owners filling out the same documents for different trade partners and leaving themselves more susceptible to forgery and human error.

Luckily there is already a growing number of businesses looking for blockchain-based solutions like we.trade.

Already one of the largest blockchain-enabled trade networks in production, we.trade is designed to improve the trade finance lending process, supporting companies as they grow their business and expand into new markets. Starting in 2020, over the last few months we.trade has seen momentum continue to build as companies move to replace paper based trade finance processes with a digitized solution. Starting July 2020, we.trade had extended services to additional banks and clients across Europe before expanding the network globally, starting with Asia.

we.trade is a digital platform for securely managing, tracking and settling trade transactions between SMEs in one place. It aims are to make domestic and cross-border commerce easier for European companies by harnessing the power of distributed ledger technology.

The platform we.trade’s is built on IBM’s blockchain platform using the **open-source Hyperledger Fabric**.

we.trade is the world’s first enterprise-grade, blockchain-enabled trade finance platform. It offers a safe and more reliable platform for buyers and sellers to Built on the latest version of the IBM Blockchain Platform. Itis backed by some of the world’s top banks including ***CaixaBank, Deutsche Bank, Erste Group, HSBC, KBC, Nordea, Rabobank, Santander, Société Générale, UBS and UniCredit****.* It is having facility to connect buyers, sellers, banks, insurers and logistics organizations in a network that simplifies cross-border trading.

we.trade also automate many of the processes around trade finance, as well as provide traders with access to *insurance, credit rating* and *logistics services*. Once on the network, traders can initiate orders, manage the order-to-payment process and attain financing. They can also search the network to discover new trading partners.trade globally using distributed ledger technology and smart contracts[^(\[6\])](#c07.xhtml#ftn6).

we.trade was established as an independent company based in Ireland in 2017 through a joint venture between seven European banks. Today the company is owned by 12 banks, technology leader IBM, and global commercial credit and business information provider CRIF with shareholders from institutions such as CaixaBank, Deutsche Bank, Erste Group, HSBC, KBC, Nordea, Rabobank, Santander, Societe Generale, UBS, and UniCredit. In 2019, the company brought we.trade technology to market under a SaaS model and has already onboarded 16 banks across 15 countries to join the platform.

Any company that is a customer of the European consortium of banks which supports the we.trade platform can easily sign up and begin trading. Abank *Flattered* — who is a client of *Nordea*, the leading bank in the Nordic region and a founding member of we.trade — was one of the early adopters to recognize how they could reap the benefits of we.trade to solve their cash flow problems and grow their business.

# [we.trade and Small and Medium-sized Enterprises (SMEs)](#toc.xhtml#s265a)

SMEs such as Flattered AB represent 90% of the businesses and 50% of the employment worldwide. According to the World Bank, SMEs traditionally do not have access to bank guarantees, invoice financing, or credit insurance to help maintain and scale their businesses. Instead, SME owners are left to navigate a laborious paper-based process to account for their international transactions with no protections against late invoice payments, fraud, or managing pre-payments.

# [Supply Chain Finance](#toc.xhtml#s266a)

By definition supply chain finance, also known as *supplier finance or reverse factoring*, is a set of solutions that optimizes cash flow by allowing businesses to lengthen their payment terms to their suppliers while providing the option for their large and SME suppliers to get paid early. The best part of Supply Chain Finance is a set of tech-based business and financing processes that lower costs and improve efficiency for the parties involved in a transaction. Supply chain finance works best when the buyer has a better credit rating than the seller and can thus access capital at a lower cost. Also, supply chain financing is a set of services available for medium-sized and big corporates. for example, loans, purchasing order finance, factoring and invoice discounting are the most common.

The difference between *trade finance and supply chain finance* is that while both trade finance and supply chain finance are designed to finance international and domestic supply chains, trade finance offers a broader set of solutions. In recent years, supply chain finance volumes have grown significantly, reaching a total value of $1.31 trillion in 2020, according to the latest World Supply Chain Finance Report.

Worldwide large manufacturers, such as airplane manufacturer Boeing Co., and other global companies, including soft drinks producer Keurig Dr Pepper Inc., are avid users of supply-chain financing to extend payment terms. But there tends to be a barrier to entry for some businesses, especially those with weaker credit ratings.

# [Digital Identity in Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s267a)

Since the dawn of the Internet, identity management has been a key concern, with billions of dollars being spent on usability, security, and privacy[^(\[7\])](#c07.xhtml#ftn7). Despite very huge investment, managing digital identities continues to be plagued by three Cs – Cumbersome, Costly, and Challenging.

With data driving the world today, digital identity is critical to most business and social transactions. But traditional identity systems continue to be highly vulnerable, with single points of failure, attracting continuous attempts to gain access to the complete repository of high-value data.

Blockchain can empower users to have greater control over their own identity.

Organizations can use the information only with customers’ consent and no central entity would be able to compromise a consumer’s identity.

# [Meaning of Identity](#toc.xhtml#s268a)

Blockchain has facilitated the so-called self-sovereign identity, which is inherently unalterable and more secure than traditional identity systems. As such this has the potential to completely change the way we use identities to connect to different online services. Individuals would use their self-sovereign ID to verify their identity, removing the need for passwords.

![](images/Figure-7.6.jpg)

**Figure 7.6:** Characteristics of Identity in Blockchain transactions

As with every lifechanging innovation, there’s been an extended period of evolution, with experts exchanging ideas and little consensus on what self-sovereign ID means! It’s a concept that stems from the belief that an individual must have control over the administration of his identity.

The ID cannot be locked into one site and there needs to be interoperability of the ID across multiple platforms, with user consent.

Most experts have been contemplating the summation of various identifying information like demographic and employment related data and even information about the individual revealed by other people.

In principle, self-sovereign identity would allow users to control their identities in following manner:

1.  Access and update information (though third-party verification may be required with some claims)
2.  Choose the information that they prefer to keep private
3.  Transporting the data (to another organization or even another jurisdiction if they relocate)
4.  Delete the identity if that’s what is wanted

Such a system would need to ensure certain things:

*   Powerful algorithms to validate an identity and its claims
*   Independent of a particular platform or architecture
*   Identities to have a long shelf life (ideally forever)
*   Interoperability (to make it widely available)
*   Sharing of data only with the consent of the user
*   Organizations should be able to access only what is needed and not the complete information
*   Protection of user rights

# [Managing Multiple Identities](#toc.xhtml#s269a)

**Use of multiple Identities**: An individual may have one identity across multiple platforms or may want different identities supporting different ‘personas’ for the workplace, friends, family, and so on.

It is noteworthy that Blockchain can support this flexibility and offer a key for each of these identities, giving the user the power to decide which persona to use in a particular situation.

# [Anonymous Authentication](#toc.xhtml#s270a)

The solution deploys anonymous authentication to ensure maximum security. If a public key is needed to access one’s digital identity, this key is prone to hacker attacks. Having anonymous authentication allows individuals to use unique attributes that identify them, eliminating the need for a public key.

**Advantages of having digital identity:**

*   It puts control of your personal data back into your hands rather than being in the hands of industry giants.
*   Addresses issues faced by refugees. They have no access to the country they belong to. They don’t have proper documentation. They cannot borrow money, open an account, buy a house or start a business. They remain as non-entities and cannot participate in society.
*   Removes the need to deal with bureaucratic processes for passport creation.
*   Enhances security and privacy of medical records and intellectual property, while facilitating compliance with regulations protecting patient data.
*   Simplifies KYC processes. Currently, every bank and financial institution individually performs the KYC process, validates the information and documents, and stores a digitized version. With digital identity being maintained on a shared ledger, banks can access relevant parts of the stored data (with customer consent) and perform due diligence.

# [Blockchain: The Challenges Facing Governments](#toc.xhtml#s271a)

To make these benefits a reality, the governments of LAC countries need to tackle the challenges that implementing blockchain implies[^(\[6\])](#c07.xhtml#ftn6).

These challenges fall into three groups:

*   The first concerns technical issues, which include the difficulties associated with developing technological infrastructure.
*   The second relates to administration and governance, including the need for open standards and the creation of sector and business coalitions in different LAC countries to develop interoperability and economies of scale.
*   The third issue is large-scale implementation, which includes data quality, the participation of all players involved in the transaction, the development of inclusive systems, and secure interfaces with legacy systems.

The IDB provides - through its Integration and Trade Sector, LAC countries with different technical and financial support programs to help spread blockchain technology and its use in international trade, drawing on the lessons learned from the development and implementation of the CADENA initiative.

For international trade this is a major new opportunity, one that is described in detail in “*Blockchain and International Trade: New Technologies for a Bigger and Better Latin America International Insertion*”. LAC cannot remain on the sidelines when it comes to increasing the quantity and quality of trade flows between countries in the region and with the rest of the world.

Tata Steel has executed a blockchain enabled paperless export order with a metals major in Bangladesh. This makes it the first such deal executed between an India-based company and a counterparty in Bangladesh assisted by different banks at respective ends. This transaction was facilitated and expediated by Standard Chartered, India, and was conducted on Contour’s blockchain trade platform, which enables banks, corporates, and other trade partners across the world to transact with each other.

Standard Chartered Bank advised and confirmed the letter of credit (LoC) favoring Tata Steel basis instructions received over Contour and was also able to present documents to the buyer’s bank over the platform. This helped in early resolution of discrepancies and reducing the overall turn-around-time from weeks to days.

The seamless transmission of LoC and e-presentation of document between the two different banks at both ends via the platform under three hours is a testament to reduced paperwork and time, and increased operational efficiency brought about by this solution.

Rajeev Singhal, Vice President (Flat Products), Tata Steel, said: “*Tata Steel has made pioneering efforts this year to digitally transform its trade practices that are sustainable, efficient, and also saves time. We are glad and more confident now that we have executed the first multi-bank blockchain enabled trade. The technology along with the pivoting efforts of Tata Steel provides efficiency and agility in enabling a better customer interface. The Covid-19 pandemic has highlighted that technology will lead the way of doing business in future and experimentation using blockchain for better customer experience is one such step towards enabling this transformation*.”

This integrated solution using blockchain technology allows all parties involved in the transaction to communicate on a single platform in real time. It aims to reduce processing time by digitizing the entire LC process and eliminating the operational inefficiencies as compared to traditional paper-intensive process of LoCs. Further, this technology also enhances the safety of transactions. The information can be shared only with other authorized members on the network and is immutable, reducing the risk of data manipulation and fraud while increasing transparency for all parties involved in the transaction.

Earlier this year, Tata Steel executed its first blockchain enabled trade and this will be its first multi-bank transaction out of India. The Company is committed to move towards digitally enabled transactions which will help in seamless and efficient trading practices in the future.

# [About Tata Steel](#toc.xhtml#s272a)

Tata Steel group is among the top global steel companies with an annual crude steel capacity of 33 million tons per annum. It is one of the world’s most geographically diversified steel producers, with operations and commercial presence across the world. The group recorded a consolidated turnover of US $21.06 billion in the financial year ending March 31, 2021.

# [Benefits of Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s273a)

Blockchain can be used to[^(\[8\])](#c07.xhtml#ftn8):

*   Facilitate the traceability of traded goods, which helps reduce logistics costs and safeguard operations from start to finish;
*   Contribute to the digitalization of the entire rules of origin process at every stage, which is key to streamlining certifications and customs clearance;
*   Address the challenges of cross-border data exchanges between public agencies or authorities and private companies. Through the CADENA initiative, which the IDB is facilitating as part of its LACChain program, eight customs authorities in LAC are now sharing data from firms that have been certified as Authorized Economic Operators (AEOs), as stipulated in their mutual recognition agreements;
*   Modernize Single Windows for Foreign Trade (VUCEs) to streamline operations and reduce transaction costs by increasing interoperability between players and facilitating access to information for all parties involved ;
*   Transfer payments more quickly and cheaply than is currently possible through the SWIFT network. As well as reducing transaction times, blockchain commissions are lower and without maximum limits, which is especially advantageous for exporting SMEs;
*   Reduce the time needed to issue a letter of credit from 7 to 10 days to as little as 4 hours.

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml#s274a)

In this chapter, we learned how blockchain technology can streamline cross-border payments by facilitating faster, cheaper, and more transparent transactions. We gained knowledge about the KYC process and its importance in financial regulations. Additionally, we are able to identify key areas within an enterprise that could benefit from blockchain implementation, such as managing currency, property records, and supply chains. By studying this chapter, we gained a comprehensive understanding of how blockchain technology can be leveraged across various industries, potentially leading to a more efficient, transparent, and secure business landscape. In the next chapter, we will learn Hyperledger and Smart Contracts.

# [CHAPTER 8](#toc.xhtml#c08)

# [Hyperledger and Smart Contracts](#toc.xhtml#c08)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml#s276a)

Smart contracts and voting systems are extra facilities which have made Blockchain technology popular. We have learnt about distributed open environment in *[Chapter 4](#c04.xhtml)* titled *Distributed Consensus in Open Environments* and similarly closed environment in *[Chapter 6](#c06.xhtml)* titled *Distributed Consensus in Closed Environment*, which provides information about consensus models for permissioned blockchain. Fabric is the first completely expandable blockchain system for running distributed applications because it supports modular consensus protocols. This has later on become a part of Hyperledger. Launched in 2016, Hyperledger has more than 200 members from a variety of global industries including financial services, healthcare, supply chains, manufacturing, and the tech sector. Notable members include Walmart, Visa, FedEx, Huawei, Aetna, and Telefonica, among others.

We shall learn about various components of the Hyperledger Fabric Network.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml#s277a)

In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:

*   Hyperledger Fabric
*   Architecture of Hyperledger
*   Writing a Blockchain Application
*   Identity
*   Policies in Hyperledger Fabric
*   Access Control
*   Membership Service Provider (MSP)
*   Channels in Hyperledger System
*   Transaction Validation
*   Executing Contracts in Blockchain
*   Smart Contract Platforms
*   Benefits of Smart Contracts
*   Challenges to Smart Contracts Technology

# [Hyperledger Fabric](#toc.xhtml#s278a)

Fabric is the first completely expandable blockchain system for running distributed applications because it supports modular consensus protocols. This means that the system can be customized to fit specific use cases and trust models. Additionally, Fabric is the first blockchain system that does not rely on a native cryptocurrency to execute distributed applications written in common, general-purpose programming languages. This is a stark contrast to current blockchain platforms, which demand that smart-contracts be cryptocurrency-dependent or written in domain-specific languages.

One of the Hyperledger projects housed by the Linux Foundation, the “Fabric” is a modular and expandable open-source architecture for implementing and running permissioned blockchains. This was later expanded as Hyperledger.

Using a portable idea of membership—which can be combined with industry-standard identity management—Fabric realizes the permissioned model. Fabric provides a whole new blockchain design and reworks how blockchains handle non-determinism, resource exhaustion, and performance threats in order to accommodate such flexibility.

# [Advantages of Hyperledger Fabric Architecture](#toc.xhtml#s279a)

The Hyperledger Fabric architecture delivers the following advantages[^(\[1\])](#c08.xhtml#ftn1):

*   **Permissioned blockchain framework:** Hyperledger Fabric is a permissioned blockchain framework with a modular architecture (plug-and-play). It utilizes container technology to host smart contract (Chaincode) which contains application logic. Before going to each component in detail, let us see a high-level transaction flow and basic components involved.
*   **Open-source blockchain:** Hyperledger Fabric platform is an open-source blockchain framework hosted by the Linux Foundation. It has an active and growing community of developers. Hyperledger Fabric networks are permissioned, meaning all participating member’s identities are known and authenticated.
*   **Chaincode trust flexibility**: Hyperledger’s architecture distinguishes between trust assumptions for ordering chaincodes, or blockchain applications. Put differently, one group of nodes (orderers) may provide the ordering service and accept some of them failing or misbehaving, and each chaincode may have a separate set of endorsers.
*   **Capability to grow**: The system may scale better if these roles were performed by the same nodes since the endorser nodes in charge of a given chaincode are impertinent (orthogonal) to the orderers. Specifically, this happens when distinct chaincodes designate discontinuous endorsers, introducing a chaincode partitioning across endorsers and permitting simultaneous chaincode execution (endorsement). In addition, the ordering service’s critical path is freed from the potentially expensive chaincode execution.
