*   **Propriety**: The architecture makes it easier to implement chaincodes with secrecy requirements for transaction, state updates, and content.
*   **Modularity of consensus**: Because of its modular design, ordering services or pluggable consensus implementations are possible.

# [Architecture of Hyperledger](#toc.xhtml#s280a)

The blockchain is a decentralized network made up of numerous nodes that exchange messages with one another. The blockchain stores state and ledger data, performs transactions, and runs programs known as chaincode. Since transactions are operations performed on the chaincode, the chaincode serves as the focal point. All transactions on the Hyperledger network must be “endorsed,” and only those that have been approved can be carried out and impact the state. System chaincodes are special chaincodes that are used for management functions and parameters. One or more of these code types may exist.

# [Transaction](#toc.xhtml#s281a)

There are two kinds of transactions:

*   **Implement transactions** makes a new chaincode, and pass in a program. Upon successful execution of a deploy transaction, the chaincode is installed “on” the blockchain.
*   Use transactions to carry out a task within the framework of previously implemented chaincode. A chaincode and one of its functionalities are referred to as **invoke transactions**. If the chaincode runs successfully, it performs the designated action, which could include changing the associated state and producing an output.

Invoke transactions are special cases of deploy transactions; an invoke transaction on a system chaincode corresponds to a deploy transaction that generates new chaincode.

# [Data Structures in Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s282a)

An ordered set of back-linked transaction blocks makes up the blockchain data structure. It can be kept in a straightforward database or as a flat file. Every block can be uniquely identified by its hash, which is produced on the block header by the SHA256 cryptographic hash algorithm. Following are the various components of structure:

*   **State**

A ledger state records a set of facts about a particular business object. In the context of blockchain and cryptocurrencies, a “state” is analogous to the contents of a database at one instant in time. For bitcoin, the “state” is the number of bitcoins that exist at that instant and what is necessary to transfer each one of them. For XRP, the “state” is how many XRP exist, which accounts hold them, the security settings of those accounts, what other assets exist, who holds them, what offers have been made to transfer one asset for another, and so on.

A “transition” is a discontinuous change, that is, a change that requires a jump. So, a “state transition” is a jump from one state to another. For bitcoin, a “state transition” occurs each time a block is accepted, causing the ownership of bitcoins to change. For XRP, a “state transition” occurs each time a new ledger is accepted, causing ownership of XRP, account settings, offers on the decentralized exchange, and other kinds of state to change.

One of the most important properties of almost all blockchains is that from minute to minute, every participant in that system can monitor each state transition as it happens and will refuse to accept that transition if it does not follow the system’s rules. So, if you have one bitcoin now, the only way to convince bitcoin participants that you do not have that bitcoin anymore is to show them a transfer that you authorized.

Peers, not customers or orderers, are responsible for maintaining a state. Because only transactions within a specific chaincode are able to edit the keys associated with that code, keys in the KVS can be identified by their name as belonging to a specific chaincode. Any chaincode has the ability to read the keys of other chaincodes in theory.

The most recent state of the blockchain, also referred to as just “state,” is represented as a versioned key-value store (KVS), in which values are random blobs and keys are names. Notably, the science fiction movie The Blob from the 1950s tells the story of an alien creature that falls on Earth and begins to devour everything in its path.

Cryptocurrency is the blob, the event horizon of today, the one-way door that has the potential to completely change the way we work, play, and live. Through put and get KVS-operations, the chaincodes (applications) operating on the blockchain manipulate these entries. Updates to the state are recorded and the state is kept around for future use. It should be noted that versioned KVS is used as the state model; actual KVSs, RDBMSs, or any other solution can be used in an implementation.

*   **Ledger**

The ledger offers a verifiable history of all successful state modifications (here, valid transactions) and unsuccessful state change attempts (here, invalid transactions) that took place while the system was in operation.

Ledger is constructed by the *ordering service* as a *totally ordered hashchain of blocks* of (valid or invalid) transactions. The hashchain imposes the total order of blocks in a ledger and each block contains an array of *totally ordered transactions*, imposing total order across all transactions. Ledger is kept at all peers and, optionally, at a subset of orderers.

*   **Nodes**

Nodes are the communication entities of the blockchain. A “node” is only a logical function in the sense that multiple nodes of different types can run on the same physical server. What counts is how nodes are grouped in “trust domains” and associated to logical entities that control them.

There are three types of nodes:

*   **Client** or **submitting-client**: A client that submits an actual transaction-invocation to the endorsers, and broadcasts transaction-proposals to the ordering service.
*   **Peer**: A node that commits transactions and maintains the state and a copy of the ledger. Besides, peers can have a special endorser role.
*   **Ordering-service-node** or **orderer**: A node running the communication service that implements a delivery guarantee, such as atomic or total order broadcast.

**Client**: The client represents the entity that acts on behalf of *an end-user*. It must connect to a peer for communicating with the blockchain. The client may connect to any peer of its choice. Clients create and thereby invoke transactions. They communicate with both peers and the ordering service.

**Peer**: A peer receives ordered state updates in the form of blocks from the ordering service and maintain the state and the ledger.

Peers can additionally take up a special role of an *endorsing peer*, or an *endorser*. The special function of an endorsing peer occurs with respect to a particular chaincode and consists in endorsing a transaction before it is committed. Every chaincode may specify an *endorsement policy* that may refer to a set of endorsing peers. The policy defines the necessary and sufficient conditions for a valid transaction endorsement (typically a set of endorsers’ signatures). In the special case of deploy transactions that install new chaincode, the (deployment) endorsement policy is specified as an endorsement policy of the system chaincode.

**Ordering-service-nodes (Orderers):**

The **orderers** form the ordering service, that is, a communication fabric that provides delivery guarantees. The ordering service can be implemented in different ways: ranging from a centralized service (example, in development and testing) to distributed protocols that target different networks and node fault models.

Ordering service provides a shared communication channel to *clients* and *peers*, offering a *broadcast service* for messages containing transactions. Clients connect to the channel and may broadcast messages on *the channel* which are then delivered to all peers. The channel supports atomic delivery of all messages, that is, message communication with total-order delivery and (implementation specific) reliability. In other words, the channel outputs the same messages to all connected peers and it outputs them to all peers in the same logical order. This atomic communication guarantee is also called *total-order broadcast, atomic broadcast,* or *consensus* in the context of distributed systems. The communicated messages are the candidate transactions for inclusion in the blockchain state.

# [Partitioning (Ordering Service Channels)](#toc.xhtml#s283a)

Ordering service may support multiple channels similar to the topics of a publish/subscribe (pub/sub) messaging system. Clients can connect to a given channel, send messages, and obtain the messages that arrive. Channels can be thought of as partitions, clients connecting to one channel are unaware of the existence of other channels, but clients may connect to multiple channels. Some ordering service implementations included with Hyperledger Fabric support multiple channels for simplicity of presentation.

**Ordering service API:** Peers connect to the channel provided by the ordering service, via the interface provided by the latter. The ordering service API consists of two basic operations (more generally asynchronous events):

TODO add the part of the API for fetching particular blocks under client/peer specified sequence numbers.

**Ledger and block formation**: The ledger contains all data output by the ordering service. In a nutshell, it is a sequence of deliver (seqno, prevhash, blob) events, which forms a hash chain according to the computation of prevhash described before.

Most of the time, for efficiency reasons, instead of outputting individual transactions (blobs), the ordering service will group (batch) the blobs and output blocks within a single deliver event. In this case, the ordering service must impose and convey a deterministic ordering of the blobs within each block. The number of blobs in a block may be chosen dynamically by an ordering service implementation.

Hyperledger Fabric network has the following components:

*   Assets
*   Shared ledger
*   Smart contract
*   Peer nodes
*   Channel
*   Organizations
*   Membership Services Provider (MSP)
*   Ordering service

Hyperledger incubates and supports a range of blockchain business technologies, frameworks, libraries, and applications. The Hyperledger project hosts several blockchain frameworks, including Hyperledger Fabric.

# [Components of a Hyperledger Fabric Network](#toc.xhtml#s284a)

A Hyperledger Fabric network has these components:

*   **Assets**: An asset is anything that has value. It has state and ownership. Assets are represented in Hyperledger Fabric as a collection of key-value pairs.
*   **Shared Ledger**: The ledger records the state and ownership of an asset. It consists of two components:  
    *   The world state describes the state of the ledger at a given point in time. It is the database of the ledger.
    *   The blockchain is a transaction log history that records all transactions.
*   **Smart Contract**: Hyperledger Fabric smart contracts are called chaincode. Chaincode is a software that defines assets and related transactions; in other words, it contains the business logic of the system. Chaincode is invoked when an application needs to interact with the ledger. It can be written in Golang or Node.js.
*   **Peer Nodes**: Peers are a fundamental element of the network because they host ledgers and smart contracts. A peer executes chaincode, accesses ledger data, endorses transactions, and interfaces with applications. Some peers can be endorsing peers, or endorsers. Every chaincode may specify an endorsement policy, which defines the necessary and sufficient conditions for a valid transaction endorsement.
*   **Channel**: Channels are a logical structure formed by a collection of peers. This capability allows a group of peers to create a separate ledger of transactions.
*   **Organizations**: The Hyperledger Fabric network is built from the peers owned and contributed by the different organizations that are members of the network. The network exists because organizations contribute their individual resources to the collective network. Peers have an identity (digital certificate) assigned by a Membership Service Provider from its owning organization. Peers of different organizations can be on the same channel.
*   **Membership Services Provider (MSP**): The MSP is implemented as a Certificate Authority to manage certificates used to authenticate member identity and roles. No unknown identities can transact in the Hyperledger Fabric network. It manages user IDs and authenticates all participants on the network which enables Hyperledger Fabric as a Private and Permissioned network.
*   **Ordering Service**: Ordering service is an open-source collaborative effort created to advance cross-industry blockchain technologies for business use. This global collaboration is hosted by The Linux Foundation.

The ordering service packages transactions into blocks to be delivered to peers on a channel. It guarantees the transaction delivery in the network. It communicates with peers and endorsing peers. The supported configuration mechanisms for the ordering service are **Solo** and **Kafka**.

*   **Software Development Kit (SDK)**: SDK is a set of tools provided by the manufacturer of (usually) a hardware platform, operating system (OS), or programming language. The Hyperledger Fabric SDK allows applications to interact with a Fabric blockchain network. It provides a simple API to submit transactions to a ledger or query the contents of a ledger with minimal code.

A software development kit (SDK) is a set of tools that provides a developer with the ability to build a custom app which can be added on, or connected to, another program. SDKs allow programmers to develop apps for a specific platform.

The SDK provides a layer of abstraction on top of the wire-level protobuf-based communication protocol used by client applications to interact with a Hyperledger Fabric blockchain network. It allows Java applications to manage the lifecycle of Hyperledger channels and user chaincode. Ideally, an SDK should include libraries, tools, relevant documentation, samples of code and implementations, process explanations and examples, guides for developer usage, limitation definitions, and any other additional offerings that would facilitate building functions that leverage the API.

An API is a set of libraries that make up the core language that you can work with out of the box, whereas an SDK is a development kit that facilitates usages of an API. Conceptually, both are a way for your program to interface with and control the resources provided by another piece of software.

![](images/Figure-8.1.jpg)

**Figure 8.1:** Components of a Hyperledger Fabric network (for simplicity, channels are not shown)

# [Writing a Blockchain Application](#toc.xhtml#s285a)

In a blockchain solution, the Hyperledger Fabric network serves as a back-end with an application front-end to communicate with the network[^(\[2\])](#c08.xhtml#ftn2). SDKs help you set up the communication between front and back end, such as the Node.js SDK and Java SDK. The SDK provides a way to execute user chaincode, perform transactions in the network, monitor events, and more.

To write a blockchain application, you need to:

*   Write a chaincode in a supported programming language like Go.
*   Deploy a chaincode on Hyperledger Fabric network.
*   Develop a client application using an SDK.
*   Know how to execute a blockchain transaction.

The high-level request flow of a transaction in a Hyperledger Fabric network flows in the following manner:

1.  The client connects to a Hyperledger Fabric network using the Node.js or Java SDK. Using the SDK API, the client creates a transaction and sends it to the endorsing peer.
2.  The endorsing peer verifies the client’s signature, simulates a transaction, and sends an endorsement signature.
3.  If the transaction is endorsed, the client submits the transaction to the ordering service. Otherwise, the transaction is cancelled.
4.  The ordering service delivers a transaction to the peers. All peers commit and apply the same sequence of transactions and update their state.

# [Identity](#toc.xhtml#s286a)

Identity is an important feature of permissioned blockchain platform. In Hyperledger Fabric, each component needs to be identified before accessing the fabric network. The identity is represented by an X.509 digital certificate. Certificates are managed by way of Certification Authorities (or CA). Fabric CA is the Hyperledger’s implementation of the Certification Authority.

For an identity to be verifiable, it must come from a trusted authority.

When interacting with a fabric network, the actor specifies and presents the digital certificate, and the fabric network accepts or denies, based on the policy.

The different actors in a blockchain network include peers, orderers, client applications, administrators and more[^(\[3\])](#c08.xhtml#ftn3). Each of these actors — active elements inside or outside a network are able to consume services — has a digital identity encapsulated in an X.509 digital certificate. These identities really matter because they determine the exact permissions over resources and access to information that actors have in a blockchain network.

A digital identity, furthermore, has some additional attributes that Fabric uses to determine permissions. It gives the union of an identity and the associated attributes a special name known as **principal.** Principals are just like `userIDs` or `groupIDs`*,* but a little more flexible because they can include a wide range of properties of an actor’s identity, such as the actor’s organization, organizational unit, role, or even the actor’s specific identity. When we talk about principals, they are the properties which determine their permissions.

For an identity to be verifiable, it must come from a trusted authority. A membership service provider (MSP) is how this is achieved in Fabric. More specifically, an MSP is a component that defines the rules that govern the valid identities for this organization. The default MSP implementation in Fabric uses X.509 certificates as identities, adopting a traditional Public Key Infrastructure (PKI) hierarchical model (more on PKI is discussed later).

# [A Simple Scenario to Explain the Use of an Identity](#toc.xhtml#s287a)

Imagine that you visit a supermarket to buy some groceries. At the checkout, you see a sign that says only Visa, Mastercard and AMEX cards are accepted. If you try to pay with a different card — let us call it an “ImagineCard” — it does not matter whether the card is authentic and you have sufficient funds in your account. It will be not be accepted.

![](images/Figure-8.2.jpg)

**Figure 8.2:** Use of identity

Having a valid credit card is not enough — it must also be accepted by the store. PKIs and MSPs work together in the same way — a PKI provides a list of identities, and an MSP says which of these are members of a given organization that participates in the network.

PKI certificate authorities and MSPs provide a similar combination of functionalities. A PKI is like a card provider — it dispenses many different types of verifiable identities. An MSP, on the other hand, is like the list of card providers accepted by the store, determining which identities are the trusted members (actors) of the store payment network. MSPs turn verifiable identities into the members of a blockchain network.

# [A Public Key Infrastructure (PKI)](#toc.xhtml#s288a)

PKI is a collection of internet technologies that provides secure communications in a network. It is a PKI that places the S in HTTPS — and if you are reading this documentation on a web browser, you are probably using a PKI to make sure it comes from a verified source.

![](images/Figure-8.3.jpg)

**Figure 8.3:** Use of PKI Certification Process: The elements of PKI

A PKI is comprised of Certificate Authorities who issue digital certificates to parties (example, users of a service, service provider), who then use them to authenticate themselves in the messages they exchange with their environment. A CA’s Certificate Revocation List (CRL) constitutes a reference for the certificates that are no longer valid. Revocation of a certificate can happen for a number of reasons. For example, a certificate may be revoked because the cryptographic private material associated to the certificate has exposed.

Although a blockchain network is more than a communications network, it relies on the PKI standard to ensure secure communication between various network participants. It also ensures that messages posted on the blockchain are properly authenticated. It is therefore important to understand the basics of PKI and why MSPs are so important.

There are four key elements to PKI:

*   Digital Certificates
*   Public and Private Keys
*   Certificate Authorities
*   Certificate Revocation Lists

# [Digital Certificates](#toc.xhtml#s289a)

A digital certificate is a document which holds a set of attributes relating to the holder of the certificate. The most common type of certificate is the one compliant with the X.509 standard, which allows the encoding of a party’s identifying details in its structure. Digital Signature Certificates (DSC) are the digital equivalent (that is electronic format) of physical or paper certificates. Few Examples of physical certificates are drivers’ licenses, passports, or membership cards. Certificates serve as proof of identity of an individual for a certain purpose. For example, a driver’s license identifies someone who can legally drive in a particular country. Likewise, a digital certificate can be presented electronically to prove one’s identity, to access information or services on the Internet or to sign certain documents digitally.

The process of obtaining DSC from Certifying Authority includes the following:

*   Digital Signature Certificate (DSC) applicants can directly approach Certifying Authorities (CAs) with original supporting documents. Self-attested copies are sufficient in this case.
*   DSCs can also be obtained, wherever offered by CA, using Aadhar eKYC-based authentication. Supporting documents are not required in this case.

**Certification Agencies:**

Certification Agencies are appointed by the office of the Controller of Certification Agencies (CCA) under the provisions of IT Act, 2000. There are a total of eight Certification Agencies authorized by the CCA to issue Digital Signature Certificates (DSCs).

**Certifying Authorities:**

Certifying Authorities (CA) has been granted a license to issue a digital signature certificate under Section 24 of the Indian IT-Act 2000. One can procure Class 2 or 3 certificates from any of the certifying authorities. Some of them are :

*   National Informatics Center (NIC)
*   IDRBT Certifying Authority

# [Policies in Hyperledger Fabric](#toc.xhtml#s290a)

The Configuration for a Hyperledger Fabric blockchain network is managed by policies. These policies generally reside in the channel configuration. The primary purpose of this document is to explain how policies are defined in and interact with the channel configuration. However, policies may also be specified in some other places, such as chaincodes. So, this information may be of interest outside the scope of channel configuration.

# [Understanding Policy](#toc.xhtml#s291a)

A policy, at its most basic level, is a set of rules that define the structure for how decisions are made and specific outcomes are reached. To that end, policies typically describe a who and a what, such as the access or rights that an individual has over an asset. We can see that policies are used throughout our daily lives to protect assets of value, from car rentals, health, our homes, and many more.

For example, an insurance policy defines the conditions, terms, limits, and expiration under which an insurance payout will be made. The policy is agreed to by the policy holder and the insurance company, and defines the rights and responsibilities of each party.

In Hyperledger Fabric, policies are the mechanism for infrastructure management as compared to an insurance policy that is put in place for risk management. In Fabric, policies represent how members come to an agreement on accepting or rejecting changes to the network, a channel, or a smart contract. Policies are agreed to by the consortium members when a network is originally configured, but they can also be modified as the network evolves. For example, they describe the criteria for adding or removing members from a channel, change how blocks are formed, or specify the number of organizations required to endorse a smart contract. These actions are described by a policy which defines who can perform the action. To sum up, everything you want to do on a Fabric network is controlled by a policy.

At its most basic level, a policy is a function which accepts an input as a set of signed data and evaluates successfully, or returns an error because some aspect of the signed data did not satisfy the policy.

More precisely, policies test whether the signer or signers of some data meet some condition required for those signatures to be considered ‘valid’. This is useful for determining that the correct parties have agreed to a transaction, or change.

For example, in Hyperledger Fabric, a policy may define any of the following:

*   Administrators from 2 out of 5 possible different organizations must sign.
*   Any member from any organization must sign.
*   Two specific certificates must be signed by both.

These policy rules are only examples, and other more powerful rules can be constructed.

# [Need of Policies in Hyperledger Fabric](#toc.xhtml#s292a)

It is the policies that makes Hyperledger Fabric different from other blockchains such as Ethereum or Bitcoin. In those systems, transactions can be generated and validated by any node in the network. The policies that govern the network are fixed at any point in time and can only be changed using the same process that governs the code. Because Fabric is a permissioned blockchain whose users are recognized by the underlying infrastructure, those users have the ability to decide on the governance of the network before it is launched, and can change the governance of a running network.

The contents of policies allow members to decide which organizations can access or update a Fabric network, and provide the mechanism to enforce those decisions. Policies contain the lists of organizations that have access to a given resource, such as a user or system chaincode. They also specify how many organizations need to agree on a proposal to update a resource, such as a channel or smart contracts. Once they are written, policies evaluate the collection of signatures attached to transactions and proposals, and validate if the signatures fulfill the governance agreed to by the network.

# [Implementation of Policies Throughout Fabric](#toc.xhtml#s293a)

In Fabric, policies are implemented at different levels in a network. Each policy domain governs different aspects of how a network operates.

![](images/Figure-8.4.jpg)

**Figure 8.4:** Visual representation of Fabric policy hierarchy

Every fabric network begins with an ordering system channel. There must be exactly one ordering system channel for an ordering service, and it is the first channel to be created. The system channel also contains the organizations who are the members of the ordering service (ordering organizations) and those that are on the networks to transact (consortium organizations).

The policies existing in the ordering system channel configuration blocks govern the consensus used by the ordering service and define how new blocks are created. The system channel also governs which members of the consortium are allowed to create new channels.

# [Application Channel Configuration](#toc.xhtml#s294a)

It is the application channels that are used to provide a private communication mechanism between organizations in the consortium.

The ability to add or remove members from the channel is governed by the policies in an application channel. Application channels also govern which organizations are required to approve a chaincode before the chaincode is defined and committed to a channel using the Fabric chaincode lifecycle. When an application channel is initially created, it inherits all the ordering service parameters from the orderer system channel by default. However, those parameters (and the policies governing them) can be customized in each channel.

# [Access Control Lists (ACLs)](#toc.xhtml#s295a)

An access control list (ACL) is a list of rules that specifies which users or systems are granted or denied access to a particular object or system resource. Network administrators will be especially interested in the Fabric use of ACLs, which provide the ability to configure access to resources by associating those resources with existing policies. These “resources” could be functions on system chaincode (example, `"GetBlockByNumber"` on the `"qscc"` system chaincode) or other resources (example, who can receive Block events). ACLs refer to policies defined in an application channel configuration and extends them to control additional resources.

# [Policy Types](#toc.xhtml#s296a)

There are presently three different types of policies implemented:

*   **Signature Policy**: It is the most powerful and specifies the policy as a combination of evaluation rules for MSP principals. It supports arbitrary combinations of *AND, OR,* and *NOutOf*, allowing the construction of extremely powerful rules such as, “An admin of org A and 2 other admins, or 11 of 20 org admins.”
*   **Implicit Meta Policy**: Less flexible type than Signature Policy, and is only valid in the context of configuration. It aggregates the result of evaluating policies deeper in the configuration hierarchy, which are ultimately defined by Signature Policies. It supports good default rules such as, “A majority of the organization admin policies.”
*   **Endorsement Policy**: Hyperledger Fabric works as an instruction for endorsing peers to validate whether a transaction is properly endorsed or not. In Hyperledger Fabric, there is a Valid System Chaincode (VSCC) invoked with every chaincode’s transaction on each peer after it receives a transaction proposal. Its purpose is to compare the endorsement signatures with which transaction is signed off, as a transaction contains one or more endorsements from many endorsing peers.

# [Valid System Chaincode (VSCC)](#toc.xhtml#s297a)

A Validation system chaincode evaluates endorsements in the transaction against the endorsement policy specified for the chaincode. VSCC had been incorporated to determine:

*   All transactions endorsements are valid (that is, they contain valid signatures for valid certificates from authorized source).
*   The number of transaction endorsements matches the requirement of endorsements declared in the endorsement policy.
*   Endorsement is from an authorized expected source.

Roles allowed in the endorsement policy to be checked by VSCC:

*   Peer
*   Client
*   Member
*   Admin

***NOTE**: These roles must only be defined as strings at the setEnrollment API after the registration of the user on the blockchain through the CA following any SDK implementation*.

VSCC checks the role of the signing identity against the endorsement policy defined. This role must be one of the aforementioned roles for succeeding the approval of endorsement policies, otherwise the “ENDORSEMENT\_POLICY\_FAILURE” error will be thrown at the endorsing peer where the signature set does not satisfy the endorsement policy or the number of signatures required by the policy cannot be collected.

Let us understand the format of the endorsement policy to be defined for the Node SDK-based client for communicating with the Blockchain.

# [Access Control](#toc.xhtml#s298a)

Access Control is a device to limit the access others have to your code. Same package can access each other’s variables and methods, except for private members. Outside package can access public classes. Next, can access members that are public.

It is obvious that Fabric uses access control lists (ACLs) to manage access to resources by associating a policy with a resource. Fabric contains a number of default ACLs.

# [Attribute-Based Access Control (ABAC) in Hyperledger Fabric](#toc.xhtml#s299a)

ABAC is used for restricting access to the specific user having the necessary attributes in their certificate. First, let us talk about certification creation with attributes.

Generally, the process of creating a certificate are:

1.  Enrollment
2.  Register

The Enrollment is the process by which a user requests and obtains a digital certificate from a given CA. Registration is usually done by a Registrar, telling a *CA* to issue the digital certificate. An *admin* (registrar) is enrolled in the *CA*. Then, the admin receives the signing key and certificate for this admin.

First, the admin registers *user1* into the *CA* with proper information. The *CA* returns with a secret. This secret is then used to enroll *user1* to the *CA*. The result is the signing key and certificate for *user1.*

All the platforms such as Hyperledger Fabric blockchain and IoT devices demonstrate the access control and establish the root of trust for IoT devices. As such, The Hyperledger Fabric is designed to be secure against unwanted access and use through encryption protocols, access restrictions, and cryptography algorithms.

Access control systems exist to protect resources from unauthorized access[^(\[4\])](#c08.xhtml#ftn4). The Auditability feature plays an important role and has its importance increased when it comes to public and private administration, and can be leveraged within a blockchain-based access control system. Hyperledger Fabric will leverage a solution that aims to discourage unauthorized accesses to confidential information while decentralizing trust when it comes to access control. This work action will not only be focused on the implementation of such Proof-of-Concept but also leverages effective techniques to its provisioning and deployment.

It is quiet challenging to build such a system based on the blockchain technology as such distributed ledger technologies are indeed a complex distributed system. Concerning blockchain access control, there are two main challenges:

*   Provisioning of features for creating scenarios with requirements for permissions, data privacy, and security.
*   The lack of standardized processes for development and operations, that is, continuous integration, continuous deployment, and continuous improvement.

The internship aims to:

*   Contribute to the open-source community, by advancing the state-of-the-art on the access control using the blockchain.
*   Create a blockchain-based access control system using Hyperledger Fabric.
*   Development of an efficient, practical DevOps framework to provision and deploy the created system.
*   Propose alterations to Fabric in order to facilitate the creation of such system (that is, creating a tutorial or scripts).
*   If time suffices, develop a small web application to help visualize the solution.

# [Membership Service Provider (MSP)](#toc.xhtml#s300a)

Membership Service Provider (MSP) is a Hyperledger Fabric component that offers an abstraction of membership operations. In particular, an MSP abstracts away all cryptographic mechanisms and protocols behind, issuing certificates, validating certificates, and user authentication.

Needless to say, a membership service provider (MSP) is a trusted authority in Fabric.

An MSP may define its own notion of identity and rules by which those identities are governed (identity validation) and authenticated (signature generation and verification).

One or more MSPs may govern a Hyperledger Fabric blockchain network. This provides modularity of membership operations and interoperability across different membership standards and architectures.

# [Channels in Hyperledger System](#toc.xhtml#s301a)

A channel is a private communication pathway between two or more members of a Hyperledger Fabric network on Amazon Managed Blockchain, in which each transaction on a Hyperledger Fabric network occurs on a channel. All channels in Hyperledger Fabric are the subnet of the main blockchain. Also, a network must contain at least one channel and a network on Managed Blockchain can have up to eight channels.

Peers subscribe to the ordering service of a channel and then receive the channel related blocks from it as they are created. In this procedure:

*   Channeling means like splitting the Blockchain (partitioning) and therefore you should imagine that each channel is like an independent chain of blocks with its own incrementing integer sequence number (`seqno`).
*   Each transaction broadcast must be done within the scope of a specific Channel (with, a default public Channel).
*   Channel is used to create sub network (Private). This network has a number of peers (member). The purpose of this channel is to prepare private network and perform confidential transactions.
*   Each transaction of the network is executed on the Channel.
*   Each channel has their separate ledger which are stored in each peer on the Channel.
*   Channels are seen as independent chains that add privacy and a transaction is done on a specific channel.

# [Multiple Channels](#toc.xhtml#s302a)

It is a built-in feature of fabric that is capable of more than just having multiple channels on one peer. Within a chaincode, users are able to call other chaincodes. If the peer is a part of the channel and the chaincode is installed on the peer, it is possible to call chaincodes from others. But on the other channel, no transaction is added to the ledger, so you are only able to make queries (reads) in the other chaincode.

We have learnt just now that Channels are like partitions. User can create a channel and invite peers to join the channel. As such, users are defining which members can have access to the channel. Channels adds in privacy as clients connecting to one channel are unaware of the existence of other channels.

In regards of privacy, we can establish a separate channel per confidential domain where only permitted peers may subscribe to. Transactions sent to a channel will be ordered respective to each other within the channel. So, a batch only contains transactions from the channel and not from any other channels.

Batch means a block. So yes, each Channel is an independent chain of transaction block containing only transaction for this particular Channel. So, with Fabric, you can run multiple Blockchains within the same infrastructure (Ordering service, Membership service, and so on).

# [Transaction Validation](#toc.xhtml#s303a)

During the simulation of a transaction by an endorser, a read-write set is prepared for the transaction[^(\[5\])](#c08.xhtml#ftn5). The read set contains a list of unique keys and their committed versions that the transaction reads during simulation. The write set contains a list of unique keys and their new values that the transaction writes, though there can be an overlap with the keys present in the read set. A delete marker is set in the place of a new value for the key if the update performed by the transaction is to delete the key. If the transaction writes a value multiple times for a key, only the last written value is retained. Also, if a transaction reads a value for a key, the value in the committed state is returned even if the transaction has updated the value for the key before issuing the read. In other words, read-your-writes semantics are not supported. The versions of the keys are recorded only in the read set; the write set just contains the list of unique keys and their latest values set by the transaction.

There could be various schemes for implementing versions. The minimal requirement for a versioning scheme is to produce non-repeating identifiers for a given key. For instance, using increasing monotonically the numbers for versions can be one such scheme. In the current implementation, we use a blockchain height-based versioning scheme in which the height of the committing transaction is used as the latest version for all the keys modified by the transaction. In this scheme, the height of a transaction is represented by a tuple, `txNumber`, which is the height of the transaction within the block.

The following is an illustration of a sample read-write set prepared by simulating a hypothetical transaction. In the following example, the use of incremental numbers represents the versions.

`<TxReadWriteSet>`

`<NsReadWriteSet name="chaincode1">`

`<read-set>`

`<read key="K1", version="1">`

`<read key="K2", version="1">`

`</read-set>`

`<write-set>`

`<write key="K1", value="V1"`

`<write key="K3", value="V2"`

`<write key="K4", isDelete="true"`

`</write-set>`

`</NsReadWriteSet>`

`<TxReadWriteSet>`

Furthermore, in the event that the transaction executes a range query while in simulation, both the range query and its outcomes will be appended to the read-write set as query data.

# [Read-Write Set Transaction Validation](#toc.xhtml#s304a)

In reality, every committer utilizes the write set and read set, of the read-write set, to change the versions and values of the impacted keys, and to verify the legitimacy of a transaction, respectively. Assuming that all previous valid transactions, including those in the same block, are committed, a transaction is deemed valid during the validation phase if the version of each key contained in the transaction’s read set matches the version for the same key in the world state. If one or more query pieces are included in the read-write set, then an extra validation is carried out.

Making sure that no key has been added, removed, or altered in the super range—that is, the union of the ranges of results recorded in the query information—should be the goal of the additional validation step. Stated differently, rerunning any of the range queries that the transaction executed during the simulation to confirm the committed state ought to produce the same outcomes that the transaction saw at the time of simulation. This check makes sure that a transaction should be flagged as invalid if it detects phantom objects during commit. An individual object that can exist anywhere is called a phantom and commit denotes the purposeful execution of any action. The `GetStateByRange` function in the chaincode, which is not yet implemented for other queries, is the only query type for which this phantom protection is applicable. Other queries should only be used in read-only transactions that are not ordered since they run the danger of phantoms, unless the application can ensure that the result set remains stable between simulation and validation/commit time.

When a transaction passes the validity check, the committer updates the world state using the write set. During the update phase, the value for each key in the write set is updated to the value for the corresponding key in the world state. In addition, the most recent version of the key is reflected in the world state by changing its version.

# [Flow of Transactions](#toc.xhtml#s305a)

The transactional processes involved in a typical asset exchange are described in the Transaction Flow paper[^(\[6\])](#c08.xhtml#ftn6). Two clients, A and B, who are purchasing and selling radishes, are involved in the situation. They communicate with the ledger and send transactions to each other’s peers on the network.

**Presumptions:**

A channel that is established and operational is assumed by the transaction flow. After registering and enrolling with the organization’s Certificate Authority (CA), the application user receives the required cryptographic material in return, which is utilized for network authentication. Thus, the chaincode is published to the channel and placed on the peers. It contains a series of key value pairs that reflect the starting state of the radish market. The logic that defines a set of transaction instructions and the agreed-upon price for a radish is contained in the chaincode. For this chaincode, an endorsement policy has also been established, which stipulates that peerA and peerB must approve of every transaction.

![](images/Figure-8.5.jpg)

**Figure 8.5:** Client A initiates a transaction

Client A is sending a request to purchase radishes. This request targets peerA and peerB, who are respectively representative of Client A and Client B. The endorsement policy states that both peers must endorse any transaction, therefore the request goes to peerA and peerB.

Next, the transaction proposal is constructed. An application leveraging a supported SDK (*Node, Java, Python*) utilizes one of the available APIs to generate a transaction proposal. The proposal is a request to invoke a chaincode function with certain input parameters, with the intent of reading and/or updating the ledger.

The Software Development Kit (SDK) serves as a shim to package the transaction proposal into the properly architected format (protocol buffer over gRPC) and takes the user’s cryptographic credentials to produce a unique signature for this transaction proposal. In the context of Hyperledger Fabric, Shim provides APIs for the chaincode to access its state variables, transaction context, and call other chaincodes. Shim contains the generic means for chaincode and validating peers to communicate with each other.

![](images/Figure-8.6.jpg)

**Figure 8.6:** Endorsing peers, verify signature, and executing the transaction

The endorsing peers verify:

*   that the transaction proposal is well formed.
*   whether it has not been submitted already in the past (replay-attack protection).
*   the signature is valid (using the MSP).
*   that the submitter (Client A, in the example) is properly authorized to perform the proposed operation on that channel (namely, each endorsing peer ensures that the submitter satisfies the channel’s Writers policy).

The endorsing peers take the transaction proposal inputs as arguments to the invoked chaincode’s function. The chaincode is then executed against the current state database to produce transaction results including a response value, read set, and write set (that is, key/value pairs representing an asset to create or update). At this point, no updates are made to the ledger. The set of these values, along with the endorsing peer’s signature is passed back as a “proposal response” to the SDK which parses the payload for the application to consume.

# [Executing Contracts in Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s306a)

Computer scientist *Nick Szabo* developed the concept of smart contracts in 1994, where he discussed the method of how contracts could be embedded into computer codes[^(\[7\])](#c08.xhtml#ftn7). The emergence of blockchain technology, and especially the launch of Ethereum, has made this an even more vulnerable possibility. This method ensures security and transparency of all records posted on network.

All stakeholders admired the efficiency of sharing and accessing smart contracts. In fact, smart contracts can *automate, calculate* and *arrange payments*, and then automatically carry out their terms and conditions. When obligations are met, the contract can be executed instantly, *with benefit of time saving, removing intermediaries, and allowing for multi-party consensus-based validation.*

It is well known that the first well known application of blockchain technology was Bitcoin, *a peer-to-peer* system custom-designed to make value transfer function without relying on a central party. It worked really well and is still working efficiently today, arguably becoming the most robust deployment of blockchain applications. But, one of the innovative blockchain technology smart contracts further elaborated the crypto revolution. There are many business advantages if you choose using smart contracts, including *trust, transparency, security, autonomy, and accuracy.*

The take-away concept as such is that the term **smart contract** refers to a piece of computer program that gets executed by a quorum of blockchain nodes independently in order to record the latest program state.

# [Basic Definition of Smart Contracts](#toc.xhtml#s307a)

A smart contract is defined as an agreement between two people in the form of a computer code. They run on the blockchain (apart from several other formats), so they are stored on a public database and cannot be changed. The transactions that happen in a smart contract are processed by the blockchain, which means they can be sent automatically without intervention from a third party (such as central or federal governments, reserve bank, and so on).

Remarkably, smart contracts are carried out by blockchain nodes as a consequence of handling user-submitted transactions. A blockchain transaction consists of the following:

*   A target smart contract function that is specified.
*   A payload that comprises function call input values.
*   Always bears the submitter’s signature. Any node in the blockchain network can receive a transaction, which is then broadcasted to all the other nodes in the network for visibility. Every node uses the executable program in the target smart contract at some point to complete the transaction. The blockchain’s internal state will be updated if the transaction is successfully executed. In the event that a smart contract determines that the input is invalid and rejects the transaction as unsuccessful, the state remains unaltered.

A majority of blockchain nodes must independently carry out smart contracts. Blockchains have no authority and are decentralized, in contrast to traditional databases. Every node in the network believes that other nodes could be evil, and it never trusts the states that other nodes are maintaining. Rather, by carrying out the transactions using the smart contract code, each node keeps up its own state database.

How is the consistency of state across all nodes ensured by this autonomous execution method in each node? Every node needs to have the same initial state, identical input values, identical execution logic, and both. Applications for smart contracts include lending, borrowing, trading, and investing in the financial sector. They can be applied to real estate, gaming, and healthcare. They can even be utilized to set up whole company hierarchies.

Smart contracts are executable programs[^(\[6\])](#c08.xhtml#ftn6). To represent arbitrary business logic or preset conditions to initiate value transfer, they are typically written in high-level computer programming languages. For a smart contracts engine to be effective in supporting a broad range of use cases, the language must be Turing complete, a fancy word in computer science that means the language can be written to handle any computation issue. This is the reason why Bitcoin is not regarded as having smart contracts, even though it is feasible to program in the Bitcoin scripting language due to its extremely limited functionality. When Ethereum first made smart contract support available, it ushered in a period of quick inventions to address some of the most difficult issues facing both society and technology.

According to IBM, smart contracts are essentially blockchain-based algorithms that execute when specific criteria are satisfied. Generally, they are employed to automate the implementation of an agreement so that there is no need for an intermediary and no delay for any parties involved in knowing the outcome right away.

The final state will always be the same if all three components are the same. One of the most important factors in generating consensus among the blockchain nodes is the chain of blocks with the linked hashes reflecting each full list of transactions (input values) and current states (starting state). Lastly, distinct blockchain protocols use various methods to ensure that the transaction is processed using the same smart contract code. When it comes to the cryptocurrency Ethereum, the smart contract code is kept directly as state on the chain. Contract code is kept off-chain in Hyperledger Fabric and Corda, the other two popular commercial blockchain systems; the intended version of the smart contract is represented by an on-chain hash.

Maintaining program states is smart contracts’ primary purpose. State is an arbitrary data point that is updated by transaction execution. A blockchain can be thought of as a database in this sense, despite the fact that its architecture places a strong emphasis on data consistency and immutability at the expense of query support and performance. Nearly every blockchain protocol is created with a state transfer conceptual model in mind.

Each smart contract therefore keeps its own set of states. Except for pure value transfers that do not require smart contracts, the majority of transactions submitted to a blockchain are intended to trigger a smart contract. The destination smart contract modifies its state following the execution of a transaction. Additionally, a smart contract has the ability to call another smart contract to update or query the state of the downstream contract.

# [EOSIO Blockchain](#toc.xhtml#s308a)

The EOSIO blockchain is designed to emulate the performance of a real computer, and the software itself uses some familiar computing concepts in its operations. There are three types of resources that power the EOSIO blockchain. EOSIO acronym for Electro-Optical System Input Output, is a smart contract platform that eliminates transaction fees and also executes millions of transactions per second.

An agreement that is modeled, signed, and completed electronically—typically via the internet—is referred to as an online contract or an electronic contract.

An online contract is written in the same way as a typical paper-based contract, using very comparable concepts. In the event of an online contract, the vendor who wants to sell their goods must show the potential customers their goods, prices, and terms of purchase. The purchasers who are interested in purchasing the goods can then choose to sign electronically, or think about using the “I Agree” or “Click to Agree” option to indicate their acceptance of the terms as stated by the vendor.

In reality, there are several methods for executing electronic signatures, such as entering the signer’s name in the designated signature field, copying and pasting the scanned copy of the signature, or selecting the appropriate choice. The transaction can be finished once the terms are agreed upon and the money is received. Essentially, servers provide communication between two computers.

In an e-contract, the offer, invitation to offer, counter offer or acceptance, and so on are all by electronic means. This form of electronic communication leads to an agreement contract being drafted.

A **bilateral contract** is a binding agreement between two parties where both exchange promises to perform and fulfill one side of a bargain.

***Note**: Anyone can write a smart contract and deploy it to the network. You just need to learn how to code it in a smart contract language, and have enough ETH (Ethereum**)*** *to deploy your contract.*

In a nutshell, the smart contracts are *digital agreements* that automatically execute transactions between parties, increasing speed, accuracy, and integrity in payment and performance. It is noteworthy that Smart contracts are legally enforceable if they comply with contract law.

A smart contract may also contain as many clauses as necessary to reassure the parties involved that the work will be finished to their satisfaction. Participants must agree on the “if/when…then…” rules that govern those transactions, investigate any potential exceptions, design a framework for resolving disputes, and decide how transactions and their data are represented on the blockchain in order to construct the terms.

In these situations, a developer can create the smart contract. However, an increasing number of businesses using blockchain technology offer templates, web interfaces, and other online tools to make the process of creating smart contracts easier.

# [Advantages of Intelligent Contracts](#toc.xhtml#s309a)

The advantages of smart contracts are as follows:

*   **Accuracy, speed, and efficiency**: The contract is carried out right away if a condition is satisfied. Because smart contracts are digital and automated, there is no paperwork to handle in this case, and there is also no time wasted on reconciling errors that can arise from manually filling out documentation.
*   **Trust and transparency**: There is no need to wonder if information has been changed for personal gain because there is no involvement from a third party and participants exchange encrypted records of transactions.
*   **Security**: It is impossible to hack because blockchain transaction records are encrypted. Furthermore, in order for a hacker to modify a single record on a distributed ledger, they would need to modify the entire chain as every record is linked to the records that come before and after it.
*   **Savings**: Because smart contracts handle transactions without the need for middlemen, the corresponding costs and delays, savings are achievable.

# [Smart Contract Anatomy](#toc.xhtml#s310a)

Consider smart contracts as program functions, with inputs, outputs, and logic to process the inputs. This will help you grasp how smart contracts work. Smart contract execution frequently produces updated states. The validity of a transaction is verified by the logic embedded in smart contracts. Not operating at the correct level of the initial state, such as trying to spend tokens without having enough balance, is one example of an invalid transaction. Updated states are only the product of valid transactions. Depending on the specific blockchain design, invalid transactions are either rejected by the network and not added to the blockchain or added but recorded as unsuccessful.

Smart contracts may potentially disseminate occurrences as a means of informing the public. When the transaction-containing block is committed to the node’s blockchain, event listeners are alerted.

![](images/Figure-8.7.jpg)

**Figure 8.7:** Anatomy of Smart Contract in Blockchain Technology

A transaction has the ability to call several public functions. Every function has the option to return the most recent state or to update the state itself. For instance, the following features are usually present in a conventional fungible token smart contract and are helpful in situations like commodity trade and payments.

States-updating functions (that call for transactions):

*   transfer (amount, to)
*   Give approval (assign, sum)
*   conveyanceFrom (to, amount, from)
*   mint (quantity, to)
*   burn (amount, from)

Functions that check the most recent states (that do not need transactions to use):

*   accountOf’s balance

If the state needs to be changed at any point, a transaction must be created and the entire transaction cycle must be followed. Because blockchain design is decentralized, transactions need to be handled by a consensus mechanism in order to guarantee that all copies kept by the nodes that participate in the blockchain network have the same records.

![](images/Figure-8.8.jpg)

**Figure 8.8:** Updating state required transaction processing with consensus from the whole network

On the other hand, querying for the latest state without updating it can be accomplished much faster and involves only a single node in the network. Any node that has the smart contract installed locally can execute the query request and return the result by reading from the locally maintained state database.

![](images/Figure-8.9.jpg)

**Figure 8.9:** Querying smart contracts for latest state can be accomplished with a single node

# [Smart Contract’s Life Cycle](#toc.xhtml#s311a)

It is necessary to deploy the smart contract on the blockchain before it can handle transactions. Ensuring that every blockchain node has the exact same code is a requirement of the deploy procedure. This requirement is handled differently by different blockchain designs. But, there are generally two ways to handle it: either store the contract code itself in the blockchain, which ensures global consensus (on-chain); or let each node owner determine whether the node should have the code installed locally and use an off-chain reference for code integrity validation (a hash-based commitment in the blockchain).

![](images/Figure-8.10.jpg)

**Figure 8.10:** Life cycle of smart contract

# [Use Cases of Smart Contracts](#toc.xhtml#s312a)

Numerous industries, including finance, real estate, healthcare, insurance, and even elections, are already undergoing a transition brought about by smart contracts. Here are a few instances of smart contracts[^(\[8\])](#c08.xhtml#ftn8):

A wide range of sectors can employ smart contracts to carry out tasks[^(\[9\])](#c08.xhtml#ftn9). Regulatory compliance, enforceability of contracts, international financial transactions, ownership of real estate, purchasing a home, supply management, material provenance, document management, and numerous other uses. Smart contracts are important in a number of domains, including the legal transfer of ownership in the sale of digital financial assets, banking and credit services, logistics, tracing the origin and route of commodities, decentralized storage, and the usage of renewable energy.

*   **Supply Chain Management**: This is a field that could benefit from the application of smart contracts. The use of smart contracts to increase supply chain transparency is facilitating the flow of commodities and reestablishing trade confidence. As products pass through the supply chain, smart contracts may track ownership rights and verify who is in charge of the goods at any given moment. Up until it is delivered to the customer, the completed product can be checked at every stage of the procedure.
*   **Insurance**: The insurance industry, which now lacks automated administration, could potentially benefit from the usage of smart contracts. The processing and payment of an insurance claim can take several months. When specific circumstances take place, smart contracts can automatically trigger a claim, which helps simplify and streamline the process. Thus, precise information might be documented on the blockchain to ascertain the precise amount of remuneration.
*   **Mortgage loans**: The mortgage application process might be made simpler by smart contracts. Here, the conditions of a mortgage contract are determined by evaluating the income, expenses, credit score, and other factors of the mortgagee. For both the lender and the mortgagee, the requirement to conduct these checks—often through third parties—can make the procedure drawn out and challenging. Eliminating intermediaries allowed parties to transact with one another directly.
*   **Financial industry**: As money and related documentation move toward electronic forms, this sector continues to employ smart contracts extensively. The financial services industry presents various potential for the use of smart contracts, such as efficient “know your customer” certification, trade finance, payment processing, and financial instrument clearing and settlement.
*   **Cutting down on expensive mistakes in trade finance**: Because smart contracts can streamline processes and improve computations, they can also cut down on labor hours. Smart contracts will enable significant adjustments to the financial service sector’s infrastructure and procedures. Distributed ledgers, in their opinion, will emerge as the go-to option for handling large numbers of transactions. By 2022, these savings totaled between $15 and $20 billion annually.
*   **Tracking property data in real estate**: By eliminating the need for attorneys or brokers, smart contracts in this sector are utilized to record property ownership of any structure and maximize transaction speed and efficiency. Sellers have the option to direct the procedure. Rob Massey, a prominent figure in the blockchain space, claims that smart contracts make property title management more affordable and transparent. Legal fees may arise from transfers that are impeded by title flaws. Smart contracts, on the other hand, maintain track of a property’s history, location, and all other crucial information required for title evaluation. Through safe, tamper-proof encrypted codes, they aid in preventing fraud.
*   **Safeguarding private health information**: Blockchain is being used by more healthcare organizations to offer dependable automation and modern security features. Because they have so much sensitive data, hospitals are the main targets of cybercriminals. About 4.5 million patient records have been stolen in data breaches that have affected even well-known companies in the field, such as UCLA Health. Ensuring the safe exchange of patient data among healthcare providers is imperative in this context to uphold care standards. By using smart contracts, patient data can be safely kept on a blockchain, accessible only with the patient’s private key. Patients can guarantee that their information is protected and that their medical professionals always have access to the information they require in this way.
*   **Increasing election convenience and integrity**: Blockchain voting technologies may be the way of future elections. Blockchain voting systems have the potential to increase accessibility, boost voter turnout, and expedite the tabulation and reporting of votes by enabling secure and simple voting over digital channels. Election hackers frequently want to prevent multiple ballot casting, and smart contracts would be able to verify voters’ identities in order to prevent this from happening.
*   **Reducing insurance industry claims fraud**: In the insurance business, claims processing costs millions of dollars annually. The bogus claims cause even more money to be lost. Smart contracts assist in managing insurance from individuals or companies by strengthening claim processing through periodic mistake checks. Additionally, customers pay less overall, even for premium rates, as a result of the quicker processing times.
*   **One more adaptable example**: Other applications of smart contracts include peer-to-peer transactions, supply chain management, product creation, and stocktaking, among others. Although there is still much space for innovation and development, the increasing use of smart contracts emphasizes their intended benefits of efficiency, accountability, and transparency.

Smart contracts are key to many use cases and provides us with an opportunity to make transactions and processes more streamlined and automated. Smart contracts are here to stay; the steady increase of deployments year over year only emphasizes their significance within the blockchain ecosystem. The open-source community has fully embraced the concept and have developed many high-quality libraries in various smart contract languages. OpenZeppelin, for example, is widely utilized in the Solidity developer’s community for Ethereum-based blockchains.

The currency known as Enterprise Ethereum, with the public Ethereum inheritance, offers a simple one-step process to deploy smart contracts. The deployment is done via a transaction, making it a chain-wide operation by nature. Code for the smart contract is saved on-chain as state. As a result, all nodes are guaranteed to have access to the code and they all have the same version.

Yet another cryptocurrency, Corda adopts a peer-to-peer transaction coordination architecture, without utilizing a globally maintain common ledger of transactions. Because of this, smart contract deployment is done on a per-node basis that is based on the need to perform bi-lateral or multi-lateral transactions. Similar to Fabric, the administrator of the Corda node is responsible for ensuring that the smart contract required to process a transaction has been installed on the node.

# [Smart Contract Usage Patterns](#toc.xhtml#s313a)

“Smart” is typically associated with artificial intelligence that is built on massive amounts of data and employs computation intensive algorithms. Despite the name, smart contracts are neither smart nor legally binding. Smart contracts exist to build trust with decentralization, in which multiple parties execute the same code and maintain the same state, independently. In such applications efficiency is sacrificed for security through duplication. This means smart contracts is not a great place to build intelligence through machine learning, or carry out any amount of deep analytics.

Note: Besides duplicating computation, smart contracts also result in state data being stored in every node. When considering what kind of data should be maintained by smart contracts, the best practice is to only use it to keep track of information that either represents commitment of data stored somewhere else, so that they cannot be changed after the fact, or information that need to be updated. Legal documents typically are overly verbose and have a lot of boilerplate content. Saving the whole legal document inside smart contracts would be a very inefficient way to utilize blockchain.

# [Smart Contract Platforms](#toc.xhtml#s314a)

These days, there are numerous platforms for smart contracts. Technology, end-user (banking, government, supply chain, real estate, insurance, and more), and regional (Europe, North America, Asia, or rest of the world orientated) divides could be used to separate them.

Their distinctions are found in:

*   Languages used for programming.
*   Blockchain consensus.
*   The price of upkeep for smart contracts inside an application.
*   Variations in blockchain security.
*   Confidence in the primary network nodes.
*   Transaction confirmation speed, and numerous other factors.

1.  **Ethereum**: Ethereum pioneered the introduction of smart contracts to a broader cryptocurrency ecosystem and is the most sophisticated platform for writing and executing them. Because this open-source platform has one of the biggest developer networks out there, it can adapt to the constantly shifting landscape of the blockchain sector. 
    
     However, the first blockchain platform to create codes specifically for dApp creation was Ethereum. Numerous platforms have emerged as a result of their emergence, including Aeternity, Cardano, Qtum, Stellar, and Waves.
    
2.  **Aeternity**: Aeternity provides a way to power so-called Turing-complete smart contracts, which may be executed off-chain. This is done by combining a Proof-of-Work and Proof-of-Stake architecture that provides protection and privacy.
3.  **Cardano**: Cardano is a cryptocurrency and decentralized blockchain initiative. Cardano is open source, just like other cryptocurrency initiatives. With Goguen release, the Cardano platform is aiming to integrate smart contract capability. Their smarter contracts should become even more advanced as a result.
4.  **Qtum**: Two of the most important features of this open-source blockchain application platform are security and adaptability. 
    
     The Qtum team has put in a lot of effort to ensure that smart contracts can be carried out securely, which makes the platform ideal for companies and their enterprise clientele. Proof-of-Stake and a decentralized governance protocol are used by Qtum.
    
5.  **Stellar**: Unlike many other cryptocurrencies, Stellar was developed by developers for developers, which enables it to manage incredibly intricate smart contracts. Stellar is a clear, user-friendly substitute for basic smart contracts for developers looking to create more efficient smart contracts.
6.  **Waves**: Waves is an open-source blockchain project that makes extensive use of Web 3.0 technology and dApps. For developers who may choose to work with Waves, the company provides various online courses to help keep their smart contract project easy in addition to other forms of support. Similar to several smart contract initiatives, Waves uses Proof-of-Stake.

# [Benefits of Smart Contracts](#toc.xhtml#s315a)

The smart contracts system provides many benefits over traditional contracts for a wide range of industries. In theory, they are more efficient and trustworthy than traditional contract law, and are also thought to offer better security as all actions are recorded and verified. As a result, they may reduce unnecessary costs and time expenditure while enhancing transparency.

*   **Greater efficiency and speed**: Smart contracts are automated so there is no need to spend a lot of time on the paperwork and also correcting the errors that are manually written in the documents. They can be executed in minutes, for a fraction of the cost, from wherever the involved parties are, and without the need for lawyers. Smart contracts are able to improve the efficiency and speed with which commercial arrangements are carried out.
*   **Accuracy and transparency**: The fact that the codified terms are fully visible and accessible to all relevant parties, there is no way to dispute them once the smart contract is established. These facilities complete transactional transparency and may remove the likelihood of manipulation, bias, or error. This however, in turn leads to decreased monitoring costs and risks of opportunistic behavior.
*   **Trust**: Smart contracts may provide parties with a degree of trust as:  
    *   They automatically perform transactions following predetermined laws.
    *   The encrypted documents of these transactions are distributed over participants.
    *   The information on the contract and the terms of the contract are straight.
    *   They have specific validation by everyone.
    *   The immutability of the work guarantees that the smart contract can never be broken.
*   **Security**: Smart contracts are also thought to offer better security as all actions are recorded and verified. This is because blockchain transaction documents are encrypted which makes it extremely difficult to hack. Security features can also be integrated into a smart contract to automatically generate backups and duplicates in the event of damages, data losses to the original ones, or hacks.

# [Challenges to Smart Contracts Technology](#toc.xhtml#s316a)

Smart contracts consists of a number of challenges that may prevent more massive adoption:

*   **Human Errors**: Similar to paper contracts, smart contracts can still experience fraud, due to human errors. As smart contracts are codes, and these codes are written by people (coders), there is a (high) chance of it consisting bugs. They can be delayed, intercepted, and corrupted. Some mistakes have proven to be very costly.
*   **Confidentiality, Security, and Privacy:** Unlike traditional contracts, all transactions executed via a smart contract, are propagated across all of the nodes in the network.
*   **Privacy Issues**: Privacy issues may be created, particularly when the accounts of the parties are associated with known entities. Even when the parties rely on pseudonymous accounts, certain identification techniques can be used to discern the identities of parties who transact with a particular smart contract.
*   **Lack of Engineering Experience:** As smart contracts begin to proliferate, there will be a need for new types of cryptography experts, and forensic experts, to verify software code and to translate the code into human-readable form. A huge amount of engineering expertise is required to make perfectly operational smart contracts. However, experienced coders are hard to find, and costly.
*   **Legal and Regulatory Challenges:** There are also a number of legal and regulatory challenges, which are preventing the widespread utilization of smart contracts. Smart contracts lack a clear legal status and is no official government regulation that applies to them.
*   **Interpretation and Enforceability**: If there is a dispute about whether a smart contract accurately memorialized the parties’ intentions or whether one party has breached the contract, the parties may still bring legal proceedings or engage in alternative dispute resolution processes. The enforceability of smart contracts is subject to jurisdictional variations in contract law.
*   **Jurisdictional Issues**: Smart contracts also raise jurisdictional issues. But as the blockchain operates as a decentralized ledger, smart contracts can be formed and accessed anywhere across the globe. They do not reside in any one location, but exist across multiple locations at one time. Yet, existing laws are jurisdiction-based. The differences in laws across jurisdictions can be highly problematic, and may result in incongruent rights and responsibilities, and confusion regarding the consequences if there is a contract violation.

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml#s317a)

We have learned that the Fabric is a modular and expandable open-source architecture for implementing and running permissioned blockchains. This was later expanded upon as Hyperledger.

The blockchain stores state and ledger data, performs transactions, and runs programs known as chaincode. We have learnt that various components of the Hyperledger Fabric Network are Assets, Shared Ledger, Smart Contract, and Peer nodes. We also learnt to write a blockchain application and elements of PKI. Furthermore, we have discussed about Hyperledger Fabric policies and its types. Additionally, we learnt about digital certificates and the process of obtaining DSC from Certifying Authority.

In the next chapter, we are going to learn about Blockchain Interoperability, its need, and use cases.
