# [CHAPTER 9](#toc.xhtml#c09)

# [Blockchain Interoperability](#toc.xhtml#c09)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml#s319a)

The users of Blockchain utilize this technology through several media, devices, operating systems, and different types of software and programming languages. Interoperability allows for the seamless transfer of data and assets between different blockchain networks, reducing the need for manual processes and intermediaries. But without interoperability, it is almost impossible to adopt blockchain technology, because each individual project would be too isolated. Interoperability solutions allow blockchain to read and write data to other blockchains.

# [Structure](#toc.xhtml#s320a)

In this chapter, we will cover the following topics:

*   Need for Blockchain Interoperability
*   Cross-Chain Technology
*   Blockchain Interoperability Projects
*   Blockchain Interoperability Architecture
*   Blockchain Interoperability Technologies

# [Need for Blockchain Interoperability](#toc.xhtml#s321a)

Blockchain technology’s development happened very fast and is now being classified into four stages:

*   Blockchain 1.0 was devoted to cryptocurrency applications. The common use case was the electronic payment system or cryptocurrency.
*   Blockchain 2.0 provides smart contracts, smart property, and more. Ethereum Smart contracts, Hyperledger Fabric, and R3’s Corda are examples of Blockchain 2.0.
*   Blockchain 3.0 developed decentralized Applications (dApps) that cater to several fields beyond financial applications or asset transfers. Common use cases of Blockchain 3.0 include dApps for sectors such as governance, IoT, healthcare, supply chain, smart city, and many other non-financial applications. 
    
    In a decentralized system, control is distributed amongst the components of the system, while in a distributed system, the components are physically separated and they communicate over a network. Both types of systems can be used to enable the sharing of resources and workloads.
    
*   Blockchain 4.0 has applications for industry 4.0. The key themes of Blockchain 4.0 includes distributed ledger technology and useful blockchain applications for industry 4.0.

To meet their needs, many user groups have created their own versions of blockchain systems. The broad range of uses for distributed ledger systems were made feasible.

Additionally, this led to the internal development of various blockchain projects with various protocols and architectures evolving. These projects make use of various technologies and consensus mechanisms, tailored to particular applications or use cases. Because there are so many initiatives, the development process is extremely fragmented, with little to no compatibility between many.

Wegner stated, “interoperability is the ability of two or more software components to cooperate despite differences in language, interface, and execution platform. As such the objective of blockchain interoperability is to support such cooperation among blockchain systems, despite those kinds of differences.”

There are already several blockchain interoperability solutions available[^(\[1\])](#c09.xhtml#ftn1).

The multinational giant IBM is the largest company in the world embracing blockchain, leading the way for companies to integrate Hyperledger and the IBM cloud into their systems.

The National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) defines interoperability in the blockchain architecture as:

“An interoperable blockchain architecture is a composition of distinguishable blockchain systems, each representing a unique distributed data ledger, where transaction execution may span multiple blockchain systems, and where data recorded in one blockchain is reachable, verifiable, and referenceable by another possibly foreign transaction in a semantically compatible manner.”

Interoperability technology enables seamless and secure execution of smart contracts among different public permissionless, private, or consortium permissioned blockchains. It is commonly examined from three broad levels, including:

*   Foundational
*   Structural
*   Semantic

Foundationally, data can be transferred smoothly among different systems. But structurally, the aforementioned exchange takes place only if there is a well-defined data format. The data contained by transaction across systems is interpretable by end-users at the semantic level.

# [Blockchain-Based Initiatives](#toc.xhtml#s322a)

These blockchain-based apps were compelled to interact with other blockchain apps running on other blockchain networks. This led to the development of a new communication mechanism between several segregated blockchain networks, establishing the groundwork for the idea of blockchain network interoperability.

Blockchain interoperable protocols can be categorized into :

*   Cross-Chain Protocols
*   Cross Blockchain Protocols

While cross-blockchain interoperability protocols help with communication between heterogeneous blockchains, cross-chain protocols are designed to provide communication between homogeneous blockchains.

The exchange of information between Polygon and Avalanche is an illustration of a cross-chain protocol. One instance of a cross-blockchain protocol is the exchange of data across cryptocurrencies such as Ethereum and Bitcoin. Since the hashing strategy, consensus algorithm, block size, execution environment, and network architecture of the source and target blockchain systems may differ, designing a cross-blockchain protocol is considered challenging.

# [Avalanche](#toc.xhtml#s323a)

One well-known Decentralized Finance (DeFi) blockchain is Avalanche. Trader Joe, Wonderland, BenQi, Curve, AAVE, and Wonderland are the main DeFi protocols that leverage Avalanche.

With a transactional throughput of 4,500 transaction per second (TPS), the leading DeFi protocol Avalanche outperforms Bitcoin (7 TPS), Ethereum (14 TPS), and Polkadot (1,500 TPS) astronomically. The native token of Avalanche, known as AVAX, is used for staking, fee payments, and serving as a unit of account across the various subnetworks that have been built atop Avalanche.

# [Polygon (MATIC)](#toc.xhtml#s324a)

To address this issue, Polygon offers numerous goods and services.

***Note:** The blockchain for Ethereum is expensive and slow. Therefore, Polygon’s SDK improves upon this by allowing the creation of Ethereum sidechains, which are blockchains connected to Ethereum through a two-way peg. These sidechains come in a variety of forms, including:*

*   The ability to group transactions into batches and submit them to the Ethereum blockchain as a single batch (Plasma Chains).
*   The ability to combine several transfers into a single transaction is known as ZK-Rollups.
*   Possessing Ethereum smart contract-scaling Plasma Chains (Optimistic Rollups).

MATIC is Polygon’s native token consisting the following uses:

*   Pay for transaction fees in the network.
*   Incorporate the feature of the unit of payment and settlement in the Polygon ecosystem.
*   Power the Polygon proof-of-stake sidechain.

It is not impossible, but rather difficult to identify and verify data recorded in one chain from another chain just by examining the information exchanged between them.

Cross Blockchain token transfer involves several challenges such as:

*   Evolving a method of issuing tokens on the blockchains, to disable tokens when they are leaving the blockchain. Tokens are rebalanced throughout the blockchain to preserve liquidity.
*   To determine which properties of the blockchain are necessary for cross-chain transfer.
*   To choose which blockchain is appropriate for cross-blockchain transfer.

# [Crypto Tokens](#toc.xhtml#s325a)

With the increasing number of blockchain systems appearing, even those that support cryptocurrencies, the subject of Blockchain interoperability is becoming a topic of major importance. The objective is to support transferring assets from one blockchain system to another blockchain system.

In technical terms, “token” is simply another synonym for “crypto-asset” or “cryptocurrency.” But based on context, it has gradually come to mean a few more distinct things. Firstly, all cryptocurrencies other than Bitcoin and Ethereum (which are also tokens in theory) will be described. The second is to explain specific digital assets, such several decentralized finance (DeFi) tokens, that operate on top of the blockchain of another cryptocurrency. A vast array of potential uses for tokens exist, ranging from the sale of rare items in video games to facilitating the operation of decentralized exchanges. But just like any other cryptocurrency, they may all be owned or exchanged.

**THORChain,** built on the Cosmos blockchain, is a decentralized liquidity protocol that specializes in cross-chain connectivity, allowing traders to swap tokens between different networks. It uses a decentralized liquidity protocol that allows users to swap assets in a permissionless setting. It enables the exchange of native layer-1 assets such as BTC by acting as a vault manager. THORChain uses Tendermint and Cosmos-SDK to secure its network.

# [Cross-Chain Technology](#toc.xhtml#s326a)

The new cross-chain protocol makes it easier for various blockchain networks to communicate with one another and permits data sharing between many networks. Users can communicate with each other directly through cross-chain protocol. As such, blockchains with comparable networks can exchange value and information[^(\[2\])](#c09.xhtml#ftn2).

Effectively, a cross-chain design promotes interoperability by letting many blockchains trade off features, security, decentralization, and efficiency. This can decrease fragmentation, increase chain efficiency, and facilitate more seamless user and feature flow between different blockchains.

Cross-blockchain interoperability is a characteristic that facilitates communication across several blockchains without the need for middlemen. This basically means that value can be transferred between blockchains that share similar networks.

# [Main Characteristic Features of Cross-Chain Technology](#toc.xhtml#s327a)

Following are the main features of the cross-chain technology:

*   This technology works on the principle of atomicity.
*   It enables proper consistency between several interconnected blockchains.
*   It enables the distribution of networks across various platforms.

Cross-chain technology is significant because it allows users to exchange tokens and transfer data without the need for a middleman. This infrastructure makes it easier for two or more blockchains to work together, improve efficiency, balance decentralization and security, and more. It is also crucial since it decreases segmentation, increases chain efficiency, and permits more open communication between users on different blockchains. Thus, cross-chain technology has enormous potential to enable blockchain interoperability, which can resolve multiple problems and eliminate a number of limitations on blockchain and other networks.

# [Working Approaches of Cross-Chain Technology](#toc.xhtml#s328a)

Blockchain interoperability is used by each network in a distinct way to facilitate transactions without requiring third-party interfaces. The following are some of the most popular and well-known methods for isolating transactions across different chains:

*   **Atomic Swaps:** Exchange brokers known as “atomic swaps” allow two parties to exchange tokens across several blockchains. There is no need for a centralized third party to facilitate the trades with this kind of method. Rather, it facilitates peer-to-peer direct token trading for the user. This mechanism either finalizes the entire processor or does not initiate it in the first place. For instance, two parties can decide to keep a one hour restriction for the atomic swap. In this case, the original owners will receive their deposited tokens once the duration of one hour is over, even though not all the conditions are met. Although it may not be easy for a new user, a professional blockchain developer would find it easy.
*   **Relays:** Blockchain networks can monitor events and transactions occurring on other chains because of the Relays feature. They function from chain to chain, allowing a single contract to serve as the core client of other nodes across several chains, all without the need for dispersed nodes. Thus, it may independently validate the complete transaction history and individual central headers. However, the relay approach requires a lot of expenditure to operate and take care of the security of operation. Apart from these two methods, there are various other approaches that cross-chain technology follows to function. Merged consensus is one of the approaches that enables two-way blockchain interoperability between several networks. On the other hand, a specific group of verified parties also comes together to check the events of the chains.

# [Benefits of Cross-Chain Technology](#toc.xhtml#s329a)

Although cross-chain mechanisms play a major role in facilitating blockchain interoperability, they also have a number of other advantages. Among the many important advantages that cross-chain technology provides are:

*   The ability to establish a solid connection and to facilitate efficient communication across two distinct blockchain networks is one of cross-chain technology’s greatest features. It is typically used to start token swaps and asset transfers, therefore blockchain incompatibility is not an issue. Furthermore, it facilitates the transmission of data, including assets, transaction receipts, and virtual contracts, by the user.
*   Because current blockchain mechanisms have limited interoperability, cross-chain technology enables better scalability. It is now possible to take advantage of the speed when it comes to many blockchains because this technology facilitates user interaction with various blockchain networks. It is now possible to leverage the speed concerning multiple blockchains that ultimately enhances scalability.
*   Improving the data flow and token transfer: Under the existing setup, a user must transmit commands from the source chain. The relayers transfer these instructions to the destination chain after the validator gets them. Cross-chain technology, on the other hand, verifies the transaction immediately upon completion and delays its arrival at the destination change until the verification is finished. It does this by streamlining the entire procedure and facilitating the seamless interchange and transmission of data. You can enroll in any online blockchain course to study the fundamentals if you also want to trade cryptocurrencies on the blockchain.
*   The potential of cross-chain technology to deter monopolization by big entities: By deterring monopolization by major firms, cross-chain technology also contributes to market stability. For example, with over 70% of the market share, Bitcoin and Ethereum are the most well-known names in the cryptocurrency sector. Because of this domination, there is no room for new competitors to enter the market, test out their methods, and emerge from the pack. In these kinds of situations, cross-chain technology can be useful because it creates an environment in which transaction processes focus equally on all networks.

Not just a few significant blockchain networks but also additional networks will be the focus. Therefore, by enabling a balanced way to state how cryptocurrency participants participate in the marketplace, cross-chain technology can establish a fair environment.

# [Blockchain Interoperability Projects](#toc.xhtml#s330a)

In recent years, a number of projects that addressed public blockchain interoperability have arisen[^(\[3\])](#c09.xhtml#ftn3). However, enterprise-level interoperability solutions are very new, and the required standardization work is still in its early stages. While there are other classifications, we may generally divide interoperability solutions into two groups based on distinct methodologies and ideologies. In the first, a permanent infrastructure known as a “settlement” chain (or hub) is established to which other chains (called “sidechains”) can connect. The common chain functions as an intermediary for interoperability, providing the sidechains with decentralized trust. This guarantees for the settlement of cross-chain transactions.

This method works especially well with distributed ledgers and public blockchains, as demonstrated by Cosmos and Polkadot.

The modules and features that enable direct network interoperability without reliance on external infrastructure are added to the second category of interoperability solutions. Hyperledger Cactus and the Hyperledger Lab Weaver belong to this second category, which is helpful in business settings where performance and privacy are of important considerations. Each of these strategies have advantages and disadvantages that vary depending on the situation.

# [Hyperledger Cactus](#toc.xhtml#s331a)

A new pluggable enterprise-grade architecture called Hyperledger Cactus allows transactions on several distributed ledgers without adding yet another rival blockchain[^(\[4\])](#c09.xhtml#ftn4). A SDK of SDKs provides a decentralized, flexible, and secure integration with and between blockchain networks. Furthermore, through an extensible plugin design that allows new protocols or functionality to be added by creating new plugins, it seeks to cover as many protocols as possible.

# [Key Characteristics of Hyperledger Cactus](#toc.xhtml#s332a)

*   The Open Digital Asset Protocol (ODAP) is supported.
*   More examples and better documentation (similar to fabric-samples).
*   A greater quantity of maintainers.
*   Enabling public testing of a Cactus network.
*   Anyone with access to a cloud provider account or a K8s cluster can replicate the Cactus benchmarks in the same manner that they do the functional test cases.

# [Hyperledger FireFly](#toc.xhtml#s333a)

An additional Hyperledger called FireFly facilitates interoperability at the application tier by offering an API orchestration layer across several blockchain ledgers. This enables the implementation of common blockchain programming elements across various blockchains in pluggable microservice code modules, such as identity verification, off-chain/on-chain data orchestration, and tokens. One such pattern that has actually been put into practice spans several chains in a single corporate transaction. It involves coordinating tokenized value transfer on an Ethereum blockchain.

# [Salient Features of Hyperledger FireFly](#toc.xhtml#s334a)

*   Multi-ledger support
*   Pluggable DID provider support
*   Pluggable API Security (for example, OAuth 2.0)
*   Active-active clustering
*   NoSQL database support

# [Weaver](#toc.xhtml#s335a)

The Hyperledger Lab’s Weaver framework and family of protocols allow autonomous networks constructed on heterogeneous DLTs to cooperate for asset transfers and data exchange while maintaining the fundamental security and decentralization principles of blockchain technology.

Weaver-based cooperation eliminates the need for dependable middlemen. The internal governance processes of the networks are used to formulate rules and regulations. Its interoperability is mainly dependent on shared infrastructure, which might offer identification services but is not involved in the fundamental protocols that facilitate the sharing of assets and data. Protocols that rely on the innate consensus mechanisms of counterparty networks to maintain trust enable interoperability.

The protocols for data transfer between ledgers with provenance and authenticity verification are supported by Fabric and Corda.

# [Salient Features of Weaver](#toc.xhtml#s336a)

*   Implementation of cross-network decentralization.
*   The relay module makes ODAP compliant by offering increased resilience (fault tolerance) and message queueing capabilities for distribution and load balancing.
*   Invoking cross-network transactions and publishing/subscribing events.
*   Complete confidentiality assistance for sharing of states and assets.
*   Contains an enhanced data/state sharing mechanism that makes use of verifiable external observation features to confirm the state’s freshness.
*   Better testing features, tutorials, and documentation.
*   Increased capacity for performance evaluation.
*   Interoperability with Hyperledger Cactus via combined and combined features.

# [YUI](#toc.xhtml#s337a)

Originating from a Japanese term meaning knot, join, and connect, comes the word “YUI.” A new initiative from Hyperledger Labs called YUI aims to establish interoperability across several heterogeneous ledgers.

With the help of our Hyperledger Lab, several heterogeneous ledgers can communicate with one another. The lab offers tools and modules for developing cross-chain applications as well as middleware and modules for cross-chain communication.

With enhancements to suit other Hyperledger projects, YUI is built on the Inter Blockchain Communication (IBC) protocol developed by the Cosmos project for cross-chain communication. IBC offers the foundation for arbitrary data transfer and computation between ledgers without relying on a reliable third party.

YUI supports cross-authentication between enterprise ledgers (Fabric, Besu, and Corda). Each actor of ledgers has to interact with only a respective ledger of its interest to complete a cross-chain operation such as an atomic swap of tokens.

# [Salient Features of YUI](#toc.xhtml#s338a)

*   Supports other ledger (possibly other Hyperledger projects).
*   Enhanced support for Solidity and Ethereum-based ledgers.
*   Has Enhanced support for connecting the Cosmos ecosystem (such as Tendermint light client for Solidity).
*   Improved documentation and release samples.

Interoperability operations can be classified in three categories:

*   Cryptocurrency interoperability approaches.
*   Blockchain engines.
*   Blockchain connectors.

# [Blockchain Interoperability Architecture](#toc.xhtml#s339a)

The draft of a blockchain interoperability architecture was produced by several individual IETF participants. The Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF) is the body that defines standard operating internet protocols such as TCP/IP. The IETF is an open standards organization supervised by the Internet Society’s Internet Architecture Board (IAB).

The Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF) is an open, global community of network designers, operators, vendors, and academics that are interested in the development of Internet architecture and its seamless functioning. To coordinates the management, development, and operation of the Internet. The IETF was founded in 1986.

The capacity of various blockchain networks to transfer certain kinds of digital assets between the networks’ individual blockchains as well as to exchange and utilize data between them is referred to as “blockchain interoperability.”

Interoperability is harmed directly by these disparate data standards. Blockchain, which uses APIs to access data, provides a solution to this problem. By doing this, data formats are standardized to allow for smooth data transfer regardless of how well EHRs can interact with various HL7 versions.

A cross-chain is the capacity of two relatively independent blockchains to communicate with one another. In other words, it enables blockchains to communicate with one another due to their uniform construction. Cross-chains overcome the constraints of a single chain.

Currently, there are at least 1,000 blockchains with at least four types of blockchain networks. While the idea of blockchain is a singular data transfer type, there are multiple platforms provided in this industry.

The definition “interoperable blockchain architecture” is incorporated in the NIST definition of “blockchain” (p.50 of [^(\[5\])](#c09.xhtml#ftn5)):

Interoperable blockchain architecture is a composition of distinguishable blockchain systems, each representing a unique distributed data ledger, where the atomic transaction execution may span multiple heterogeneous blockchain systems, and where data recorded in one blockchain is reachable, verifiable, and referenceable by another possibly foreign transactions in a semantically compatible manner.

In this context it is useful to revisit some key architectural designs of the Trusted Platform Module (TPM) from the late 1990s which provided a basic understanding on trustworthiness[^(\[6\])](#c09.xhtml#ftn6). Reusing some of the concepts in trusted computing, a node can be considered to exhibit technical trust if it:

*   Operates unhindered and shielded from external influences or interference
*   Operates for a well-defined task
*   Has the ability to report results of its computations and its internal status truthfully

![](images/Figure-9.1.jpg)

**Figure 9.1:** Example of Inter-Domain Transaction across Two Blockchain Systems

To illustrate **Inter-Domain Transaction**, a simple example is shown in *[Figure 9.1](#c09.xhtml#fig9_1)* in which an asset recorded in blockchain system BC1 is to be transferred to blockchain system BC2.

Both blockchain BC1 and BC2 are permissioned/private blockchain systems.

In *[Figure 9.1](#c09.xhtml#fig9_1)*, a User A with Application X has his or her asset ownership (example, land title deed) recorded on the ledger inside blockchain BC1. The local transaction-identifier for this ledger entry is T x1privateID. The User A wishes to transfer legal ownership of the asset (example, sell) to a different User B running Application Y. However, the User B requires that the asset be “moved” to the blockchain BC2 and be authoritatively recorded on the ledger of BC2.

This would allow User B to later sell the asset locally in blockchain BC2 to other users in BC2. Note that being private blockchain systems, none of the gateways or nodes in BC1 can directly read/write to BC2, and vice versa.

The following is a high-level summary of the transaction flows between BC1 and Bc2 :

**In step 1**, the User A initiates the transfer to User B by submitting a (candidate) transaction to BC1 with the recipient address being the public-key of User B in BC2 (example, pubkeyB/BC2).

Because the destination blockchain BC2 is a foreign system, only the gateway nodes in BC1 are permitted or have the capability to process this transaction.

**In step 2**, the gateway G1 selects the candidate (unprocessed) transaction of User A in BC1 and proceeds to process the transaction. Seeing that the pending transaction is destined for pubkeyB/BC2, gateway G1 locates one or more gateways G2 in BC2 and proceeds to perform trust negotiations with G2.

**In step 3**, identifier value T x1privateID with a new public value T x1publicID. G1 has to persistently maintain this table of mappings (T x1privateID, T x1publicID). In the future, Gateway G1 must provide a means to resolve the public value T x1publicID back to the internal private value T x1privateID, should that be required.

**In step 4 and step 5**, (multiround) gateways G1 and G2 must establish trust by executing a key establishment protocol that includes the exchange of keying parameters, hardware root of trust certificates (example, AIK certificates in TPM), hardware status reports such as Quote protocol reports, and other relevant trust establishment parameters.

**In step 6**, after pairwise technical-trust has been established between gateways G1 and G2, the gateway G2 proceeds to submit a new local transaction with identifier T x2privateID into the ledger of BC2 addressed pubkeyB. This local transaction references the asset (in BC1) identified by the public value T x1publicID. In effect, gateway G2 is “registering” this asset T x2privateID as belonging to User B with public key pubkeyB.

**In step 7**, confirmation has been achieved in the ledger of BC2. Gateway G2 needs to indirectly report this confirmed status of T x2privateID to gateway G1. As such G2 has to mask local transaction-identifier T x2privateID with a new public identifier T x2publicID. Gateway G2 must henceforth maintain a persistent mapping between T x2publicID and T x2privateID.

**In step 8**, Gateway G2 issues a signed assertion to G1 stating that the asset with transaction identifier T x2publicID has been confirmed on ledger BC2, and includes a hash of the private identifier T x2privateID in the assertion.

**In step 9**, upon seeing the signed assertion from G2, gateway G1 proceeds to submit a new “invalidation” transaction in BC1, essentially marking that the asset previously known as T x1privateID has been moved to BC2. The invalidation-transaction also includes a reference to the new home of the asset, namely T x2publicID/BC2. It should also include a hash of the signed assertion from G2. Finally, in step 10 the User B is able to see the confirmed transaction T x2privateID/BC2, while User A sees that T x1privateID/BC1 as also being confirmed.

***Note**: The above use-case is an abstract example only. Many variations are possible for these flows, including the incorporation of commitment protocols (example, 2-phase commit) in Steps 2 to 9*.

# [Conclusion](#toc.xhtml#s340a)

In this chapter, we have learned that these blockchain-based apps were compelled to interact with other blockchain apps running on different blockchain networks. This led to the development of a new communication mechanism between several segregated blockchain networks, establishing the groundwork for the idea of blockchain network interoperability.

For revision, we learned that Blockchain technology development happened very fast and is now being classified into four stages:

*   Blockchain 1.0 was devoted to cryptocurrency applications.
*   Blockchain 2.0 provides smart contracts and property.
*   Blockchain 3.0 developed distributed applications such as governance, IoT, healthcare, supply chain, smart city, and many other non-financial applications.
*   Blockchain 4.0 distributed ledger technology.

We have seen that Blockchain interoperable protocols can be categorized into: cross-chain (homogenous) and cross blockchain protocols (heterogeneous).

As a form of money that symbolizes a digital asset kept in a blockchain, crypto tokens are the widely used application of blockchain technology. The new cross-chain protocol makes it easier for various blockchain networks to communicate with one another and permits data sharing between many networks.

In the next chapter, titled *Legal and Environmental Aspects*, we will study implications of using cryptocurrencies and about Non-Fungible Tokens and the Indian rules on cryptocurrency.
